2,2026-10-26,"Anthropic filed key paperwork on Monday ahead of a highly anticipated initial public offering, providing new details about its finances and the risks facing its business.
According to the filing, the artificial intelligence company plans to go public on Nasdaq under the ticker symbol ANT. The IPO could come later in November, The Wall Street Journal reported. The S-1 prospectus does not include details on how much the company plans to raise or its targeted valuation; The New York Times reported in August that it was looking to raise more than $100 billion with a valuation around $2 trillion.
“The advent of machine intelligence is one of the seminal moments in human history,” Anthropic wrote in the filing. “We encourage you, as a prospective owner of our stock, not to think of your purchase in purely transactional terms. We hope you will see it instead as an opportunity to be part of something greater.”
The paperwork offers a window into the company’s finances through the first half of 2026. Its revenue grew to more than $16 billion, up from about $4.5 billion in 2025. The Claude maker emphasized its growing enterprise business, noting that more than 11,900 customers spent at least $100,000 in the past twelve months; more than 1,900 spent $1 million or more; and roughly 250 customers spent $10 million or more.
Despite its growing revenue, Anthropic has yet to turn a profit. It posted a net loss of $80.7 billion in the first half of 2026, compared to a net loss of $13 billion in the same period last year.
While it’s expected that a company accurately reports its risk factors, the prospectus also features a particularly thorough list of potential business quagmires, noting that investing in the stock carries “a high degree of risk.”
Those risks range from the ability to secure adequate compute to intense competition from rival labs to advanced AI systems’ potential to “pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.”
The filing warns about the possibility that sophisticated AI could be abused by threat actors to launch cyberattacks and develop novel biological weapons, or by authoritarian governments to surveil their citizens. Anthropic also pointed to the risk that it could lose control of the AI models it develops.
It also highlighted the volatility of government action, specifically noting the Trump administration’s decision to designate the company as a supply chain risk.
“While we continue to work with government agencies, we cannot be certain that the resolution of these lawsuits will not adversely affect our ability to sell to government agencies and other customers that sell products and services to the US government, and in turn adversely affect our business, financial condition, and results of operations,” the filing concluded.
In September, Anthropic’s rival OpenAI—which filed confidentially for IPO in the summer—announced that it no longer intended to go public this year, citing concerns over the risks posed to humanity by AI.
Anthropic was founded in 2021 by seven former employees of OpenAI, who had reportedly lost faith in the lab’s ability to develop advanced AI models safely. Anthropic markets itself as highly safety-conscious; its CEO, Dario Amodei, has repeatedly commented on the risk that AI could eliminate scores of jobs, and act against human interests.
The filings also outline Anthropic’s ambition to develop sophisticated AI models capable of accelerating scientific discovery, curing disease, and spurring economic growth.
“To build AI is to harness cognition itself, and anything so powerful can be used for ill as well as good,” the company wrote.
The company did not immediately respond to WIRED’s request for comment.

4,2026-10-26,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Kate McNeil, a 41-year-old small-business owner based in Atlanta. It's been edited for length and clarity.
The idea to open a romance bookstore started during COVID.
I joined an online group of fellow romantasy readers, looking to connect with other people who also enjoyed my favorite form of escapism. That group had over 2,000 members, and it was difficult to feel a sense of community. Eventually, it broke off into local chapters.
After COVID, I didn't know of any dedicated space for these same readers to meet in Atlanta. That's how I started the journey to opening the first romance bookstore in my neighborhood. In the two years since I opened, more romance bookstores have opened up, including one less than two miles away.
When I launched All the Tropes bookstore, I was working a job I thought I'd retire from. I taught theater at Decatur High School for six years. My students always came first. I didn't want those kids to feel like I wasn't present or that they didn't have my undivided attention, so the bookstore was a back-burner idea for a few years.
I'd get home from rehearsal at 11 p.m., conduct business research until 2 a.m., then get up at 6 a.m. and go to work the next day and teach.
I learned about dealing with publishing companies and book distributors, connected with local artists to showcase their work, and reached out to local authors. One of the biggest challenges was finding a place for the store.
I knew that I wanted to stay on the east side of Atlanta because I lived in Kirkwood and felt very connected to the community.
In 2024, I found a location a four-minute walk from my home, and I knew it was time to make my idea a reality. In May of that year, I quit my teaching job and prepared to open the bookstore by the end of summer on Romance Bookstore Day.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
I received the keys to the bookstore six weeks before the day I planned to open. The timeline I had for opening day came with a lot of risks. I had cashed in my retirement fund, quit my job, and had a large preorder of ""Onyx Storm"" before there were bookshelves in the store.
There were many times my friends and I didn't leave the store until 4 a.m. A local artist painted the shop; I printed my merchandise at a local shop in Kirkwood; and I created the fabric wall. There were many nights I had doubts that All the Tropes would come to fruition.
I was a mom with two young kids opening a niche business that, to my knowledge, would be the first in the state, but I was prepared. I had my accounts with publishers, and my branding was complete. Perseverance, my husband, my village of friends, and an invested community got me to opening day.
All the Tropes opened August 17, 2024, on Romance Bookstore Day. In the span of a few months, I went from a passionate high school teacher to the owner and operator of a romance bookstore.
Even in a city like Atlanta with several thriving independent bookstores, I wonder about the percentage of residents who read for fun and, more specifically, read romance. While becoming a general indie bookstore would allow me to sell so many other things, I still believe All the Tropes can continue to thrive in a niche market, despite oversaturation.
Unlike a large book retailer, All the Tropes is a destination spot people can come from out of town to visit. Our social presence and promotion of local writers and artists also offer something unique to readers.
All the Tropes could pivot to general fiction, but so far, more stores opening has not impacted store traffic or sales.
All the Tropes continues to thrive because of my original vision of creating a community space for romance book lovers to connect. We now have eight active book clubs and continue to see consistent revenue.
Passion, trusting my gut, therapy, and a supportive group of friends are the reason I was able to turn an idea I brainstormed late at night into a successful niche business ready to grow.
We are now ready for a larger space and to expand our three-person team.
Do you have a unique small business story to share? Email the editor, Manseen Logan, at mlogan@businessinsider.com.

5,2026-10-26,"While many Americans are sitting on the sidelines, waiting for the real-estate market to become more affordable, ultrawealthy buyers are still snapping up homes.
Sotheby's International Realty's 2026 Mid-Year luxury outlook survey found that 55% of its real-estate affiliated professionals worldwide who specialize in properties priced at $10 million and above saw an increase in luxury homebuyers in their local markets over the previous 12 months. Respondents also said they expect strong demand for the remainder of 2026.
""The affluent remain engaged and confident in the luxury housing market. They are deliberate but not deterred,"" Philip A. White Jr., president and CEO of Sotheby's International Realty, said in the brokerage's report.
Here are four trends gaining traction among ultrawealthy homebuyers according to Sotheby's report, from a revival in San Francisco's real estate market to a growing demand for homes that allow buyers to age in place.
Americans are increasingly focusing on health and wellness, transforming not only how they live but also what they want in their homes.
Sotheby's International Realty found that luxury buyers — many of whom already invest in products and routines that help them stay healthy — are less likely to downsize or move into senior housing. Instead, they are opting to buy homes that better allow them to age in place.
Sotheby's International Realty's survey shows that nearly 40% of real estate professionals said aging in place is a growing priority among homebuyers.
""Increasing life expectancy, coupled with affluent homebuyers' desire to 'age in place,' is reshaping everything from home design and location choice to how inventory circulates in prime real estate markets,"" Tammy Fahmi, senior vice president of global servicing and strategy at Sotheby's International Realty, said in the report.
For luxury buyers, a home's ability to support a high quality of life is a top priority. Sotheby's found that they want spa-like amenities, including saunas, cold plunges, and red-light therapy; advanced air and water filtration, and easy access to the outdoors through nearby hiking or walking trails. Those who can afford it are also interested in integrated health-monitoring technology or in-home staff.
While home sales are still down across the country, Sotheby's International Realty's report suggests that the luxury end of the real-estate market is doing well.
""There's a little more inventory that has helped keep that property market moving,"" Lawrence Yun, chief economist and senior vice president of research at the National Association of Realtors, said in Sotheby's report. ""The stock market has helped, plus the ability of upper-end homebuyers to pay with cash and not be concerned about mortgage rates.""
In the US, several markets continue to attract luxury buyers, especially Miami. In recent years, celebrities and billionaires, including Jeff Bezos and Mark Zuckerberg, have purchased multimillion-dollar homes in the Miami area.
""The ultra-luxury property market in Miami is on particularly solid ground,"" Yun said. ""They had a little oversupply, but with their infrastructure development and new luxury condominium construction, it's becoming an even bigger city.""
Global uncertainty is prompting many people to reconsider both where they invest their money and where they live. For ultra-high net worth individuals, that often means looking to cities that offer stability and greater value.
Among those places, Milan is becoming an increasingly attractive destination for luxury homebuyers, according to a Sotheby's report.
Milan's appeal is also tied to Italy's tax incentives and residency-by-investment program, which provides access to the Schengen Area, which includes countries such as the Netherlands, Spain, Sweden, Portugal, and Greece.
""That has attracted a lot of ultra-high-net-worth individuals,"" Diletta Giorgolo, a global real estate advisor at Italy Sotheby's International Realty, said in the report. ""More people were coming to Italy for not only residency, but their fiscal regime. We were always used to international buyers, but they came for second homes. The big difference is that now many are buying primary homes.""
As the AI industry booms in the Bay Area, younger affluent buyers who left for cities like New York and Los Angeles are being drawn back by job opportunities.
""San Francisco thrives on innovation and new technology, and now the AI boom is real,"" Alex Hachiya, a San Francisco-based senior global real estate advisor at Sotheby's International Realty, said in Sotheby's report. ""Wealth flooded back into the city in 2025, and we saw 84% more sales of homes priced at $7.5 million and up that year, compared to 2024.""
Hachiya said it is typical for younger luxury buyers, many of whom work in tech, are entrepreneurs, or come from family wealth, to buy homes in San Francisco before later purchasing a second property in the suburbs, where they plan to raise their children.
He added that when it comes to homes, these buyers want move-in-ready homes with flexible living spaces and walkable neighborhoods.

6,2026-10-26,"Last year, the federal government made a windfall of $627 million when it sold Anthropic shares it had seized from associates of crypto fraudster Sam Bankman-Fried.
If it had held on to that stake, its windfall could have been a lot bigger.
Business Insider previously reported that the federal government last year sold shares of Anthropic that had belonged to Caroline Ellison and Nishad Singh, two executives at Bankman-Fried's now-defunct companies.
Anthropic's S-1 filing on Monday revealed how much the federal government made from the sale of their shares. The prospectus doesn't name Ellison and Singh specifically. It says the government in October 2025 sold 4,453,900 Series B shares of Anthropic that ""had been forfeited by former holders in legal proceedings to which we were not a party.""
The figure matches up exactly with the combined $50 million in shares that Singh and Ellison purchased for $11.2261 each in Anthropic's 2022 fundraising round, and later forfeited to the government.
The shares represent about a quarter of a percent of Anthropic today. But given the company's explosive growth, they are still worth a fortune.
Anthropic hasn't yet announced the stock price for its expected November initial public offering. At a $1.5 trillion valuation, the shares would be worth a total of about $3.94 billion. That would be six times as much as the government sold the shares for a year ago.
How the federal government came to own a chunk of Anthropic in the first place is a story involving a notorious fraudster and a complicated criminal forfeiture process.
Bankman-Fried participated in Anthropic's 2022 Series B fundraising round with Singh and Ellison. Singh purchased $40 million of the Series B shares, and Ellison acquired $10 million.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Singh and Ellison later pleaded guilty to crimes related to their work with Bankman-Fried and testified against him at his Manhattan federal criminal trial. The jury found Bankman-Fried guilty, and a judge sentenced him to 25 years in prison. The federal government took ownership of Singh's and Ellison's Anthropic shares by April 2025.
The US Marshals Service sold Singh's and Ellison's shares, rather than hold on to the red-hot Anthropic stock, Business Insider previously reported. The US Marshals Service Complex Assets Unit is typically tasked with dispensing shares of private companies acquired through criminal forfeiture.
Now we know that Anthropic purchased the stock from the feds in October 2025 at the price of $140.9676 per share, which is the same price Anthropic set for its Series F-1 stock in a fundraising round a month earlier.
Anthropic resold the shares to nine investors at the same $627 million total price it paid to purchase them from the federal government, so it didn't make a profit from the sale, according to the S-1 filing. It's not clear which nine investors purchased the shares originally owned by Singh and Ellison. Representatives for the US Marshals Service didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.
The Anthropic shares owned by Ellison and Singh took a different path than the $500 million in Series B shares owned by Bankman-Fried himself. His shares ended up as part of FTX's bankruptcy estate, which sold them in 2024 to a few dozen buyers for a total of $1.3 billion.
The proceeds went to repaying creditors, including FTX customers and investors who had been scammed by the former crypto wunderkind.
During Singh's and Ellison's sentencing hearings, Justice Department prosecutors told the federal judge overseeing their criminal cases that it would use the proceeds from the sale of Ellison's and Singh's Anthropic shares to compensate victims through a procedure called remission. The federal government would either establish its own claims administration process or work with the FTX bankruptcy process to identify victims and provide forfeited funds to them.
A Justice Department spokesperson told Business Insider it ""used funds from these forfeitures in remission,"" as a prosecutor said it would during Singh's and Ellison's sentencing hearings.
""One of the Justice Department's top priorities is returning forfeited funds to victims through the asset forfeiture program whenever the law allows,"" the spokesperson said.
This story has been updated with a statement from the Justice Department.

7,2026-10-26,"Neocloud companies are sliding even lower.
Shares dropped further on Monday after Elon Musk warned that many of them face ""guppy-level death.""
CoreWeave and Nebius have both fallen more than 30% over the past month, largely due to growing concerns about the debt they've taken on to build more data centers.
Soon after Monday's open, shares in both companies were down a further 4%, while Iren, another publicly traded neocloud, fell 2%.
The declines follow a string of X posts from Musk on Sunday evening questioning the neocloud business model.
""As of the past few weeks, debt markets have almost entirely shut off access to money for any company that doesn't have substantial revenue independent of AI data centers,"" he said.
""That means the 200+ neoclouds are going to see guppy-level death and consolidation,"" he added. ""Some will make it through, but not many.""
Musk said that while SpaceX has closed $40 billion in debt to buy GPUs, his company makes money through Starlink and rocket launches.
Meanwhile, investors are worried that a slowing AI boom could leave debt-heavy neoclouds struggling to service their loans.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
After one X user posted arguing that Musk was trying to drive down the share prices of neocloud companies to acquire them, Musk replied: ""SpaceX is not buying any neoclouds. Pointless.""
Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang remains bullish about the sector. ""The demand for the neoclouds is off the charts,"" he told CNBC last Thursday.
Nvidia holds significant stakes in CoreWeave, Nebius, and other cloud-computing companies.
A CoreWeave spokesperson said: ""The AI cloud market is not a monolith,"" and that lenders distinguish between speculative capacity and businesses with contracted revenue and a record of delivery.
They added that the company is ""already a market leader operating at scale"" with expertise, which it sees as key for opportunities to finance further growth.
A recent Bernstein Research report analyzed thousands of planned US data centers and estimated that only about 35% of the announced capacity will be built.
""Press releases are cheap … concrete is not,"" Bernstein analysts wrote in a research note.

8,2026-10-26,"As Microsoft sends out its buyout offers to more than a quarter of its workforce, specific offers reviewed by Business Insider reveal exactly what employees get if they choose to leave the company.
The offers are individualized for each employee based on their level, location, and tenure. Those reviewed by Business Insider confirm that the US buyouts align with the company's US standard severance offers. A Microsoft spokesperson said about 60,000 employees would be eligible for the buyout.
Microsoft uses a levels system to represent seniority, from entry levels starting at 59 to senior levels at 70, or vice president. The company offered buyouts to employees on select teams in the US, Canada, India, and the UK at level 70.
Offers reviewed by Business Insider show employees at internal levels 64 and below were offered one week of base pay per six months of service, and employees at levels 65 to 67 were offered two weeks of base pay per six months of service.
Buyout offers also include continued regular stock vesting and health insurance. The standard stock-vesting severance offer is six or 12 months for levels 67 and below, based on years of service.
Eligible employees will have until early December to decide whether to take the offer and exit at the end of the year, and there are no plans for another buyout at this time, a Microsoft spokesperson said.
Standard severance is capped at 39 weeks of base pay for levels 67 and below, while the standard severance offer for level 68 includes 12 months of salary.
Earlier this year, Microsoft rolled out a voluntary retirement program for employees. About 7% of Microsoft's US workforce, or about 8,750 people, were eligible at the time. As Business Insider previously reported, Microsoft's voluntary retirement program offer also aligned with the company's standard severance offer, including continued regular stock vesting for six or 12 months, based on years of service.
Earlier this year, another tech giant, Google, offered buyouts to staff in its business unit, Business Insider previously reported.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
As more tech companies offer buyouts, some human resources professionals previously told Business Insider that this approach could soften job cuts, and also that when they're made available to workers of all tenures, companies risk losing talent.

Ghost of YoteiSucker Punch Productions 
DespiteÂ Ghost of Yotei selling over 5 million copies since it launched last year, developer Sucker Punch Productions is undergoing layoffs, reminding us that even releasing a very commercially successful game is not enough to guarantee you’ll keep your job in 2026.
According to an internal email verified byÂ Aftermath, the cuts will affect 26 people at the roughly 229-person studio, with leadership citing “challenges in the market” and assessing the needs of its next project after Ghost of Yotei, though the email doesn’t specify what that next game might be.
After carefully observing the challenges in the market and reviewing the team needs for our next project, we’ve decided to make some staffing reductions. Several positions across Sucker Punch will be eliminated as a result.
We know this is a lot to take in, and we want to assure you that we did not make this decision lightly. We have given this a lot of attention and worked to support our affected colleagues during this transition as well as we can. â¦ There’s no getting around that this is a hard day. Next week we will go into more details about the future.
“Challenges in the market” is wild to read considering Ghost of Yotei was Sony’s best-selling game last year. Perhaps the actual “challenges” are that video games are too expensive to make and penny-pinching corporate executives are getting too large a slice of the pie?

12,2026-10-26,"Shortly after the iPhone Duo launched, a few users reported hinge issues that were causing cracks in the inner display. Apple has now responded to the reports in a statement to MacRumors, saying a small number of units are affected.


The response to iPhone Duo from early users has been incredibly positive, and we are seeing exciting things from developers who are rapidly updating their apps to take advantage of its unique form factor. We are aware of an issue impacting the inner display on a small number of iPhone Duo units. If users experience issues with their iPhone Duo display, they should contact AppleCare for a replacement.
Apple did not say what causes the cracking, or if a fix is planned. Affected users have been able to get in-store replacements, though with limited stock available, some are having to wait.
According to Apple, the problem is limited in scope. Most users are not going to see an issue with their ‌iPhone Duo‌ display, and it's not a defect people have to worry about showing up over time. On affected units, the crack seems to show up shortly after purchase. 
It is our understanding that some of the images of alleged cracking are AI-generated, which makes it harder to determine the actual scope of the problem.
Many of the iPhone Duos experiencing issues cracked in the same place on the lower half of the inner display. The crack is along the hinge, and users say there is a stuck feeling and sometimes a popping noise just before the damage appears.
iFixit took apart the ‌iPhone Duo‌ and found several possible causes for the hinge failure. The site said multiple components converge in that location, and it's possible a center support bar in the hinge that's supposed to retract isn't doing so, leading to damage.

13,2026-10-26,"
AILayoffsMicrosoft 
Microsoft’s latest cost-cutting plan involves offering buyouts to roughly a quarter of the company, essentially providing 60,000 people with the option of laying themselves off, Business Insider reports. They’ll get a generous severance package in exchange for choosing to voluntarily leave the tech giant.
“The Voluntary Exit Offer is new for us. We’ll learn from it,” Chief People Officer Amy Coleman wrote in a memo to staff. “At the same time, we’ll keep helping people focus on the work that matters most, build new skills, and, where it makes sense, find new opportunities across Microsoft. There is no one-size-fits-all approach for every team or every employee. These are a few of the ways we’re trying to meet different business needs while continuing to create opportunities for our own people to grow and contribute.”
It’s not immediately clear which divisions and departments within Microsoft will be eligible for the new cost-cutting program. Xbox is one of the more high-profile parts of Microsoft that has undergone substantial rounds of annual layoffs in recent years. The gaming division previously announced a goal of 3,200 layoffs through June 2027 as part of its current “reset” of Xbox.
This is the second voluntary layoff program Microsoft has instituted this year. The company launched a Voluntary Retirement Program back in the spring that gave veteran employees the chance to collect their severance packages and certain vested stock options early. The offer was extended to 9,000 employees, and about a third of them accepted, according to Business Insider.

14,2026-10-26,"Hellraiser: Revival is the perfect addition to horror writer Clive Barker’s boundlessly horny franchise because it’s also disgusting. Like the 1986 novella “The Hellbound Heart” and the several movies full of nipple-ripping and blood-suckling it inspired, developer Saber Interactive’s new action game is sexually depraved in a way that flies past Freud and into a pile of soiled underwearâand I’m grateful. I’m scared that the art of video games will be lost to major corporations’ mass layoffs in pursuit of untenable growth and live-service shooter after shooter, but I think Hellraiser: Revival can keep us safe in its big, flayed arms for now.Â Â 
The game follows protagonist Aidan Lynch, who is revealed to have a urethra piercing within the first 15 minutes of gameplay, as he tries to save his girlfriend Sunny from the extra-dimensional Cenobitesâincluding the illustrious Pinhead clad in supple black leather, voiced by original actor Doug Bradley. This is a game that bows to its source material’s freak factor, and it also often uses the very ’80s, dense science-fiction logic of movie sequel Hellbound: Hellraiser II (1988) to explain its unique Genesis Configuration puzzle box, the franchise’s monster god Leviathan, and the degenerate cult that wants Aidan shaved over rice.Â 
Nothing about Hellraiser: Revival can be mistaken as having “mass appeal.” As I play it, I often find myself in pleasant disbelief about this. Its first-person combat feels as lubricated as the best Call of Duty, and its sickeningly realistic graphics could easily slot into a fancy EA sports thing, if those games ever decided to feature people’s congested intestines falling out of their butts. Saber could have easily made an expensive, generic horror shooter that won’t get demonetized on YouTube, but it didn’t.Â 
Hellraiser: Revival is easygoing about this. The game’s executive producer, Nenad Tomic, said in a 2025 Xbox blog that Clive Barker was “deeply involved with the game from the very beginning,” and it shows. As a writer and director, Barker tends to deliver disgusting news with cheerful energy, like reapplying glitter lip gloss.
“It smelled of soured milk and fresh meat. When she was flung down the boards beneath her were wet and warm,” Barker writes straightforwardly in “The Hellbound Heart.” “Her belly wanted to turn inside out.”
Hellraiser: Revival, likewise, isn’t preoccupied by its goreâthere aren’t really any anguished screams or music cues to command you to be scared. (Light spoilers to follow). Instead, its developers seem to want players to witness all the ways the human mind and body can be corrupted. When I pause to take stock of all its details, I find Revival has everything: curved needles slid through taut skin, pregnant women parts sewn to other women parts as a grotesque statue, a meat grinder halfway through extruding pearls of a fatty human thighâ¦Â 
Since the Hellraiser franchise’s thesis is that pain and pleasure are indistinguishable, characters in Revival are also vulnerable, naked, and perverted whenever possible, presenting their shiny latex breasts to you at a party, encouraging you to use the girthiest dildo, and so on. Enemy cult members taunt you with how much they want you to plunge your little knife into their side, twist it until they burst. They wear tiny shreds of latex around their pubic bone and not much else.Â 
Cult members have this wrong idea that their devotion means anything to the Cenobites, so they cut up each other’s faces and vaginas hoping that their favorite demons will notice them as worthy of sweet suffering. I witness a torture scene in which one special cult member has the honor of getting her jaw removed and tongue extended, and I recall Hellraiser: Revival devs telling me offhand at Summer Game Fest that one of their gameplay demos made people puke.Â 
So, Hellraiser: Revival is not only alienating a broad consumer market, it is actively off-putting. Relatedly, Bulgarian-French theorist Julia Kristeva wonders in her 1980 treatise Powers of Horror, “In these times of dreary crisis, what is the point of emphasizing the horror of being?”Â 
She observes, “We prefer to foresee or seduce; to plan ahead, promise a recovery […] or make art not too far removed from the level of the media.” In the 2026 video game landscape, these more flattering kinds of art appear as AAA titles featuring burly men with an infinite capacity to be heroes, wield sniper rifles, and be replicated easily by generative AI.Â Â 
But we are not in a world that needs congratulations. Microsoft, EA, Ubisoft and other major corporations like feeding us sycophantic chatbots and hagiographic protagonists to encourage us to buy increasingly expensive, worse consoles. The more stuff we buy, the more distracted we are from the layoffs eroding every layer of the video game industry, since corporations assume we are like spit-soaked babies always focused on a new toy. But “abjection, when all is said and done, is the other facet of religious, moral, and ideological codes,” Kristeva says. Ad-friendly cleanliness isn’t the whole truth of our existenceâwe need vomit, blood, piss, and pus to be human. Therefore, we need repulsive art.Â 
And, as much as behemoth corporations try to dissuade me, I believe that AAA games are art. Hellraiser: Revival is our best, bleeding example to date, and it’s come at a perfect time. It counteracts cynical marketing and job loss across the industry with torture, a woman with the skin on her back torn up like butterfly wings, and it’s the only mainstream game to make me feel like my brain is working. Blood is flowing, and I am alive. The industry doesn’t need more artificial intelligence, but arterial incisions.

16,2026-10-26,"Apple's expected switch to OLED for the MacBook Pro will help accelerate a sharp decline in mini-LED laptop displays, with annual panel shipments forecast to fall roughly 75% between 2025 and 2027, according to Counterpoint Research.
Apple's ‌MacBook Pro‌ models have used mini-LED displays since 2021, but Counterpoint expects notebook panel shipments using the technology to drop 38% in 2026 and another 60% in 2027. Those consecutive declines would leave shipments at approximately a quarter of their 2025 level.
‌OLED‌ is heading in the opposite direction. Notebook ‌OLED‌ panel shipments are projected to grow 50% this year and 24% next year, amounting to an 86% increase over 2025. Counterpoint identifies the anticipated ‌OLED‌ ‌MacBook Pro‌ and demand for premium AI PCs as drivers, even as the overall notebook display market is expected to shrink 3% in 2026.
The forecast points to a rapid change in manufacturers' display choices. Counterpoint says ‌OLED‌'s thinner profile, image quality, and improving cost competitiveness are encouraging adoption, with tandem ‌OLED‌ designs and larger production facilities supporting further growth.
For ‌MacBook Pro‌ users, the display transition is also expected to bring touch input to the Mac for the first time. Samsung Display is reportedly the exclusive supplier of the new 14-inch and 16-inch ‌OLED‌ touch panels, manufactured on its 8.6-generation line in Asan, South Korea, according to The Elec.
Apple is expected to unveil the ‌OLED‌ ‌MacBook Pro‌ models on Wednesday, October 28 at its ""Pro and tell"" media experience in Los Angeles. According to Bloomberg's Mark Gurman, the laptops will be significantly lighter and replace the display notch with a Dynamic Island.
The ‌OLED‌ models are expected to sit above the existing mini-LED versions initially, giving buyers a choice during the transition. Gurman says Apple will eventually discontinue the mini-LED models, although the new ‌OLED‌ machines could carry starting prices up to $500 higher.

17,2026-10-26,"Elon Musk is a neocloud doomer.
The SpaceX CEO took a hard stance on the future of neoclouds — cloud companies like CoreWeave and Nebius, that specialize in providing computing power for AI — in a Sunday X post.
He wrote that debt markets are reluctant to finance any company that relies almost entirely on AI data centers for revenue. He said SpaceX has secured $40 billion in debt to buy GPUs, but unlike neoclouds, his company earns revenue from its Starlink and rocket-launch activities.
""That means the 200+ neoclouds are going to see guppy-level death and consolidation,"" he wrote. ""Some will make it through, but not many.""
Musk's post was in reply to one by venture capitalist Chamath Palihapitiya, who wrote on X that neoclouds are ""disorganized and poorly capitalized.""
Replying to another X thread speculating if he was trying to drive down the prices of neocloud stocks and scoop them up for cheap, Musk replied, ""SpaceX is not buying any neoclouds. Pointless.""
This comes as the stocks of neocloud companies have taken hits recently amid fears of high debt levels and a lack of other revenue streams. CoreWeave's stock has dropped about 37% in the past month, while Nebius' has dropped 30% in the same time.
Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, however, has maintained that demand for AI cloud infrastructure is ""off the charts."" Nvidia is a major backer of neocloud companies like CoreWeave, Nebius, Iren, Lambda, and others.
Another point that Musk added, in a separate X reply on Friday, was that the industry would see a lot of ""stranded compute in partially complete data centers.""

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
By ""stranded,"" Musk was referring to compute that is ready to use but can't be deployed because data centers aren't yet equipped to turn it on.
As Business Insider's Eugene Kim wrote last week, Amazon Web Services is expected to receive $5 billion worth of Nvidia GB300 racks, but the company does not have enough power in its data centers to power them. AWS will be storing the units in warehouses until its centers are ready.
Data centers, too, are running into some challenges. A recent Bernstein Research report projected that only about 35% of planned data center capacity in the US will be built.
Hyperscalers like Amazon, Google, Meta, and Microsoft, which have experience building and operating data centers, will likely successfully complete construction of their planned centers, the report predicted. 
But Bernstein Research said newcomers, like celebrity investor Kevin O'Leary whose company has pledged to build 15 gigawatts of data center capacity, were called ""bragawatts,"" and their projects are unlikely to be realized.
It's not just the neoclouds facing a troubled stretch amid an AI reckoning; neolabs are, too. Neolabs are small AI model development startups, like ex-OpenAI CTO Mira Murati's Thinking Machines Lab and OpenAI cofounder Ilya Sutskever's Safe Superintelligence. These labs are heavily funded and face strong competition from frontier AI behemoths like Anthropic and OpenAI.
Several VCs told Business Insider's Ben Bergman recently that neolabs were overhyped and investing in them was a contentious topic.

18,2026-10-26,"In 2020, Amazon introduced its ""Halo"" series of fitness wearables and accessories. The line included a faceless fitness band and, later, the ""Halo View"" with a touchscreen face, and even a bedside sleep-monitoring device called the ""Halo Rise."" They synced with the Halo app to track fitness data, provide recommendations, and even interact with Halo-exclusive workout videos. However, Amazon shuttered its Halo fitness brand in mid-2023 after just a few years of operation. Once the Halo line was shut down, so went the subscription and cloud servers that kept the Halo products operational.
While the official announcement from Amazon didn't give a specific reason, it's likely that the reason Amazon shut down Halo was that the profits just weren't enough. An internal memo from Amazon VP Melissa Cha, obtained by The Verge, pointed to an increasingly crowded market and an uncertain economic environment. Bloomberg noted that the shutdown was announced just days before an earnings report where cost-cutting measures, like shuttering struggling product lines, would likely appeal to investors. I personally used a Halo View when it was still operational, and from my experience with it, I can understand why Amazon's fitness line struggled to establish a meaningful customer base.
When the Halo line arrived on the scene, the fitness wearable market was already saturated. Fitbit had been on shelves for 11 years, and the ever-popular Apple Watch covered many people's fitness tracking needs in addition to all of its smartwatch capabilities. To succeed, Halo products had to stand out by offering novel features. And they did, but the unique features lacked mass appeal, to say the least. One of them was a mood tracker, of sorts, that let you know how your ""positivity"" and ""energy"" changed throughout the day by listening to the tone of your voice during conversations. Another could estimate your body fat for you — if you were willing to strip down to your underwear and take photos of yourself for Amazon's servers to analyze. No pinky promises about privacy were going to make those features not feel weird.
Even Halo's standard, non-creepy fitness features were lacking compared to the competition. It was missing basic features like distance tracking. The features the Halo line did have mostly relied on an active Halo subscription to work, while other fitness bands usually offered basic functionality under a ""freemium"" payment model. Amazon did offer a generous free subscription of up to a year with the purchase of a Halo device, which itself didn't cost much, with the wearables priced under $100. That generosity itself probably resulted in profitability issues that, in turn, led to the end of the Amazon Halo era.
In case you were wondering if you can still make use of a cheap second-hand Halo device you found on eBay or Facebook Marketplace, I can tell you from first-hand experience the answer is no. The Halo mobile app, which the tracker relied on to operate, is long gone, along with the subscription that locked many of its tracking features. My Halo View band, which I still have in storage, is essentially a brick now that I can only hope will someday become collectible. I've looked into open-source projects that reverse-engineer wearables to let you keep using them without subscriptions, like Noop, an open-source app that lets you use your Whoop band without a subscription, but I've not been able to find one that supports Halo trackers.
You're better off looking into alternate wearables other than smartwatches that can make your life easier to meet your fitness goals. Most of them don't have creepy features or force you to buy into an ecosystem that's about to be shuttered just to get basic health data."
19,2026-10-26,"Anthropic has warned artificial intelligence (AI) could pose ""catastrophic or existential risks to humanity"" as it unveiled details of its planned blockbuster stock market listing in the US.
The firm's valuation is expected to exceed that of SpaceX, which was the biggest-ever listing at an initial valuation of about $1.8 trillion in the summer.
Anthropic has not yet disclosed the number of shares to be released, their price range, or a date for the listing.
The Claude chatbot developer reported $16.3bn (Â£12.2bn) of revenue in the first half of the year, with losses of more than $80.7bn. 
Anthropic, which said it will list on the Nasdaq under the symbol ANT, will be the first fully AI-focused company to go public, making it a test case for the future of the technology.
In a filing ahead of its listing, Anthropic said concerns had been raised by policymakers, regulators and researchers as well as by people within its organisation that AI systems posed a significant risk to humanity.
It said advanced AI systems ""could, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity, particularly if such systems were misused, replicated, weaponised, or allowed to operate with a high degree of autonomy"".
Anthropic was founded five years ago by chief executive Dario Amodei, his sister Daniela Amodei and five others, and announced its intention to go public in June.
Its main rival, OpenAI, quickly followed suit. That listing is now expected to occur early next year, according to a person familiar with OpenAI's plans.
Those announcements were followed by a wave of cyber-security and other technological breaches involving their AI agents - bots designed to operate somewhat autonomously.
Current and former staff of both companies also warned AI technology was being developed without the ability to protect humanity from possible future harms enacted by AI tools.
As Anthropic revealed its stock debut plans on Monday, the owners of the rights to Elvis Presley's music sued the AI firm accusing it of using hundreds of the rock-and-roll star's songs to train its chatbot Claude.
Anthropic's blockbuster share listing - 5 surprising things we now know
In Anthropic's new documents, it reiterated promises it has made in the past to develop AI in a way that is beneficial to people, while at the same time warning investors of its dangers.
Anthropic detailed losses in its financial filings that exceeded its revenue for the first half of the year, but said it would often make forward investments ""that may seem irresponsibly large at first glance"".
""In general, we will aim to be bold but sane, and to win trust with a track record of successful bets,"" the tech giant said.
Its success depends largely on its ability to develop the most advanced AI models, Anthropic said, adding, ""any failure to do so could adversely affect our business"".
Across about 90 pages detailing possible risk factors, Anthropic set out the potential threats to its ability to safely and competitively develop AI. 
Anthropic warned it relies on a cluster of cloud computing providers, Taiwanese chipmakers already facing huge demand and data centres concentrated in the US.
It also warned of ""regulatory action arising from harms associated with our AI models"" - citing concerns over AI being used by children or individuals at risk of suicide - and uncertainty about where and how it might be held liable for actions taken by its systems.
However, Anthropic also used the filing to laud the potential benefits of the tech itself, envisioning it unlocking ""millions of virtual agents with the ability to revolutionise every area of human endeavour"" across a range of fields.
""We believe that AI is one of the most consequential technologies in human history,"" the firm said.
Anthropic said AI would also ""profoundly accelerate the rate of progress in biology and medicine"", potentially finding cures to diseases including cancer and Alzheimer's.
The firm added it could also establish ""sustained acceleration in economic growth"" by boosting productivity.
Matt Orton, chief market strategist at investment management firm Raymond James Investment, said investors expected to see rapid growth as well as rapid financial losses in Anthropic's finances, so Monday's filing would not come as a surprise.
""Getting the details from this filing, I think will help provide the market with more visibility, because we need to know where its revenue growth is coming from,"" he told the BBC.
Peter Andersen, founder of Andersen Capital Management, told Reuters that Anthropic's plans to list was ""a significant milestone in the development of artificial intelligence"".
He added that the outcome of its stock market debut would be ""fairly indicative of the sentiment"" among investors to continue ploughing cash into AI development.
Why are there concerns AI could threaten humanity, and how real are they?

This will be the cheapest way to upgrade for GTA6 (if you can grab one)!

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If you're still waiting for a reasonable price on next-gen consoles to upgrade for Grand Theft Auto 6, you'll be waiting a long time. That said, Argos have just made a surprise cut to the Xbox Series S 512GB model, taking it down to pre-RAMpocalypse prices of £299.99. I can't believe I'm calling this a deal but at this point in 2026 with the current climate, it actually is the cheapest way to upgrade to current gen right now and I can't see stocks lasting much longer. In fact, I'd argue the even better deal is the 1TB model at £349.99, down from £519.99, as the extra storage is kind of essential nowadays.
If you are still gaming on older hardware and looking for the absolute most cost effective way to get ready for GTA6 and the next wave of current-gen exclusives, this is officially the lowest barrier to entry. The Xbox Series S is fully digital, so no disc drive, but does play all the same games as the Xbox Series X. There is also a 1TB model on sale, for those who will easily fill up a 512GB SSD. Xbox Series S Review ⭐⭐⭐⭐
This deal couldn't have come at a better time for UK consumers, given that Xbox stock is so dire right now that Xbox is begging lapsed users to trade in their consoles and make them available for new customers. The Xbox Series S has always been the easiest and cheapest way to get into Xbox's eco system, and launched in November 2020 for £249.99 as an alternative to the beefier Xbox Series X, but it plays all of the same current generation games. I wrote a full Xbox Series S review if you want to read further, but I still love and use mine daily (and at current prices it will be a family heirloom.)
Those prices are now a thing of the past, and £299.99 is a bargain by current standards, with the price being recently hiked to a whopping £519.99. Thanks to Argos, we are now at a reasonable price point again, but that will be entirely dependent on your postcode and whether there are any in stock, so go fast!

21,2026-10-26,"Every computing device, big or small, has seen an astounding price rise in the last few months. Memory and storage prices have quadrupled, and it's even hard to find “in-stock” products. Like everything else, the Raspberry Pi lineup is feeling the impact. Nothing is selling at its original price, and if you've got an older board like the Raspberry Pi 4 Model B, it's a valuable commodity now.
The board comes in multiple memory versions, and each one now sells for double (or more) on e-commerce platforms. It's amusing that a board that's built on older technology has a higher cost. Usually, it's the other way round, but these are testing times. Let's discuss the current pricing of Raspberry Pi 4 Model B boards, and why it's still useful for home servers.
 The 1GB Raspberry Pi 4 Model B launched at $35 in 2019. The 2GB version costs $45, while you could snag a 4GB version for just $55. Even an 8GB version launched a year later at $75. Five years back, this seemed expensive. The Raspberry Pi 5 launched with just a $5 increase in price for the same memory capacity but a newer generation (LPDDR4X).
I lost interest in the older board because the memory was 1.5 times faster than the Raspberry Pi 4 Model B, along with other upgrades. Currently, the 1GB Raspberry Pi 4 Model B version is the only one without a price hike. All the other versions have updated their pricing to reflect the memory apocalypse. The 2GB version retails for $67.50, while the more popular 4GB version is available for $83.
Though, it's not all bad news
These are official prices, but realistic pricing is $5–15 more for high-demand boards. I'm surprised by the 8GB version's price because it's more than double, and now sells for $165. The 1GB model prices have been kept intact because these boards find a lot of use in industrial applications. Higher-density memory (4GB and 8GB) has seen the biggest price spike, which is good news for hoarders and reseller markets.
The Raspberry Pi 4 lineup was modestly priced at its launch time. If you bought these boards anywhere between 2019 and early 2025, you would've escaped the inflated pricing. Some who have multiple Raspberry Pi 4 boards can make good money in the reseller or even box-sealed market. I don't expect average users to be sitting on piles of these SBC boxes. But small retailers and some hoarders will surely benefit from this unexpected opportunity.
If you've got a couple of spares and have upgraded to Raspberry Pi 5s, you can earn some profit in the used market. However, it's not a great time for consumers. They have to pay more money for an older-generation board, and the limited availability makes it even worse. Even the used boards will sell for a few dollars less than the current price. So, it'll be a bad couple of years for anyone looking to build a cheap mini server.
Not all SBCs cost an arm and a leg
A refurbished thin client seems like a better proposition now. I recently bought one for about $45 with 8GB of memory and a 128GB SSD, and both components are new. So the price-to-performance ratio doesn't seem appealing enough. I also purchased a 1GB Raspberry Pi 4 Model B for $35, and adding an SD card bumped its price to $60. Apart from the low power usage, there is nothing in favor of either the Raspberry Pi 4 or 5 lineups. But that doesn't make your old Raspberry Pi 4 Model B a bad device.
 Ignoring the current prices, the Raspberry Pi has the best software support and community, and that advantage is hard to beat. I've tried boards from other brands, but they are nowhere close to this one. The new Raspberry Pi 4 Model B, except for the 1GB version, is not a smart pick. But if you already own the board and haven't built anything with it in a while, now’s the chance.
Even the lowest memory capacity in the Raspberry Pi 4 Model B lineup can act as a great local cloud storage alternative, a DNS sinkhole and server, or a password manager. I've run some of these projects on a Raspberry Pi Zero 2 W, so the Raspberry Pi 4 Model B will undoubtedly have better performance. The 1GB memory versions can run most of the self-hosted tools without falling short of memory.
Old DDR3 single-board computers actually beat the Raspberry Pi on price
The focus has also shifted to more lightweight tools like BentoPDF, Papra, Vaultwarden, and the board can run multiple tools like these. Another advantage is that it's a full-sized board with all the necessary ports. The Ethernet port and additional USB ports are an advantage for network-focused projects like Pi-Hole.
Even the dedicated 3.5 mm audio port is great for audio-focused projects like reviving old speakers that lack HDMI. If you use a Raspberry Pi Zero 2 W or even the Raspberry Pi 5, you'll have to buy an additional USB DAC to get the job done. While it may not support native NVMe SSDs or AI HAT modules, it can still offer better storage speeds via the USB 3.0 connection ports.
Old hardware tends to depreciate, but this memory and storage apocalypse has turned the tables for them. An old SBC like the Raspberry Pi 4 was slowly becoming less popular after the launch of the Raspberry Pi 5. But the current pricing suggests otherwise, and even with a huge spike from the original rates, the demand is there. The 4GB and 8GB editions seem to be out of stock everywhere because anyone looking for serious computing projects will want them. Meanwhile, you can rejoice a bit because your old board is worth more now.
The Raspberry Pi 4 is a single-board computer that can be used for much different DIY computing projects, from programming to retro gaming emulation.

The app that wanted to be the ""HBO"" of micro dramas is shutting down.
Prolific film producer Tommy Harper is shutting down his app, VeYou, after about six months. A message on VeYou said the service would end November 6.
Harper told Business Insider he would continue producing series, distributing them via TikTok's micro-drama app PineDrama, YouTube, and other partners.
Harper, whose credits include ""Star Wars"" and ""Top Gun: Maverick,"" is one of several Hollywood vets trying to bring their prestige sensibility to micro dramas, the soapy, low-budget series that got their start in China and have exploded in the US.
The move shows how fast the space is changing as Big Tech companies lean in. Micro dramas — also called ""verticals"" — cost a fraction of a traditional feature-length movie, but apps often lose money because of the high cost of marketing, and VeYou didn't have massive financial backing.
""In the past year, TikTok, Meta, and YouTube all moved hard into short drama,"" Harper emailed. ""When the three biggest attention platforms commit to a format, distribution is decided. The opportunity for an independent company is to be the best supplier to those platforms, not to compete with them for installs. So we're focused where we have the edge: making the shows.""
Harper raised an undisclosed amount of seed funding for VeYou from investor S32, a venture firm started by Google Ventures founder Bill Maris, who's also backed 23andMe, Impossible Foods, and Nest. VeYou has made a handful of originals, starting with ""Love Under Fire,"" a romance drama starring Kasey Esser, who wrote the series alongside Harper.
The micro drama space has changed dramatically in recent months.
In the past year, AI-generated characters have increasingly taken their place alongside human actors, and free, ad-supported offerings are competing with the ""freemium"" apps that had dominated the landscape.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
A report by media consultancy Owl & Co. found two-thirds of films released by leading apps in the third quarter of this year outside China were AI-generated, up from 3% in the first quarter.
TikTok's free app PineDrama rolled out in January, and by the third quarter, it accounted for more than half of all micro drama views across 16 leading apps, according to the Owl & Co. report.
Other tech giants have also moved quickly into the space and made themselves bigger viewing destinations for micro dramas.
YouTube released a new feature that allows creators to bundle their short videos in a series of episodes or seasons, designed to be viewed sequentially.
On Meta, the dominant platform used by micro drama apps to acquire customers, some apps are running portions of their films lasting 20 minutes or more as ads to get people to subscribe.
Lori Golden-Stryer, the CEO of RIVR Media, a producing partner for VeYou, said VeYou's films set themselves apart through their scripts, acting, and direction. She said she was encouraged to see its series perform well on TikTok, based on platform data and her company's monitoring.
""People want live characters,"" she said. ""I don't think they want AI. I've seen a lot of different verticals, and I'd like to think ours were a little cut above.""

28,2026-10-26,"Investors react to Lula’s election victory with a sharp decline in the Brazilian real and concerns over increased spending and fiscal challenges.
Save
Share
Brazil’s real dropped against the United States dollar on the heels of Brazil’s President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva’s unexpected re-election after right-wing Flavio Bolsonaro, the son of former President Jair Bolsonaro, led in the first round of voting earlier this month.
Brazil’s currency dropped against the dollar by 12 percent in spot trading. Bovespa stock index futures tumbled nearly 15 percent on Monday.
“I’m not surprised the real is sinking sharply; traders are covering their bets that assume a Bolsonaro victory and tighter fiscal policy,” Rachel Ziemba, adjunct senior fellow at the Center for a New American Security, told Al Jazeera.
Brazil’s stock exchange halted trading for 30 minutes. The Bovespa index fell 10 percent from its previous close, triggering a circuit-breaker safety mechanism and reversing a rally earlier this month amid expectations of a shift to the right after Bolsonaro won the first round.
Ziemba says Bolsonaro’s refusal to concede the election has driven some financial uncertainty.
“The market pressure is also amplified by the objections raised by Bolsonaro, but the scale of the victory suggests that the result will stand,” she said.
Interest rate futures had also reflected expectations that tighter fiscal policy under Bolsonaro would allow borrowing costs to decline.
“They [investors] assume more domestic spending and higher rates in Brazil. However, the real is still likely to be supported by strong commodity revenues,” Ziemba noted.
Stocks closed on Friday almost 7 percent above their level before the first round, while the Brazilian real was nearly 5 percent stronger against the US dollar.
Interest rate futures are up 3.1 percent, rising sharply after President Lula’s win. Interest rate futures had previously reflected a tighter fiscal policy amid expectations that Bolsonaro would oust the incumbent and that he would allow borrowing costs to decline.
Lula faces an increase in Brazil’s debt and will be challenged with protecting both social programmes and investment, with investors looking for signs of whether his gradual fiscal approach can bring borrowing costs down.
Lula’s team aims to produce a budget surplus of about 1.3 percent of gross domestic product (GDP) by 2030. That would leave money to cover part of the interest bill, reducing the need for fresh borrowing. But public debt already exceeds 82 percent of GDP, while pensions and other legally required spending limit where savings can come from.
“Lula’s policies have talked about spending cuts, but he has not gone into the details of how that is going to look,” said Divya Singaravelu, head of research at Meridian 1305 Capital.
Attention will now turn to Lula’s choice of finance minister and what it signals about his fiscal approach.

Anthropic confirms plans to go public despite shaky financials

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AI giant Anthropic has revealed more details on its plans to go public in what is likely to be the biggest stock market debut in history - higher than SpaceX’s record breaking valuation of $1.8 trillion earlier this year.
The company, which announced its plans to go public back in June 2026, will also be the AI firm to go public. OpenAI has also announced plans to become a publicly traded company shortly after, but will likely debut on the market early next year.
Anthropic has reported losses of more than $80.7 billion in the first half of the year, with a revenue of $16.3 billion over the same period. The company will list on the Nasdaq under the symbol ‘ANT’.
Despite these financials, the company has said it will make investments “that may seem irresponsibly large at first glance.”
“In order to fully capture the opportunity in front of us, we need to invest more aggressively than would a business that operates in a more slowly changing technological regime,"" Anthropic said. ""In general, we will aim to be bold but sane, and to win trust with a track record of successful bets.""
As part of being listed as a publicly traded company, Anthropic will have to provide greater visibility into the company’s finances - including whether or not the company is currently profitable. Much of the economic growth in the US is currently driven by AI companies, and the greater transparency into the finances of one of the biggest AI companies in the world could have serious implications for the economy.
The company has not revealed how many shares will be released, nor their price range.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
Founded five years ago by chief executive Dario Amodei, his sister Daniela Amodei and five other co-founders, Anthropic’s history up to this point has been a whirlwind of breakthroughs and controversy.
The company’s vulnerability hunting model, Claude Mythos, was dubbed as too dangerous to be released publicly. Under Project Glasswing, select countries have been given access to the model to help hunt for flaws and vulnerabilities in critical services and infrastructure.
Claude AI agents undergoing testing were also involved in hacking three companies earlier this year during an explosion of “rogue” AI agent events that have also affected OpenAI and Meta.
Numerous researchers and staff at major AI companies have warned about the potential threats of AI. Former Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon said that workers are “genuinely frightened” about the pace of AI advancement, adding “I believe that if we don't slow down at the current rate of progress, there is a strong chance that we could all die in the immediate future.”

Spot why Google Ads targets fail to drive business growth, from outdated profit margins and repeat buyers to weak leads and limits on profitable reach.
Smart Bidding is optimized to achieve its targets, not necessarily to grow the business. That’s why an ad account can consistently hit its Target CPA or Target ROAS while revenue, profit, and overall business growth barely change.
Unfortunately, this is often the result of a disconnect between advertising metrics and business reality. The algorithm keeps optimizing toward the goal it was given, even when that goal is no longer the bottleneck to growth.
That disconnect may become even more important following Google’s August 17 bidding update. Google has indicated that budget-constrained campaigns using tCPA and tROAS will now be pulled closer toward their stated targets rather than significantly outperforming them.
In practical terms, the number in the target field in account settings should no longer be viewed as a suggestion. It’s an instruction.
If that instruction is outdated, Smart Bidding may execute it more faithfully than ever.
Which raises an important question: What causes an account to hit its goals while the business itself stops growing?



Very few targets in the ad platform get revisited as COGS, shipping rates, overhead, product mix, and customer behavior shift over time.
Most ROAS and CPA targets get set once and forgotten.
They come from historical performance, an industry benchmark, or a number that felt safe at the time. Very few get revisited as COGS, shipping rates, overhead, or product mix shift.
A 4.0x ROAS on a product line running a 20% margin produces a completely different financial reality than a 4.0x ROAS on a line running 60%. If your targets don’t reflect current unit economics, hitting them might mean breaking even or acquiring customers at a loss while the ad account reports a massive win.
See also: How To Calculate Your ROAS & Ways To Use It
Smart Bidding tends to favor the audiences and queries most likely to help it achieve its target. Returning customers and people already familiar with your brand convert faster and cheaper than cold prospects, so bids naturally drift toward them.
An account can hit every target on paper simply by repeatedly converting people who were going to buy anyway. The metrics look strong, but the actual customer base never expands.
See also: The Conversion Setup Errors That Break Smart Bidding
Accounts weighted heavily toward branded search or retargeting tend to post the best ROAS in the portfolio. In many businesses, a significant share of that revenue would have arrived anyway through organic search, direct traffic, or existing customer demand.
When those campaigns take full attribution credit, reported ROAS climbs while true incremental revenue stays flat. The account looks like it’s growing when it’s really just attributing performance to Google Ads.
See also: The PPC Clean-Up: How to Audit and Fix ‘Value Inflation’ in Google Ads
Lead generation and B2B accounts frequently optimize for form fills, PDF downloads, or booked calls. Those are reasonable proxy signals, but they aren’t revenue.
The ad account can produce a record month of conversions while the actual sales pipeline produces nothing new. In many accounts, the bidding system continues optimizing based on historical conversion patterns long after the quality of those conversions has changed.



Strict efficiency targets force the algorithm to sit out auctions that cost more, even when those auctions would have produced profitable, net-new volume.
A highly efficient campaign that has stopped growing is often doing precisely what its target allows.
The ceiling is in your account settings, not the market of new potential customers.
The highest-growth opportunities are often more expensive than the easiest conversions.
By insisting on an extremely low CPA or extremely high ROAS target, you’re effectively telling the algorithm to ignore portions of the market that may still be profitable. The result is an account that becomes increasingly efficient while capturing less of the available demand in the larger market.
When every conversion is valued equally, Smart Bidding has no way to distinguish between your best customers and your worst customers.
Not every customer brings the same value to the table.
If bidding is built around immediate CPA, the algorithm will naturally favor price-sensitive, low-tier buyers who convert easily and churn just as fast.
For subscription models, SaaS, or any business where lifetime value matters, a wave of cheap acquisitions that cancel after month one will drag company growth down while the ad account reports stellar efficiency.
Sometimes the ad account is doing everything right, but the rest of the business breaks the chain. Leads come in and sit untouched for three days. Orders arrive for products that are out of stock. Onboarding stalls out.
Google Ads can only deliver demand to the front door. If revenue leaks out between the conversion event and the balance sheet, no bid strategy will show it, and no bid strategy can fix it.
Diagnosing these issues means asking questions the platform doesn’t prompt you to ask on its own:
Success in Google Ads answers one question: Did the ad account hit the target it was instructed to hit?
Business growth answers a different question entirely.
What really matters is whether that target still reflects what the business needs to grow. Until someone compares Google Ads targets and conversion goals against actual financial results, the bidding algorithm can appear highly successful while business growth remains flat.

32,2026-10-26,"Every weekend, my partner makes the best breakfast: over-medium eggs, handmade tortillas, and fresh hash browns.
We often eat outside thanks to Colorado's bright, sunny days, then we'll walk around the lake, watch some football, and spend the evening roasting marshmallows over the fire pit.
Then, when Sunday night arrives, I get in my car and drive the 45 minutes back to my dream condo to get ready for the week ahead.
My partner and I met at work 13 years ago, and we intend to spend the rest of our lives together, but we still live apart. We know each other's rhythms, quirks, and needs, and have no secrets, just separate addresses.
I often joke that we're in a ""middle-distance"" relationship. At least we're in the same state, but it's also not always easy or convenient to drop by for dinner.
Both of us are divorced, and we both love the houses we have.
My partner lives in rural northeastern Colorado and bought his home after his divorce. It's bigger than mine, with a huge yard and a custom-designed patio built for entertaining.
His neighborhood isn't exactly walkable, but he's originally from a small town in Texas, so he doesn't mind driving longer distances to get to places he wants to go.
Meanwhile, after my divorce, I moved back into the Boulder condo I had been renting out while married. My unit is smaller than my partner's, but I love its mountain views and proximity to all my favorite places, like my go-to local bakery.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
It needed a lot of work when I moved back in, but I invested in new paint and flooring to really make it mine again.

Though we've toured many houses together over the last six months, we haven't been impressed by any of them enough to leave the places we've already made our own.
One thing that has made this arrangement easier is that we're used to living out of our suitcases. I'm a professional travel writer, and he works for a company based on the East Coast. We also love to explore new places together whenever we can.
When we are both home in Colorado, we've found a routine that has worked pretty well for us. My partner often spends some weeknights here, where we're close to restaurants, friends, walkable downtown Boulder, and other things to do after work.
Then, I'll go up to his house for quiet, relaxed weekends, and to watch my favorite team on the 100-inch screen (go Raiders!). We also tend to entertain at his bigger house.
Both of us get bored easily, so having a change of scenery and routine keeps life feeling fresh. My 14-year-old pug has adapted, too, and travels with me between places.
To make things easier, I've bought duplicates of staples like deodorant and an electric toothbrush, so I'm not schlepping them (or forgetting them!) every weekend.
That said, there are challenging things about our setup. Packing up every few days can be a hassle, and it can be stressful to land at the airport and decide which house to go to, especially when I miss both my partner and the feeling of being ""home.""
We always make an effort to overcommunicate our plans and extend understanding whenever one of us just needs to get home to do a load of laundry.
Every day, I'm scrolling listings that meet both our picky criteria and calling up agents for showings, but so far, we haven't loved anything as much as our current homes.
It sounds frustrating, but we both think this is a pretty good problem to have: It gives us more leverage in the homebuying process and means we don't have to settle for something that is only half-right or makes just one of us happy.
We're confident that we'll find a home that fits us both, but in the meantime, navigating the middle-distance has only made our relationship stronger.

33,2026-10-26,"Ukraine said its access to Starshield was restored this weekend, but Elon Musk isn't finished with Kyiv yet.
In an X post on Sunday, Musk accused Ukrainian forces of breaching Starlink's terms of service by using the civilian platform in drone attacks.
""Ukraine military violated Starlink terms of service and used it for offensive drone strikes to kill people. That loophole was closed last week,"" wrote the SpaceX owner.
""Using Starlink, a civilian commercial service, for offensive drone strikes to kill people has never been allowed, nor should it be,"" he added.
Musk made it clear he was talking about the commercial platform and not Starshield, the military version of the satellite communications service owned and controlled by the US government.
""Were Starlink to be turned off, the Ukrainian front line would collapse immediately. This is NOT a threat, it is simply a statement of fact,"" Musk added.
Two prominent Ukrainian drone figures, however, disputed Musk's statement, saying that it was technically impossible for any of Kyiv's troops to have independently used Starlink in drone strikes.
""As someone who was inside the process at the Ministry of Defense, I can say for sure that this is not true,"" wrote Serhii Sternenko, a former advisor for drones to Ukraine's defense ministry, on his Telegram channel.
""It is very strange that Elon Musk does not know about the restrictions on Starlink operation in Ukraine,"" wrote Serhii ""Flash"" Beskrestnov, another former advisor.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Both worked in the ministry in early 2026, when it negotiated with SpaceX to prevent Russia from using black-market Starlink terminals in the war. SpaceX agreed in February to block Starlink over Ukraine and Russia, allowing access only to whitelisted Ukrainian terminals.
Sternenko said that Ukrainian civilians and troops could register their terminals on the whitelist, but that these would only work while subject to a speed requirement.
For one to connect while moving, it would have to be put on a special list that uses Starshield instead, he said.
""That is, it was technically impossible to use civilian Starlink to perform missions on drones. All drone missions that required satellite communication could only be carried out using Starshield,"" Sternenko added.
Beskrestnov said the Ukrainian Starlink terminals were subject to a speed limit of 90 kmph, or about 55 mph. The civilian service is also completely cut off over Russia, he added.
""Thus, we physically cannot use commercial Starlink for deep strikes and middle strikes,"" Beskrestnov wrote.
Tactical drones operating on the Ukrainian battlefield at speeds below 55 mph, such as first-person-view attack drones, typically use radio-frequency signals rather than satellite communication to maintain contact with an operator. These are also often too small and lack enough onboard power to reliably carry even a Starlink Mini terminal.
What Ukraine usually uses satellite internet for — hence its reliance on Starshield — is mid-range and deep strikes on Russia's rear or Russian territory, with larger, more powerful drones that fly much faster.
Kyiv also relies on satellite communications for its small interceptor drones, which fly at up to 265 mph to catch incoming attack drones.
Musk followed up on his Sunday X post with a screenshot of Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy writing on social media in mid-October that Kyiv's forces ""also use Starlink for mid-range strikes"" against Russian targets.
However, Zelenskyy's post was describing Ukraine's recent troubles with Starshield, and he repeatedly used the term ""Starlink"" to refer broadly to the military satellite service instead.
Musk and SpaceX did not respond to requests for comment sent outside regular business hours by Business Insider.
Earlier this month, Ukraine raised the alarm that its access to Starshield was being throttled at high speeds, reducing its ability to conduct deeper strikes and to control interceptor drones.
The crisis lasted 10 days and came as President Donald Trump and Musk openly criticized Ukraine for targeting Russian oil facilities at a time when the White House was trying to ease fuel prices.
The Wall Street Journal reported, citing unnamed US officials and sources, that Musk had personally ordered the speed cap. Musk and Trump have separately said they were not responsible for the slowdown.
On Saturday, Zelenskyy said Starshield services were running normally again.
