35,2026-10-26,"Independent modders have released a series of firmware-level utilities that effectively circumvent the artificial limits NVIDIA places on its consumer cards. The result is a near doubling of the machine learning and AI compute performance of the RTX 20, 30, and 40 series hardware on Linux systems. You can try it out for yourself via the KohakUnlock repo, but it will void your warranty.
LACT will overclock your machine with almost no restrictions.
For the average user, it's a long-standing pain point with corporate software gatekeeping. NVIDIA has traditionally built its consumer gaming graphics cards on the same silicon as its high-end workstation cards, but used firmware to limit consumer hardware's raw mathematical throughput.
Starting with the GeForce RTX 30-series ""Ampere,"" a researcher created a tool that makes these graphics cards process certain math instructions twice as fast. This means cards like the GeForce RTX 3090 Ti can do matrix calculations up to 80% faster. This tool works on GeForce RTX cards with GA102, GA103, GA104, and GA106 chips.
For the newer GeForce RTX 40-series ""Ada Lovelace,"" another tool doubles the speed for certain types of math used in AI. For example, with this unlock, the GeForce RTX 4090's performance jumped from 165.6 to 331.6 trillion operations per second. Normally, only expensive professional cards like the NVIDIA RTX 6000 ""Ada Lovelace"" reach these speeds. It appears NVIDIA purposely slowed consumer cards for some AI tasks, but this unlock lets you restore their full power on AD102, AD103, and AD106 chips.
The newly developed open-pu-unlock and related modules intercept the boot process before official drivers bind to the kernel, overriding PCIe Function Level Reset restrictions. Rather than modifying the permanent video BIOS, the utility removes software bottlenecks on instruction-issuing rates for FP16 and BF16 matrix calculations. In benchmarks, high-end consumer cards like the GeForce RTX 4090 and RTX 3090 Ti achieve nearly double their previous tensor throughput, effectively matching workstation hardware that normally costs thousands of dollars more.
The engineering feat is certainly impressive, but the real-world impact on the average person is mixed and dependent on how you use your computer. PC gamers, in particular, should know these changes provide no benefit to traditional games, real-time rendering, or frame rates, since rendering engines already use the available graphics resources.
Instead, the main beneficiaries are hobbyists, developers, and researchers running their own local LLMs or doing the sort of heavy lifting you get with AI upscalers (e.g., AI upscaling of video), where the faster your maths throughput, the more tokens you can process.
At the same time, the community feels real unease about the secondary market. iameatingjam on the forum pointed out that if you're going to unlock enterprise-level AI performance on an older consumer architecture, you're going to create a lot of secondary market demand. If the hobbyists and small developers start to go out and pick up the older-gen consumer cards in order to build their own cheap ML rigs, then that's going to further drive up the demand and the price and will effectively price out the average gamer who just wants to get an entry-level card. For the moment, they're still Linux-only, require manual configuration at boot, and will void your hardware warranty.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

36,2026-10-26,"

Artificial intelligence startup Anthropic on Monday unveiled its public IPO filing ahead of what is expected to be one of the largest stock debuts in history.
That offering, widely expected to come sometime in mid-November, could value the Claude developer at more than $2 trillion. That could surpass the current record holder, Elon Musk’s SpaceX, which raised $75 billion in an IPO in June that valued the rocket-AI-social-media firm at $2.1 trillion.
The filing did not include a potential share price, the number of shares to be offered or a valuation for the whole company, all of which will be determined after Anthropic holds a “road show” with investors, prospective investors and its bankers.
Monday’s filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission showed that Anthropic, like other AI firms, is splashing out unprecedented amounts of cash on cloud services to run its AI models.
The company plans to spend at least $110 billion with Amazon for cloud services through April 2036 and another $120 billion with SpaceX through 2029. 
Anthropic also said it planned to spend $115 billion with Google and $45 billion with Microsoft, both through 2033, for computing infrastructure.
The filing said that the company recorded revenue growth of “1,368% year over year for the three months ended June 30, 2026.”
The cost of renting servers and chips grew by 300% over the same period.
Anthropic’s net loss for the first six months of 2026 was $80.7 billion. For the entirety of 2025, the company reported a $42 billion net loss.
Anthropic added that it expects its current cash and sales to be sufficient for the next 12 months but warned that future revenue growth depends on a number of important factors, including the cost of infrastructure. As a result, it said, “we may be required to seek additional financing sooner than we currently anticipate.” 
The company said it has raised about $141 billion of capital through the end of June and is sitting on $112.4 billion of cash.
The company plans to list on the Nasdaq under the ticker symbol “ANT.” Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley are leading the historic offering, as they did with SpaceX’s record deal.
But already, the offering is coming slightly later than expected as recent market moves give investors pause about the trajectory of the seemingly ever-expanding AI boom. 
Monday’s filing is unlikely to calm those fears.
Alongside its dramatic spending and borrowing figures, Anthropic warned that it “may not generate sufficient revenue to achieve or sustain profitability in the future.”
“While we have experienced significant revenue growth in recent periods, our business is highly capital intensive,” the company said. “Our revenue growth may not be sustainable, and our rate of growth may decrease.” 
Nevertheless, Anthropic told regulators and investors that it plans to keep burning through capital: “We expect our costs will increase over time and our losses will continue as we expect to invest significant resources towards growing our business and operating as a public company.”
On Sunday, a top Wall Street economist warned that the sector had yet to deliver broad gains to the wider economy. 
“Despite massive investments and widespread hype around artificial intelligence, the technology has delivered zero financial payoff to corporate America outside of tech,” said Torsten Slok, chief economist at investment firm Apollo Global Management. 
“Profit margins are rising for the Mag 7 but are flat for the S&P 493,” he wrote Sunday, referring to the companies left in the benchmark S&P 500 after subtracting Meta, Microsoft, Alphabet, Apple, Amazon, Nvidia and Tesla. “And there are still no signs of AI boosting productivity growth.”
The Nasdaq 100, which tracks the largest public AI developers, is up 15% this year. That’s more than the broader S&P 500’s 11% gain so far this year.
Market jitters over potential cracks in the AI boom have also caused a number of smaller IPOs to put their plans on hold. Earlier this month, a number of small offerings delayed or canceled their plans to go public, citing “market conditions.”
But still, the largest AI companies continue to raise massive amounts of funding for their projects. On Monday, Meta Platforms announced an up to $95 billion stock offering, while SpaceX said it had secured $40 billion in funding to buy Nvidia graphics processing units, which it called the largest type of fundraising ever.
As part of every stock offering, companies are asked to outline risks and uncertainties facing their businesses. Some of the risk factors outlined by Anthropic included safety, reliable computing infrastructure and its ability to “navigate an evolving, uncertain, and at times divergent global regulatory landscape.”
“Concerns have been raised both within our organization and by other researchers, regulators, policymakers, and industry participants that sufficiently advanced AI systems could, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity,” the filing said.
Some of those potential risks have manifested themselves recently, as the firms have faced intense scrutiny over a swath of incidents in which their models and agents have hacked into government and private websites and services. 
The companies, including Anthropic, have said they’re taking steps to bolster security. But those incidents also came as Anthropic co-founder Dario Amodei wrote in September that companies should “pace the frontier,” a way of saying that AI models were advancing faster than humans could grasp their capabilities. 
Some within the industry conditionally agreed with Amodei, but Washington sharply rebuked the idea. He and President Donald Trump met privately and again at an industry meeting that took place at the White House. 
Trump later said he wanted no holds at all placed on the progress the industry is making. Trump and the leaders of Google, Anthropic, Meta, OpenAI, Nvidia and SpaceX signed what Trump said was a “morally binding” agreement to police AI safety.
“Look, I say, if you’re going to seek regulation, they can put you out of business,” Trump told Time magazine earlier this year. “But my attitude is, we’re winning against the world by a lot, and I want to keep winning.”
Steve Kopack is a senior reporter at NBC News covering business and the economy.

Topic:Stock Market
Investors say Anthropic could be worth a record-breaking $US1.5 trillion ($2.1 trillion) when it is listed on the Nasdaq. (Reuters: Dado Ruvic/Illustration)
Anthropic, the AI giant behind Claude, could be floated on the Nasdaq as soon as next month, despite making a $US80 billion ($114 billion) loss in the first half of 2026.
The company has flagged the potential harm its AI models could pose to humankind.
The company could be valued at up to $US1.5 trillion, investors say.
Anthropic has disclosed steep losses and red-hot revenue growth in its US initial public offering filing, as the Claude-parent laid out its ambition to build AI systems capable of reshaping the global economy despite the high costs of developing the technology.
The prospectus highlights the AI company's rapid growth over the past year, fuelled by Big Tech's breakneck spending that has driven a rally in AI stocks while raising concerns about lofty valuations and rising debt. 
The company also flagged growing risks from AI technology, underscoring concerns over its potential to cause harm which had led chief executive Dario Amodei to call for a slowdown in development of advanced models.
Anthropic IPO live updates: For the latest reaction and analysis, plus market moves from the ASX and Wall Street, read our blog
Anthropic's net loss in the first half of 2026 widened sharply to $US80.7 billion ($114 billion), largely due to a rise in the fair value of bonds convertible into company's shares. Revenue in this period stood at about $US16.3 billion.
The company's revenue grew a staggering 1,368 per cent in the three months ending June 30 from a year ago, but warned in the prospectus that revenue growth rate could decline over time and fluctuate from time to time.
Anthropic's chief executive Dario Amodei is among those warning about the need to limit the risks of artificial intelligence. (AP: Markus Schreiber/File)
The IPO, which is likely to be as early as later this month, is expected to establish Anthropic, the AI lab behind the viral Claude models, as a benchmark for how Wall Street values leading AI companies, including rival OpenAI.
The IPO push comes despite a once-busy fall listing calendar thinning out as choppy markets prompt companies to delay their offerings. 
Analysts said that would give Anthropic access to a deep pool of capital to fund its costly AI ambitions.
Growing investor uneasy with the debt-fuelled AI investment boom has led to a global sell-off in AI and tech stocks as they start to doubt if the trillions spent on data centres, chips and computing capacity will generate fast returns to support lofty valuations. 
Increasing public opposition to data centres could limit investor enthusiasm for AI companies. (Reuters: Hollie Adams)
Some current and prospective investors are valuing Anthropic at about $USUS1.5 trillion.
Anthropic said more than half of Fortune 500 companies and over 30 per cent of Global 2000 firms were customers generating at least $US100,000 each in revenue for the company over the 12 months ended September 30.
The federal government may have a big job ahead convincing voters that there are benefits to AI.
The IPO comes as Anthropic's own research raises concerns about advanced AI systems, which in controlled tests have shown behaviours including code sabotage, fraud assistance and information manipulation.
The findings have fuelled concerns about how companies can keep powerful AI systems under control as they race to deploy them commercially.
The ‌prospectus also highlighted risks associated with its AI models, which it said could exhibit ""self-preserving behaviours"", including behaviour ""resembling blackmail"".
""Our increasing use of AI systems to accelerate our own research and development may cause the capabilities of our models to advance more rapidly than our ability to develop, validate, and implement corresponding safety, alignment, and security measures,"" it said.
Anthropic will trade on the Nasdaq after the IPO closes under the ticker symbol ""ANT"".
The filing did not reveal the terms of its offering, including the number of shares it plans to sell and the proposed price range.

Topic:Education
Anthropic, founded in 2021 by a team of researchers, was valued at $US965 billion in May in a funding round that drew some of the biggest names in Silicon Valley and Wall Street.
""We believe this abundance of AI will rapidly transform every sector of the global economy in ways greater than the impact of industrialisation, electricity, semiconductors and the internet,"" the prospectus said.
The company said it expects compute and infrastructure expenses to increase in absolute dollars and remain its largest operating expense for the foreseeable future.
Anthropic remains locked in a fierce battle with OpenAI to lead the race to build the most advanced AI systems. 
The ChatGPT maker confidentially filed for an IPO in June and is expected to list by early 2027.
Anthropic's IPO follows that of Elon Musk's SpaceX, which rewrote the IPO record books in June, when it priced a $US75 billion offering that valued the company at $US1.75 trillion.

38,2026-10-26,"SECZ’s onchain market cap fell to $208.6 million as Circle-linked tokens reached a combined $383.6 million across several issuers in the $3.3 billion tokenized equities market.
The value of tokenized shares of Securitize (SECZ) have lost more than half their value in less than a month as the gap with market leader Circle (CRCL) widens.
The market capitalization of SECZ tokens fell from a high of $430.4 million on Sept. 28 to $208.6 million by Sunday, according to Token Terminal.  The NYSE-traded shares  closed Friday down 2.1% at $7.80 apiece, with a market cap of $1.27 billion.
Tokens tracking Circle’s shares have gained about 20% in market value since Sept. 28 to reach $383.6 million, reclaiming the top spot in the $3.3 billion tokenized stock market.
Tokenized stock market capitalization by underlying stock, excluding ETFs. Source: Token Terminal
The decline in tokenized SECZ coincided with a sell-off in Securitize’s underlying shares on the NYSE, which erased gains that followed the Oct. 8 launch of its stock trading platform on Solana. Citi downgraded SECZ from Buy to Neutral on Oct. 13 amid concerns about tokenization revenue.
Securitize helps asset managers including BlackRock issue securities on blockchains. The company brought its own common shares onchain alongside its July 2 NYSE listing, becoming the largest tokenized equity at the time.
SECZ remains the largest individual tokenized stock as Circle’s total is spread across products from several issuers. Binance’s CRCLb on BNB Chain leads those offerings at $154.1 million.

By Nassim Khadem
Virgin Australia has introduced hot chips to its economy menu in what it says is an Australian airline first.
Overseas airlines including United, Ryanair and Brussels Air already offer hot chips on some flights.
Virgin Australia chief customer officer Andrew Cleary said the airline had worked to overcome the challenges of serving hot chips mid-flight and had developed a cooking process to keep the chips crispy at 35,000 feet.
A serve of hot chips onboard, with a side of aioli, will cost passengers $12 or 2,400 Velocity points, and will be restricted to flights of 1.5 hours or more.

By Nassim Khadem
Anthropic is the next cab off the rank for mega-tech initial public offerings (IPOs), and is slated to surpass SpaceX as the biggest capital markets fundraising in history.
An IPO involves a company asking big financial institutions and ""mum and dad"" investors for cash via the stock exchange.
Elon Musk's SpaceX played this game in June, raising $US85.7 billion, with a listing price of $US135.
It hit the boards of the Nasdaq and subsequently shot up to $US161, with unprecedented investor interest pushing the company to a multi-trillion-dollar valuation.

Key Event
By Nassim Khadem
Superannuation complaints rose sharply in 2025-26, with the financial ombudsman receiving 8,755 complaints, up 42% on the previous financial year.
The Australian Financial Complaints Authority (AFCA) says across all complaints areas, it received the highest number in its history, with almost 120,000 complaints lodged by consumers and small businesses.
Scams and unauthorised transactions were the top two issues in banking and finance, with 6,580 scam complaints and 6,038 unauthorised transactions complaints.
For general insurance, 36,022 complaints were lodged, marking a fifth year of growth for the industry. The most complained about product was motor vehicle insurance with 14,430 complaints lodged.
Complaints about super funds refusing to pay claims rose by 80%.
""As more Australians move into and through retirement, their relationship with their superannuation fund becomes increasingly important,"" AFCA chief ombudsman and CEO David Locke said.
""For many people, these funds represent a lifetime of work and financial security in retirement. Funds must take care to respond to members' concerns and focus on achieving good outcomes wherever they can.""
He said large-scale collapses were the main driver of investments and advice complaints, with complaints from investors who lost retirement savings as a result of the Shield and First Guardian Master fund collapses, which I investigated in the article provided below, making up the bulk.
Financial difficulty complaints rose 17% to 5,566, with financial firms failing to respond to requests for assistance remaining as the top issue.
Key Event
By Nassim Khadem
The May budget announced that the government would be removing the 50% CGT discount for indexation and that the change would include startups.
But after heavy campaigning from the startup and tech sector, Treasurer Jim Chalmers said the tax break would be retained for them.
He said the Innovative Business Capital Gains Tax concession (IBCC) introduced to parliament today ""supports early investment in innovative businesses by providing eligible founders, employees and investors with access to a 50% capital gains tax discount on equity investments"".
""This means early investors in innovative startups that begin with a low or zero cost base still receive a significant discount on a future capital gain,"" he said.
""This will support investment in innovative Australian startups, and the continued growth of Australia's startup and venture capital system.""
The legislation also includes changes to the Research and Development Tax Incentive by increasing core R&D offset rates by up to 50%.
Chalmers noted it ""extends access to the refundable offset for young, growing firms for longer by increasing the turnover threshold from $20 million to $50 million, reduces the R&D intensity threshold so that firms that invest more substantially in core R&D can benefit from higher offset rates, and increases the maximum expenditure threshold"".
Earlier in the year, I wrote about how the tech sector onslaught against proposed changes to capital gains tax could see startups carved out. Read more of their views here:

By Nassim Khadem
Woodside Energy formally agreed to sell its participating interest in the Sunrise Joint Venture to Timor-Leste's state-owned energy company, TIMOR GAP, for up to $US200 million.
TIMOR GAP currently holds a 56.6% interest in the Sunrise Joint Venture.
Following completion, expected in 2027, Woodside will no longer hold a participating interest in the Sunrise Joint Venture.
On September 2 it announced it had entered into an agreement with Osaka Gas to acquire Osaka Gas' 10% interest in the Sunrise Joint Venture, subject to satisfaction of relevant conditions precedent.
""Greater Sunrise has been an important part of Woodside's portfolio for many years, and we recognise the project has the potential to deliver long-term economic and energy security benefits for Timor-Leste and the region,"" Woodside CEO Liz Westcott said.
""While Sunrise remains a high-quality resource, the transaction further simplifies Woodside's portfolio and allows capital and management attention to be focused on opportunities that present the greatest shareholder value.""
Completion of the transaction is subject to conditions, including relevant government, regulatory and joint venture approvals.

By Nassim Khadem
Canadian public pension fund OMERS has taken a minority stake in La Trobe Financial, one of Australia's largest non-bank lenders, from Brookfield.
The Ontario Municipal Employees Retirement System (OMERS) is one of Canada's largest defined benefit pension plans.
La Trobe did not disclose the price or the size of the stake but  Brookfield said it will remain majority shareholder and OMERS will sit alongside Brookfield and Axight as an institutional investor.
Earlier in the year, Brookfield also sold a 30% stake in the non-bank lender to Abu Dhabi-based private equity firm Axight for $3 billion.
That was almost double the amount Brookfield paid to acquire it in May 2022.
The latest transaction needs customary regulatory approvals and is expected to close by January next year.

Key Event
By Nassim Khadem
The corporate regulator has released a review on business liquidations that gives us an insight into how often directors of one company that goes under have been there before.
ASIC's review says there were 26,664 grouped appointments — 7,023 court liquidations and 19,641 creditors' voluntary liquidation (CVLs) — for the period from July 1, 2021, to June 30, 2025.
It found that roughly one in five involved a company with at least one director who had previously been a director of another company when it entered liquidation.
Many companies in the review were small in financial and operational scale, with limited assets available by the time they entered liquidation.
The median age of a company entering liquidation was seven years, with 60.9% of appointments involving companies aged six years or more.
About half of all liquidations realised no more than $10,000 in assets, and about one-quarter realised no assets.
About 69% reported liabilities of less than $1 million and 89.6% had five or fewer employee creditors. Liabilities reported by companies entering liquidation remained relatively stable across the review period, with median liabilities ranging between $410,000 and $530,000.
Limited assets also affected the work and remuneration of liquidators. In 88.1% of finalised liquidations, no dividend was paid to ordinary unsecured creditors and either no remuneration was approved or remuneration paid to the liquidator was less than the amount approved. This indicates that unrecovered remuneration was likely common.
""Directors should closely monitor their company's financial position and seek advice early when financial difficulties arise,"" ASIC Commissioner Kate O'Rourke said.
""Taking action early gives a company more options to address financial difficulties. The longer action is delayed, the fewer options may remain to restructure the business, preserve value or limit further losses to creditors.""

By Nassim Khadem
The US Federal Reserve will meet this week to decide whether to raise interest rates for the second time this year in a bid to tackle inflation.
Most economists and Wall Street analysts expect the Fed to leave its benchmark rate unchanged at 3.75% to 4% when officials release the latest policy statement on Wednesday US time.
Wolfgang Bauer, fund manager at Royal London Asset Management, says ""this should be viewed as a tactical pause rather than the end of the renewed tightening cycle"".
""Growth and employment remain resilient in the US, inflation is still well above target, and Fed Chair [Kevin] Warsh recently described financial conditions as difficult to characterise as restrictive,"" he said.
""As further inflation data will only be published after the meeting, the Fed has good reason to wait without thereby adopting a softer monetary policy stance.""
He adds the key question will be whether ""Warsh maintains his assessment of financial conditions and clearly keeps a December rate increase open as an option"".
""In our view, the most likely scenario is therefore a rate pause with a restrictive undertone: no change this week, but equally no signal that interest rates have already reached their peak,"" he said.

By Nassim Khadem
Many Australian investors will be keen to participate in Anthropic's IPO, either directly if they are wholesale investors with access, or indirectly through ETFs.
Should they? Analysts are divided.
Some like Wall Street tech analyst Dan Ives are upbeat (although he usually is on the tech sector). He says: ""You're going to see probably a strong appetite for this offering.""
In his view, despite making a loss, the company is ""going gangbusters"".
""For investors, they're going to basically need to look past this year,"" he told The Business.
""Looking out to the next two, three years, what does this company look like when they have revenue?
""And I think it's very similar to SpaceX in terms of that scenario.""
He says while there's risk, there's also opportunity.
""I think that's something investors are going to have to weigh because we're all going into it, whether it's here in the US, Australia, wherever we are, we're all going into this fourth industrial revolution together.""
Cameron Gleeson, Head of Investment Strategy at Betashares, is less positive.
He says ""despite Anthropic's size and scale, investors should be mindful of letting their portfolios become too concentrated in the AI trade"".
""We've seen Australian ETF investors ease back on US equities in recent months, as sentiment has cooled somewhat [with] the AI capex build-out,"" he said.
""While Anthropic's IPO would be historic, investors should stick to their broader investment plans and avoid seeing these stocks as a ticket to quick riches.""
Renaissance Capital senior strategist Matt Kennedy says investors should be looking at the revenue per compute spend as the company grows.
Currently, the company says it makes more than $2 in revenue for every dollar it spends on computing infrastructure.
""We know that it can grow, but can it can actually turn that money into earnings? And the biggest cost right now is the compute cost, the expense side there.
""In addition to sales and marketing, they are spending heavily with their channel partners like Google to grow fast.
""But I think investors will want to take a look at that compute spend to really see if they can get the kind of margins that you get from a software company. Is this a company that can get to 60%, 70%-plus gross margins after the compute spend?""
-with Reuters 
By Katie Silver
Australia is getting better at protecting itself and responding to increasing cyber attacks, according to the Australian Signals Directorate.
The agency says there were more than 37,500 hotline calls and 82,000 cybercrime reports in 2025/26, as AI models speed up incidence and security threat. Cybercrime is costing individuals in the order of $33,000.
Identity fraud remains the top reported cybercrime.
The cybersecurity agency has called for further testing of frontier AI models in collaboration with operators and Five Eyes countries as well as urging for outdated technology across all sectors to be updated.
It also recommends ""digital hygiene"", which includes changing passwords, updating software and disconnecting tech from the internet if it doesn't need connection to reduce risk in the emerging AI landscape.

By Katie Silver
The Tax Ombudsman fielded 2,376 complaints against the ATO in the six months to September, up 74% from the same period in 2025 and 180% above the same period two years ago.
Debt collection and payment-related matters each accounted for 19% of complaint investigations from April to September, the agency said.
""Our latest complaint data shows more people are struggling due to the ATO's debt collection activity or interest charges or simply need to resolve delays to payments they are due,"" ombudsman Ruth Owen said.
In response, an ATO spokesperson said the tax office had only received 1,358 complaints over the same period, as the ombudsman's figures included cases that were resolved without any ATO involvement or that related to the Tax Practitioners Board or state tax issues. The ombudsman only investigated 501 complaints out of 2,376 it received and found in most cases the ATO had acted appropriately.
""At a time when many Australians are facing financial distress, we are seeing increased complaints about how the ATO is collecting tax debt owed and how interest is applied to the debt, often making the amount due impossible to pay back,"" Owen said.
In 63% of debt collection-related complaints, the agency found the ATO acted reasonably, down 9% from the same period last year.
Complaints about penalties and interest made up 18% of grievances, up from 13% a year ago.
Amid rising inflation and interest rates, tax liabilities were adding to mounting debts owed by taxpayers and small businesses, the ombudsman said.
The ATO was owed $54.2 billion in outstanding tax debt in 2024/25, according to the Australian National Audit Office.
Debts owed by small businesses amounted to $35.9 billion, an increase of $19.4 billion from 2018/29.

By Daniel Ziffer
Probably our most successful regulator is one that only pops up in a very specific field: financial crimes.
The Australian Transaction Reports and Analysis Centre (AUSTRAC) worked out long ago how to get the attention of boards, directors and executives.
Absolutely massive fines.
Which is why embattled casino operator and anti-money laundering law breacher Star Entertainment is sweating today.
The company finds out tomorrow the size of the penalty.
Its annual general meeting (AGM) will be held from 10am (AEST)  today and I'll follow any developments.
In the annual report, the company noted the civil penalty proceedings in the Federal Court would be pricey.
""AUSTRAC submitted that a penalty of $400 million would be appropriate while The Star submitted that a penalty of greater than $100 million, payable in the 12 months following the hearing, would be challenging based on available liquidity options, including accessing equity markets. The Star is awaiting the court’s judgment.""
Disclosure: I own shares in Star Entertainment, which I bought so I could ask questions at the AGM. The share price has had a precipitous fall in the past few years of relentless scandal. Five years ago a share was worth $3. Currently one is worth 0.12c. As if you needed a reminder that this blog does not constitute financial advice.

By Katie Silver
Further to our story on the The Business last night (see the video below), NEXTDC is the story I can't get enough of at the moment. Everyone hungrily using AI, huge demand for data centres (DC), yet the big question — who wants to live near them?!  And what happens when vocal residents object to the potential noise pollution, air pollution, eyesore pollution. 
We've seen it in Lane Cove in Sydney and, more recently, NEXTDC's shares have plummeted after it said opposition to a key project in Melbourne would cause it huge delays and torpedo its profit forecasts.
Victoria's electricity distribution network provider delayed the upgrade due to community and stakeholder opposition.
I like some of these takes from Commonwealth Bank's John Oh: 
""It points to the social licence being one of the key requirements for the data centre build‑out.""
To upgrade the powerlines, the network provider Jemena needs to install taller power poles in residential areas ""which may reduce visual appeal in residential areas"" likely underpinning the opposition, Oh says.
""It's worth noting, although underground cabling is an alternative option, it is a costlier (i.e. 2–6 x higher in Ireland) and timelier option.  With electricity network operators making the final decision on upgrades made in their network, it points to the strong alignment required between DC operators and electricity network operators.""
The Australian standards for AI will be geared towards ensuring DC rollout doesn't adversely affect electricity prices, water use, and consumers and communities. It's this stakeholder management that's going to potentially be the gamechanger when it comes to whether these centres are successful or not.
That and, Oh says, the need to ensure it doesn't negatively affect the power market:
""Australian Energy Market Operator (AEMO) expects data centre demand to grow from 2.5% of underlying National Electricity Market (NEM) demand (i.e. total electricity demand) in 2025‑26 to 7.6% of underlying NEM demand in 2030‑31.""

By Katie Silver
The euro fell against the dollar after a selloff in French government bonds last week.  It drove the country's debt risk premium to its highest level since the 2011 sovereign debt crisis, reigniting concerns about renewed financial stress across the euro zone.
While French government bond prices rose on Monday, Kit Juckes, chief global FX strategist at Societe Generale, says the fundamental issue with the euro hasn't changed:
""Euro is still vulnerable at this point because the growth outlook isn't good and energy prices are still elevated.""
The dollar has been supported by resilient US economic indicators, notwithstanding headwinds including rising inflation. The US currency came under selling pressure during a tech-led market selloff last week.
Five major central banks will announce policy decisions later in the week. The US Federal Reserve, the European Central Bank and the Bank of Japan are all expected to hold rates steady despite inflation pressures.
New Zealand's and Canada's central banks are expected to raise rates this week.
The real currency to watch has been the Brazilian real, which fell sharply against the US dollar after Lula won a historic fourth term.

By Katie Silver
Anthropic has filed to list on the Nasdaq under the ticker ANT.
Here are some of the key metrics or findings from the filing, from Dan Ives at Yorkville Ives:
In September 2025, 950 customers spent at least $100,000 a year on them. This grew to 11,900 a year later, including the US's 10 largest companies and more than half of the top 500.
The company has Net Revenue Retention of approximately 900%,  which means that for every $1 of revenue on average from a customer, Anthropic is generating about $9 from them a year later.  Almost 2,000 customers spend more than $1 million, and roughly 250 more than $10 million.
""Concentration is easing: no single customer exceeded 10% of revenue for the nine months ended September 30, 2026, and roughly 79% of Q3 revenue came from outside the top five, up from roughly 64% in Q1, even as each top-five account hit a record. Revenue is overwhelmingly consumption-based ($13.1 billion in 1H26) with subscriptions at $3.2 billion, up from $180 million, and remaining performance obligations were $10.8 billion,"" Ives says.
Let's take a look at how revenue has changed in recent years:
The first half of last year it made $1.2b, and increased it to $16.3b for the first half of this year.
With this said, Ives says ""there are some concerning (not unexpected) risk factors"" in the filing.
""Anthropic has asked its cloud partners for more compute than they were willing to provide, and those same partners build competing models and could curtail, reprice, or terminate access, all while the take-or-pay commitments above are owed regardless of actual usage and have been partly debt-funded. The filing also discloses past failed or underperforming training runs, warns that scaling laws may plateau, and flags that safety-driven release delays such as holding back Mythos through Project Glasswing could slow capability gains versus competitors and may become more frequent and costly as models advance."" 
The filing also flagged some potential liability of the AI agents including misuse by bad actors, models acting in unintended or autonomous ways that no user directed, and contractual protections and insurance that may prove insufficient. 
Ives finishes his note as the quintessential AI bull he is:
""Overall, the numbers are showing massive, eye-popping growth from a broadening group of customers as ANT aggressively goes after the enterprise market in this AI Revolution. The near-term losses are massive but investors on the IPO will be laser-focused on the growth trajectory into 2027 and 2028 as the next phase of this AI Revolution takes hold.""

With Reuters
By Daniel Ziffer
Yesterday we brought you news that retailer Myer has told employees that when it moves to a new office just around the corner from the ABC in Melbourne, everyone will be in it.
That is, work-from-home (WFH) is essentially dead, with exceptions on a person-by-person basis for people with illness, caring responsibilities or mega-commutes.
It's another flashpoint in a long-running issue that was meant to be settled.
A key case about the Clerks Award, the broad agreement about the rights and conditions of people working in administration, was meant to provide a definitive answer about this.
It didn't.
Essentially employees can ask for WFH. Employers must answer. 
But it doesn't clear up if there is a clear entitlement to the right.
Here's a note from Ryan Murphy of McInnes Wilson Lawyers about the end of the case:
""Will this give all employees a standalone 'right' to work from home? No. Will it actually change a whole lot? Potentially not.""
Here's the key bit:
""So, does the new clause in the Clerks Award create a right to work from home for all? No, we don't think so. 
""By saying that an 'employee may request, and an employer may agree to', the proposed new clause is softer than the flexible work right in the Act. Indeed, the proposed Award clause anticipates that an employer may not agree to an employee's work from home request.
""What the clause may do, however, is broaden the scope of those who have a right to request to work from home. That presents the possibility that employees will seek to rely on this 'right' in disputes if an employee feels they have been treated less favourably because they have made the request.""
The analysis from software firm Employment Hero spells it out pretty clearly:
""Can a request be refused? Yes. Because clause 5A operates on a consensual basis, an employer can decline a request. The clause does not set a response time, a requirement to give reasons, or rules around having to have any grounds for refusal.""
So no 'right' but the reality that most employers and employees will negotiate.
As ever, the best bosses are not the ones that say yes to everything but those who are open to anything.
By Katie Silver
Prices current at around 7:50am AEDT

Whether you're a first-time tablet shopper or you're starting to notice the signs that you need to upgrade your tablet, there's a lot to consider when browsing today's expansive tech market. The easy answer might be to buy the latest high-end flagship tablet from a brand like Apple or Samsung. But if you just want an affordable tablet you use for work and home, you're likely to be overwhelmed with options. And some of them are simply not worth the money.
Experts are likely to give negative reviews to tablets that don't live up to the use cases they're designed for. In today's market, even a lower-end tablet is expected to run any productivity app or simple games at a smooth refresh rate. Battery life is a huge consideration, too, and the modern expectation is that a tablet should last for a minimum of 10 hours with a fast recharge rate. We came across two particular tablets that were released in 2026, with expert feedback indicating that they might not meet current standards: the Amazon Fire HD 10 and the second-generation Lenovo Tab Plus.
The 2026 iteration of the Amazon Fire HD 10 is an updated version of the model that was released in 2023. The recent refresh increased the RAM of this budget tablet to 4GB, though it is otherwise unchanged. The reviewers at PC Mag praised the Fire HD 10 for being a surprisingly decent tablet for under $150, but with a huge caveat: it runs on Amazon's restrictive version of the Android operating system and is only compatible with Amazon's very limited app store. The Fire HD 10 frequently goes on sale for as little as $73 according to Amazon's price history tool, but experts have expressed doubts as to whether it's worth it even at that price.
It's much more practical to spend your money on a tablet that isn't bound by arbitrary limitations. Fortunately, Amazon actually gave users a reason to buy its Alexa tablets with a new lineup that has full access to the Google Play Store. In particular, the Amazon Alexa Tablet 8 is in a similar price bracket to the Fire HD 10, with its 64GB model at $229.99. It's arguably a much better buy, especially when you factor in the Alexa Tablet's superior rear-facing camera quality and built-in Alexa+ compatibility.
The Lenovo Tab Plus Gen 2 is a tablet with an outstanding built-in JBL speaker. In fact, Tom's Guide calls it a ""giant speaker with a tablet attached,"" which could be interpreted as a comment on how the tablet itself is overshadowed by its gimmick. It's powered by a MediaTek Dimensity CPU that isn't suitable for powerful work or gaming. And even if you wanted to use the Tab Plus Gen 2 for everyday tasks, its jutting speaker makes it heavy and awkward to hold. It's slow to charge, too, with one test showing that it took 30 minutes just to go from zero to 20%.
Perhaps the biggest caveat to the Tab Plus Gen 2 is its short software lifespan. Lenovo has only guaranteed support through two major Android versions and security patches through 2030, which the experts at Notebookcheck aptly describe as ""problematic."" At its original list price of $399.99, the Tab Plus Gen 2 is a difficult purchase to justify for most users. If you're looking for a great tablet-and-speaker combo, your money may go farther by choosing the more affordable Lenovo Idea Tab Plus and pairing it with one of the best Bluetooth speakers for outdoor use."
41,2026-10-26,"Alongside Anthropic raising as much as $100 billion, Meta on Monday announced a $95 billion share sale to fund its own AI ambitions.
SpaceX SPCX$146.89·Market Closed CEO Elon Musk caused a stir over the weekend, predicting the imminent collapse of much of the so-called neocloud sector.
“200+ neoclouds are going to see guppy-level death and consolidation,” said Musk. “Some will make it through, but not many.”
These companies rent out computing power for AI workloads. They use contracts with AI labs as collateral to borrow money for massive numbers of GPUs and data center buildouts, putting them in highly leveraged positions — meaning even a modest stumble or higher interest rates can put their equity at risk.
The formerly red-hot stocks of the neocloud sector have already seen severe price declines and questions about solvency. 
SpaceX — with its own data centers and sales of computing power — is a competitor with the neoclouds, but is it possible Musk rang the bell for the bottom?
Sharply lower in pre-market action on the Musk remarks, the neoclouds have reversed course and risen on Monday.
Among the gainers: CoreWeave (CRWV) +3.5%, IREN IREN$25.41·Market Closed +4%, Hut 8 HUT$62.93·Market Closed +4.2%, TeraWulf WULF$11.97·Market Closed +8.1%.
Polymarket now offers gold and silver “Up or Down” markets across its US and international platform, marking the prediction market’s first short-duration markets outside crypto.
The new contracts let users predict whether gold or silver prices will finish higher or lower than their opening prices over 15-minute, hourly, or daily trading windows on Polymarket US. The international platform will offer 15-minute markets.
Markets will settle using Chainlink LINK$12.64 price data at each trading window’s close. Neither platform will offer Saturday trading. Polymarket also plans to introduce WTI and Brent crude oil markets next, expanding its commodities-focused prediction offerings.
In the latest round of cost-cutting at tech giants, Microsoft MSFT$532.61·Market Closed is offering voluntary buyouts to about 60,000 employees, reports Business Insider.

The company has recently employed a combination of layoffs and voluntary programs to trim staff, but none approaching this size.
“The Voluntary Exit Offer reflects an important part of how we're evolving our approach to change: creating greater flexibility and, where possible, more ways for employees to shape their next chapter,” wrote Chief People Officer Amy Coleman in a memo on Monday.
“Burning cash?” Not currently.
Anthropic’s prospectus shows that after years of negative results, operating cash flows in the first half of 2026 were a positive $3.929 billion. That’s against negative cash flow of $1.513 billion in the first half of 2025.
Operating margin has flipped as well. In the second quarter, the company reported an operating margin of 8.4%. That’s up from a negative 35.% in the first quarter. 
Operating margins throughout 2025 were in the triple-digit negative percentages.
Of the much-talked-about $80 billion first-half net loss, it appears that about $72 billion was related to accounting charges on convertible debt as the company’s valuation rose.
Galaxy Digital believes bitcoin's bear market ended in June and that the crypto asset is now in the early stages of what could become its next bull market.
In a research report released Monday, Alex Thorn, Galaxy's head of research, argued that much of the selling that drove bitcoin down from its October 2025 peak has run its course. Meanwhile, investors who held through the downturn have shown little sign of leaving.
Bitcoin traded around $79,800 on Thursday, roughly 35% below its record high. Thorn believes it can return to at least $125,000, partly because of who's buying and selling.
Asset managers have cut their net long positions in CME bitcoin futures by 57% since last year's peak, while spot bitcoin ETFs have added 87,000 BTC since the June low. That suggests speculative traders have pulled back even as other investors continue to accumulate, said Thorn.
Bitcoin's price charts tell a similar story. The cryptocurrency has climbed back above its 50-week moving average after spending 44 weeks below it, a pattern that has marked the end of past bear markets. Thorn also argued that mounting U.S. debt and the risk of further currency debasement make bitcoin's fixed supply more appealing.
There are risks. A sustained drop below $76,540 would call the recovery into question, according to Thorn. Nevertheless, his broader point is that bitcoin no longer needs a surge of speculative buying to find its footing as much of that money has already left, and the investors who remain appear willing to wait.
Between Meta’s $95 billion share sale and Anthropic’s possible $100 billion IPO — both announced this morning — that’s about another $200 billion in capital being siphoned up by the AI boom.

43,2026-10-26,"

      Kirk Siegler
    


                Logs from a salvage timber sale after a recent wildfire in the Willamette National Forest in Oregon.
                
                    
                    Kirk Siegler
                    
                
hide caption

EUGENE, Ore. - President Trump is widely expected to soon open up some 44 million acres of the country's last pristine national forests in a move he says will bring timber jobs back to struggling small towns and help prevent wildfires in overgrown lands.
Trump signaled the move with executive actions last year aimed at dramatically boosting timber sales on U.S. Forest Service land and reversing the agency's 25-year-old Roadless Rule, which blocked most new logging on undeveloped national forestlands.
Today with the formal repeal of the Roadless Rule rule expected early next year, the picture on the ground in timber country looks messier. Those who make a living in the forests say the problems are much bigger than access to more trees.
""I'm not saying these EOs [Executive Orders] are useless, they set the tone,"" says Sean Smith, CEO of the family owned Starfire Lumber in Cottage Grove, Ore.
Smith, standing in a bustling lumber yard with the sawmill cranking behind four story high stacks of freshly cut timber, says the industry has more pressing challenges now, starting with the record high price of diesel fuel. Economists largely blame that on Trump's war with Iran. And then there's inflation, which is driving up the costs of just about everything.
""Steel, you know, we have to buy sawblades, we have to buy ball bearings,"" Smith says. ""We have to buy a million different parts to keep this mouse trap going.""
So repealing the Roadless Rule - which has largely banned new road building and logging projects on some 44 million acres of pristine national forests since 2001 - is lower on the priority list.

                Sean Smith, owner of Starfire Lumber in Cottage Grove, Ore. says opening up local roadless forests to forest health projects could create a more reliable local supply for mills.
                
                    
                    Kirk Siegler
                    
                
hide caption

Smith, a burly man with a dry sense of humor and a neatly trimmed salt and pepper beard, does support the reversal saying it will eventually give industry a more reliable local timber supply. He says a lot of the nearby Willamette National Forest, roaded or not, is dangerously overgrown. That built-up fuel makes these forests more prone to catastrophic wildfires.
""This is not a 'gee I sure want more cheap timber volume,' you know, this is not me promoting an agenda,"" Smith says. ""This is a guy who lives here and is tired of seeing these beautiful places burn up.""
Trump says a boom is coming but the industry is a fraction of what it was
The administration alongside the timber industry and some local officials are pushing for the repeal, saying it will give the Forest Service more flexibility to carry out wildfire prevention, including tree thinning, on land that's largely functioned as wilderness since 2001.
But under recent Republican and Democratic administrations, the Forest Service has struggled to get those projects going on forests that already have roads. Lawsuits and red tape are an ongoing challenge, as is finding enough workers.
""Right now, there's not a ton of people that want to wake up at 2 a.m. and drive a log truck for the same price that you could go work at McDonald's for,"" says Jeff Brink, a second generation logger near Eugene, Ore.
Brink says the industry is a fraction of what it was when his dad and uncles started Brink Land Development in 1981. The company is getting by ok, for now he says, due to a steady stream of forest restoration projects on private land as well as some recent salvage logging timber sales from nearby wildfire burn scars.
But for many smaller, less established loggers already struggling, Brink says, labor shortages and high diesel prices could be the tipping point.
""For an industry already operating on low margins, this is a tough one,"" Brink says.
Due to tightening environmental laws and other market forces including cheaper Canadian lumber imports, timber sales on U.S. federal land dropped by close to 90% since the 1990s. Brink says today the infrastructure just isn't there for the forest work that needs to be done, let alone a promised timber boom.
""Rural Oregon is struggling and all the mills that aren't along the I-5 corridor or near urban centers that can source wood are closing,"" he says.
Since the 1990s, an estimated 400 mills across the broader Northwest have closed, amounting to the loss of about 35,000 jobs. Seven mills have closed in Oregon since 2024 alone.
Industry experts project that overturning the Roadless Rule could lead to a five to ten percent boost in federal timber sales. That's hardly enough to boost much enthusiasm on the ground.
""We're in an industry that for decades has been in a state of contraction,"" Brink says.
Several national environmental groups have also already signaled they plan to sue once the administration formally repeals the rule, which is expected to happen by early next year.
Some fear a revival of the bitter Timber Wars
Timothy Ingalsbee turns his hybrid station wagon onto Forest Service Road 2408, near the scene of an infamous year-long tree sit and blockade that he helped lead against a Forest Service old growth timber sale in the nineties.
""The industry [back then] was headed for a crash, they had exhausted the supply,"" Ingalsbee says, as he stops the car and gets out at a vista.
Today, his hair cropped shorter and greying, Ingalbee notes that many of those towering, old growth trees, their bark cloaked in moss, have burned in recent wildfires. Ingalsbee, a retired Forest Service firefighter, today runs a national firefighter safety and advocacy group.
""The claim that they're going to do all this new road building and logging in roadless areas on behalf of wildland firefighters, and make it more efficient for them, is just a big lie,"" Ingalsbee says. ""Not in our name you don't.""

                Retired U.S. Forest Forest Service firefighter Timothy Ingalsbee stands by a road in the Willamette National Forest, which was once the epicenter of the so-called Timber Wars.
                
                    
                    Kirk Siegler
                    
                
hide caption

Ingalsbee fears that punching even more roads into these forests will lead to more human-caused wildfires, which have been rising steadily for decades. But he doesn't think any of this is really about wildfire prevention or creating more logging jobs.
""What's driving a lot of Trump's executive orders for public lands management is not real economic drivers. I don't hear industry clambering for this,"" he says. ""It's more ideology.""
For Ingalsbee, the U.S. government is just racing to open up as much of the West as possible so industry can squeeze out maximum profits on timber, oil and coal.
Everyone is tired of policies 'whipsawing' from one president to the next
One thing that almost everyone on the ground in the Northwest agrees on is that Congress has largely failed to act on either protecting or opening up federal forest lands to more development.
When President Clinton signed the Roadless Rule by executive order at the end of his second term, Republicans controlled both the Senate and the U.S. House where appetite for passing more wilderness legislation was slim. The rule was the culmination of a sweeping inventory of land conducted by the Forest Service in which the agency had marked certain forests as prime acreage for wilderness protection.
Congress never codified it, and since then, the administrative rule has become an environmental flashpoint in a polarized country.
Back at Starfire Lumber, CEO Sean Smith is tired of federal forest and timber policy whipsawing from one presidential administration to the next.
""Sometimes the cynical side of me says, 'hey, what could be better than a do-nothing Congress at least they're not screwing anything up,"" Smith says, with a sardonic chuckle. ""But that's no way to run a country long term.""
Not to mention, he adds, it makes for a hard environment to keep a business going in.

DNEG, the visual effects house on Christopher Nolan’s “The Odyssey,” has come aboard Francesco Lucente’s fantasy adventure “Sinbad” as co-financier and producer, with Hong Kong-based Cappu Films handling sales in a wide spread of territories.

	The deal was unveiled ahead of the Tokyo International Film Festival‘s TIFFCOM market. Lucente’s fresh screen version of the classic sailor tale is due to begin pre-production in early 2027 and to deliver in March 2029. 

	











	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	














	
		
					Emmys Move to Prime Video in Six-Year, Global Deal Worth Around $15 Million a Year		
			








	Cappu first showed the project to buyers at the Busan market, and sales conversations are ongoing as casting and the wider package take shape.

	“‘Sinbad’ is a story that has captured imaginations for generations, and our ambition is to bring it to life on a scale and with a sense of wonder that audiences have never experienced before,” said Lucente, who wrote the script and will direct. “We’re creating a breathtaking world of adventure, extraordinary creatures, ancient mysteries and unforgettable characters, while keeping the heart of the story deeply human. Having DNEG’s extraordinary creative talent behind us, together with Cappu Films’ international reach and commitment to the project, gives us an incredible foundation to realize that vision. We want ‘Sinbad’ to be more than a spectacular cinematic experience – we want it to be the kind of adventure that stays with audiences long after they leave the theater.”

	The sales company’s rights cover parts of Asia, Europe, the CIS, Latin America, the Caribbean, the Middle East and Africa, along with some Pacific island markets. China, Hong Kong, Japan, South Korea, German-speaking Europe, Spain, Portugal, Turkey and Eastern Europe are all on the list.

	










	The film reunites Cappu with Lucente after “Starbright.” Janice Woo of Cappu Films said that following their work together on that film, the company was excited to team with the director on a project of this scale. “DNEG’s involvement is particularly compelling for a fantasy adventure such as ‘Sinbad.’ We introduced the project to buyers in Busan and look forward to developing those conversations as the package comes together,” Woo said.

	DNEG’s teams have won eight Oscars for visual effects, and the company’s credits include “Inception,” “Interstellar,” “Blade Runner 2049,” “Dune” and “Dune: Part Two.”

	Lucente’s fantasy feature “Starbright” counts Ted Levine, John Rhys-Davies and Diego Boneta among its cast. He also directed the drama “Badland.” Cappu is separately handling international sales on “A Grim Life,” a supernatural thriller starring Joseph Mazzello and set at Los Angeles’ Cecil Hotel.

	Cappu partners with independent filmmakers on both arthouse and commercial titles. Its catalog takes in horror, action, fantasy and arthouse fare, alongside library titles from Hong Kong’s Golden Era and TV series.
The Business of Entertainment"
46,2026-10-26," by Jason Lemkin | 5 Interesting Things, Blog Posts, Scale
Anthropic filed its S-1 this morning. It plans to list its Class A common stock on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the symbol “ANT,” and the share count and price range haven’t been set yet.
Largest IPOs in History, by Deal Size 
1. Anthropic (2026)        ~$100.0B2. SpaceX (2026)               $75.0B3. SK Hynix (2026)             $26.5B4. Saudi Aramco (2019)   $25.6B5. Alibaba (2014)                 $21.8B
*Anthropic estimateSource: Renaissance Capital
— Morning Brew ☕️ (@MorningBrew) October 26, 2026

Revenue was $41 million in 2023, $386 million in 2024 and $4,585 million in 2025. In the first half of 2026 alone it was $16,286 million, roughly fourteen times the $1,163 million from the first half of 2025.

The valuation and the $80 billion net loss will get the headlines. For founders, the metric definitions, the expense table and a few lines in the business section have more to teach. Here are five.

Anthropic reports Net Revenue Retention of approximately 900% as of September 30, 2026.
The definition matters. It’s current-quarter revenue from qualified customers divided by the same customers’ revenue in the quarter a year earlier, and it excludes Claude Pro and Max subscribers because fixed-price subscriptions don’t reflect expansion and contraction. That means the usage business grew 9x inside its existing customers in a year.
The expansion shows up at the top of the customer base. As of September 30, 2026, Anthropic had more than 11,900 customers with $100,000+ of TTM revenue, more than 1,900 with $1 million+, and approximately 250 with $10 million+.
Anthropic calls its model “pay-for-intelligence”: customers pay for usage metered in tokens, priced by the model they choose. As the models improve, customers send them more work, and spend rises with the work.
What to take from it: Anthropic priced on usage from day one, and 250 customers now pay it more than $10 million a year. Few software line items at any company are that size. That money is coming from labor and new-project budgets, and a seat-based price would have capped it at whatever the software budget allowed.
Anthropic keeps lowering what customers pay per task, and its compute ratio keeps falling.
Claude Opus 5.5 cut customer costs per task by roughly 40% versus Claude Opus 5, and the filing says lower costs per task drive more usage, which drives broader adoption.
Compute and infrastructure cost $2,334 million in the first half of 2025 and $8,945 million in the first half of 2026, against revenue of $1,163 million and $16,286 million. That’s compute at 201% of revenue a year ago and 55% today. For the quarter ended June 30, 2026, revenue grew 1,368% year over year while compute and infrastructure expenses grew 300%.
A note for anyone modeling this: Anthropic books compute and infrastructure as an operating expense rather than cost of revenue, so the prospectus doesn’t show a conventional gross margin. The compute line includes training and research as well as serving customers.
What to take from it: Anthropic handed customers a 40% lower cost per task in one model generation, and over the same twelve months compute fell from 2x revenue to about half of it.
Sales, marketing, and partnerships was $120 million in the first half of 2025 and $3,168 million in the first half of 2026. Personnel in the same half was $3,152 million. That’s 26x year over year, and it went from about 10% of revenue to about 19%. It’s the steepest relative increase in the expense table.

The business section shows where the money goes. Anthropic says much of Claude’s diffusion has been organic, and it adds go-to-market and Applied AI teams plus distribution partnerships that put Claude inside customers’ existing stacks. Applied AI teams include forward-deployed engineers who build custom solutions on the customer’s own systems. Partners include SIs like Accenture, Deloitte, KPMG and PwC, plus data platforms like Salesforce and Snowflake. Anthropic also just launched Ode with Anthropic, an AI services company built with private equity firms to bring Claude to mid-sized companies.
What to take from it: with developers already pulling Claude in on their own, Anthropic still spent $3.2 billion in six months on forward-deployed engineers, SI partnerships, marketing and a new services company for the mid-market. AI startups planning a purely product-led enterprise push should look hard at that line.

Anthropic reports TTM revenue per employee of over $6.6 million as of September 30, 2026, compared to $1.4 million a year earlier. The filing credits talent density, an efficient go-to-market, and how heavily its own teams use Claude in their work.
Personnel was $3,152 million in the first half of 2026, and that includes $2.2 billion of stock-based compensation. Roughly 70% of the personnel line is equity, so cash payroll is small next to first-half revenue that annualizes to more than $32 billion.
What to take from it: revenue grew about 14x in a year while revenue per employee grew about 4.7x, so Anthropic added people far more slowly than revenue. We run SaaStr the same way with 3 humans and 20+ agents in production.
The filing calls Claude Code the clearest example of what Anthropic calls Virtual Collaborators, and says that for enterprise use cases its launch was comparable in impact to the first widely adopted conversational AI assistants.
Anthropic then took the same architecture to everyone else. The filing describes developers as internal champions for new technology, and says non-technical teams are increasingly adopting Claude Code and Claude Cowork for their own workflows. As of September 30, 2026, Cowork adoption outpaced what Claude Code achieved at the equivalent point after its research preview, and in September 2026 Anthropic began merging Cowork and Claude.ai into one app.
What to take from it: developers used the agent loop in Claude Code for about a year before Anthropic handed the same engine to finance, marketing and ops teams in Cowork, and the second product ramped faster than the first.

47,2026-10-26,"Soldered RAM is easy to hate. The majority of thin laptops manufactured in recent years have their RAM soldered directly to the motherboard, meaning you have almost zero chance of upgrading its capacity or fixing your laptop if something goes wrong.
On the one hand, it's anti-consumer, but it does come with other benefits, such as higher speeds and lower latency, both of which are really useful.
But a new memory standard is changing all of that. LPCAMM2 brings the ultra-fast, low-latency RAM found on soldered devices to removable memory modules you can swap out and upgrade, finally handing some power back to consumers.
I bought a 6,000 MB/s SSD and learned how meaningless that number can be.
LPCAMM2 stands for Low-Power Compression Attached Memory Module. Instead of an angled stick clicking into a slot, it's a flat module pressed onto a grid of contacts on the motherboard and held in place with screws. That keeps the connection short and clean enough for LPDDR5X to run at full speed.
On that, it's worth noting that the module isn't the RAM. It still holds LPDDR RAM in the module; it's just that the module is removable with the RAM inserted, rather than linked directly to the motherboard.
It also has another unique feature: a single LPCAMM2 module can run in dual-channel mode on its own, saving some of the space lost to returning to removable RAM modules. Normally, you'd need two RAM modules to achieve dual-channel mode.
LPCAMM2 testing is also very positive. The first laptop to use LPCAMM2, the Lenovo ThinkPad P1 Gen 7 Intel Core Ultra 7, ran LPDDR5X at 7,467 MT/s on Micron's 7,500 MT/s modules. That puts it firmly in the same range as soldered memory, which typically achieves between 7,500 and 9600 MT/s.
There are other benefits, too. Lenovo claims its LPCAMM2 setup uses 61 percent less power and saves up to 64 percent space compared to traditional DDR5 SO-DIMM modules.
The proof is right there: same speeds, better power efficiency, and the same space savings. But those aren't the reasons we're not seeing huge LPCAMM2 uptake.
The problem is that LPCAMM2 isn't free of the insane memory price increases seen worldwide across the past 12 months. After all, it's still using LPDDR RAM, which means prices have risen in lockstep.
Framework's Laptop 13 is a prime example. As one of the first brands to consistently switch to LPCAMM2, it's also had to set prices higher to account for market pressures. At launch, its 64GB LPCAMM2 modules were $849, and in July 2026, they were pushed to $1,600. Its 32GB modules ramped up to $800.
More than that, it's actually becoming difficult to find these modules available for general purchase, for a couple of reasons. One is that Micron closed its consumer-facing memory brand, Crucial, in December 2025 — the main supplier of LPCAMM2 modules.
Other memory manufacturers like SK Hynix and Samsung don't sell directly to consumers, either, meaning the LPCAMM2 module market is doubly-hammered by AI-driven memory demands.
So while having removable memory is excellent, market forces aren't helping us take advantage.
Now, there's a good question, and it's a difficult one to answer for a couple of reasons.
If you replace your laptop every two or three years, LPCAMM2 doesn't change much. You'll pay inflated memory prices either way, and you'll probably never open the bottom panel. Soldered RAM is fine for that, and you'll have far more laptops to choose from.
The aforementioned LPCAMM2 module production issues don't really look like they're going to end soon, and soldered RAM is everywhere. And, let's face it: it works really well, despite the feeling it's anti-consumer.
The case for LPCAMM2 is longevity. Say you're buying a laptop you want to keep for five years or more. With soldered memory, the capacity you pick at checkout is the capacity you're stuck with, and soldered memory can't be repaired or expanded if something goes wrong. With LPCAMM2, both of those problems become a module swap, and that's refreshing in this day and age.
It's a hard ask, still. RAM prices are locked in throughout 2027, and it doesn't appear that 2028 will be any easier. That means finding any savings possible is important, and if that means soldered RAM over replaceable LPCAMM2, no one will begrudge you that decision.
LPCAMM2 won't save you money this year. Memory is expensive whether it's soldered or screwed down, and the laptops using it aren't exactly budget machines. I did a quick Framework Laptop 13 build with 32GB LPCAMM2 memory, and quite quickly tipped into the $2,000+ mark.
But if you're planning to buy a laptop that you'll keep for a good few years, look for LPCAMM2 on the spec sheet and buy the capacity you need today rather than what you might need in a few years' time. If prices come down, you're laughing either way.
My PC wasn’t necessarily running out of RAM, despite the 91% reading.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

48,2026-10-26,"What just happened? It seems that spending up to $145 billion in a year requires some extra fundraising, even when you're Meta. The Facebook parent plans to raise approximately $95 billion to support its AI infrastructure and superintelligence ambitions. The announcement comes just after Elon Musk warned that many smaller companies riding the same boom are heading for extinction. 
Meta launched roughly $35 billion in offerings on Monday, comprising $22.5 billion in Class A common stock and $12.5 billion in securities representing mandatory convertible preferred stock.
Reuters reports that a separate $60 billion share-sale program would follow once its new nonvoting Class C shares begin trading, expected in early 2027.
The later program would sell shares into the market over time, and the company says no agreement has been signed. Its final timing, size and terms remain undetermined.

Data courtesy of App Economy Insights
Some familiar names are interested in purchasing some of the shares. Nvidia, AMD and Broadcom have indicated interest in buying up to $5 billion worth collectively. Meta shares rose 1.6% in premarket trading following the announcement.
Investors were less enthusiastic about last week's earnings. Third-quarter revenue climbed 26% to $64.6 billion, beating expectations, but attention turned to negative free cash flow and a higher spending forecast. Meta now expects annual capital expenditure of $135 billion to $145 billion, raising the lower end by $5 billion.
It comes as little surprise to learn that the money will support Meta's AI infrastructure buildout. The company says it is building four superclusters with more than one gigawatt of capacity each and expects to nearly double its total infrastructure capacity in 2026. It plans to fund these investments through a mix of operating cash flow, debt, partnerships and equity.

Musk also said SpaceX had secured $40 billion in debt for GPUs, although Bloomberg reported that the deal had yet to close. Established businesses, he argued, could still borrow while smaller specialists struggled.
Meanwhile, Citadel founder Ken Griffin warned last week that investors are ""almost certainly"" in an AI bubble, with businesses extracting uneven value from the technology. Meta's latest fundraising suggests the warnings haven't discouraged it from spending billions more on AI-related projects.

Meta's spending plans have been raising eyebrows for months. It was reported in April that the company had lifted its annual capex forecast by $10 billion, citing higher component and data center costs. If spending reaches $145 billion this year, it will exceed what Meta spent in 2024 and 2025 combined.

Heidi Ledford reports for Nature from Seattle, Washington.

Not just for humans: gene therapy to extend longevity is under investigation for dogs, too.Credit: Juliana Yamada/Los Angeles Times/Getty
Vera Gorbunova has attended many a longevity conference over her decades of studying ageing. But never one quite like this.
Earlier this month, Gorbunova, a researcher at Rochester University in New York, joined scientists from industry and academia — and a fair few of their dogs — for the Animal Longevity Summit in Toronto, Canada. Given it was the inaugural summit, it was small. But it highlighted a growing interest in the science of extending the lifespan of pets.
On the agenda were presentations by academic researchers such as Gorbunova, who studies ageing in various animal species. Others presented projects about understanding cancer in dogs, regeneration in axolotls and the genetics of mammals that thrive in extreme environments. Alongside the university scientists were researchers at companies pursuing gene therapies for dogs, probiotic kibble for cats, and cryopreservation of pets.
Big dogs age faster than little ones — rogue ‘jumping genes’ might be to blame
Big dogs age faster than little ones — rogue ‘jumping genes’ might be to blame
“It was maybe a somewhat different crowd of people than you see at the typical ageing conferences,” Gorbunova says.
The conference reflected a market reality: as the pursuit of increasing human longevity and healthspan has grown, fuelled by hefty investment, it only stands to reason that some of that interest would spill over to pets, says Cameron Hurlburt, a senior analyst at Ani VC, a venture-capital firm in Miami, Florida. The US pet- and animal-health market is estimated to be worth US$150 billion and could grow as more people in the United States purchase pet health insurance, he says.
Most of the current efforts to promote longer pet healthspans focus on improving animal diets, including probiotic formulations, Hurlburt says. And when it comes to therapies, many companies are looking to repurpose drugs that are already approved for humans, rather than starting from scratch.
But not everyone is taking that approach. The Cat Health Company in Bucharest presented its strategy for identifying new drugs for ageing-associated diseases in cats, including a muscle-wasting condition called sarcopenia, and kidney disease. The company has two compounds in clinical trials now, with others on the way, says Alex Băciţa, co-founder and chief operating officer of the company.
How ageing changes our genes — huge epigenetic atlas gives clearest picture yet
How ageing changes our genes — huge epigenetic atlas gives clearest picture yet
Others are venturing into gene therapies. Rejuvenate Bio, a biotechnology company in San Diego, California, is working on one to treat mitral valve disease, the most common heart condition in dogs. But co-founder and chief scientific officer Noah Davidsohn hopes that this will be just the first use for the therapy, and that the same treatment will eventually be used to treat metabolic syndromes and improve longevity. Rejuvenate has worked to decrease the cost of manufacturing gene therapies, which are complex treatments, to a level that would be compatible with the pet market.
Another company, called Genflow Biosciences, is pursuing a gene therapy to enhance longevity and reduce sarcopenia in dogs. If the drug shows success, the company hopes to sell it to a larger animal-health company, and then use the proceeds to help to fund studies of the same approach in humans, says Eric Leire, founder and chief executive of the firm in Brussels.
Academic researchers also stand to gain by interacting with the latest companies to spring up, said Noah Snyder-Mackler, a comparative geroscientist at Arizona State University in Tempe, before the meeting. Government grants for studying dogs often focus on applying the results to human health, rather than canine health. That means that academic researchers might need to collaborate with industry to conduct studies that lead to treatments for dogs.
“We don’t have the resources to follow up with dog-centric interventions,” says Snyder-Mackler, a researcher on the Dog Aging Project, a large collaboration to study ageing in pooches. Gorbunova says she enjoyed the meeting. “It was a revelation to me to see how interested people are in extending the life of their pets,” she says.
