51,2026-10-26,"Unless you've been living under a rock for the past year, you know about the PC hardware crisis we're going through. It started with RAM, but soon engulfed GPUs, SSDs, and even hard drives. Storage may not be a priority for gamers, but NAS and home lab users need tons of it at affordable prices. And that combination has all but disappeared if you look at consumer and NAS drives.
If you've been looking to buy new hard drives, paying $350–$450 for an 8TB–12TB NAS drive has probably felt insane. Even 8TB consumer HDDs are going for around $300, so the situation is bad all around. In this market, the only way out is to look at pre-owned enterprise drives, specifically of the SAS variety. With a cheap adapter, you can use them with your DIY NAS, beating the market without losing out on performance.
Using old drives inside your NAS can be a good idea whether you want to store some backups or save money.
The ""RAMageddon"" headlines focus mostly on RAM, SSDs, and GPUs, but NAS owners have been lamenting hard drive pricing ever since the hardware shortage hit our shores. The core hardware for building your own NAS doesn't need to be fancy; almost any old PC can be repurposed as a home NAS. The main concern is storage—buying the right kind and doing so without blowing your budget.
For years, NAS owners have used regular HDDs, NAS hard drives, and even SSDs for their storage needs, but the last 12 months have upended the natural order. Capacious drives are out of reach of the vast majority of DIY NAS owners and enthusiasts, delaying or shelving their plans. Even the pre-owned market for consumer and NAS HDDs has caught on, closing another door to potentially affordable storage.
That said, once you're open to looking elsewhere, a few additional storage opportunities open up. And SAS drives are perhaps the most attractive of these opportunities, allowing you to install enterprise-grade hardware for a fraction of the cost. All you need is to ensure compatibility with your PC.
Some things to bear in mind when turning an old PC into a NAS.
Unlike consumer hard drives, NAS drives are optimized for 24/7 use and RAID error recovery. They aren't any faster in terms of read/write speeds, but feature a higher workload rating and vibration protection in multi-drive setups. SAS drives are designed for server use, so they naturally sport excellent reliability and massive capacity. They essentially marry the best of NAS and consumer drives, combining long-term reliability with affordable pricing.
The best thing is that once you start browsing SAS drives on eBay, you'll see they're even more affordable than regular HDDs. Since they cannot be used as-is with consumer PCs and require a Host Bus Adapter (HBA) card, they're tied to lower demand and, hence, lower prices. In terms of performance, they're rated for up to 12 Gb/s, which translates to 249 MB/s, which is faster than consumer and NAS drives.
The obvious roadblock here is the HBA card. Thankfully, you can source that on eBay, too, for as low as $30. Sellers don't always bundle the necessary cables with the card, so that will be an additional expense. You'll need a Mini-SAS cable that connects to the HBA card on one end and breaks out into SFF-8482 on the other end to connect to the SAS drive. Depending on the card, you can drive multiple 6 Gb/s or 12 Gb/s SAS drives simultaneously.
Before buying the card, ensure that it's flashed in Initiator Target (IT) mode (which most eBay listings are), so that the drives are exposed directly to the OS. Most NAS owners prefer LSI 9207-8i cards, but you can also get an LSI 9300-8i or LSI 9400-8i card for full 12 Gb/s support. These cards typically need x4 or x8 PCIe ports, so you'll need the respective spare slot on the motherboard.
Overall, your total investment for, say, three SAS drives, an HBA card, and the right cables can be as low as $500, which is quite impressive once you compare it with regular hard drive pricing.
You'll have to hunt down ex-enterprise gear for that price, though
It goes without saying that simply having the right adapter and cables won't eliminate the friction of using enterprise drives on your DIY NAS. Many SAS drives pulled from enterprise systems have a sector size of 520 or 528 bytes instead of the standard 512 bytes. For ZFS to work with your SAS drive, you'll need a reformat, which isn't a quick process on Linux at all.
Then, there's the hassle of making your HBA card work with your consumer motherboard. You may have to investigate the card's boot options, disable CSM in the BIOS, or remove the card's option ROM completely before it can allow the system to POST. Dell's LSI SAS2 cards are known not to work with some consumer motherboards unless you tape over the B5 and B6 pins on the card, isolating the card's SMBus from that of the motherboard.
Even if you manage to run the card properly, your SAS-powered NAS can be quite noisy, depending on the number of drives. These are server drives designed to operate in large racks, not compact consumer machines. Moreover, you'll need to cool both the card and the drives. Some sellers generously bundle a tiny fan with the HBA card, but most don't. You can use the same strategy with the drives by attaching a small fan directly facing them.
Lastly, power consumption can be a concern for some users, especially with 24/7 use. SAS drives need more power than SATA drives, so the power bills over the long term may be a factor in your purchase. The savings are considerable, so I'd recommend looking into used SAS drives seriously before you make the decision.
SAS is a standard that comes with its own unique pitfalls
For your NAS or home server, buying used enterprise drives is better than expensive consumer or NAS hard drives. Even users who prefer SSDs should consider U.2 enterprise SSDs on the pre-owned market. You can save money while still enjoying great performance and superior reliability for your 24x7 machine.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

53,2026-10-26,"A hot potato: As the world's second-largest company by market cap, Apple appears to be the very definition of successful. But Peter Thiel isn't overly impressed. The billionaire investor says that Cupertino has done ""nothing new"" since co-founder Steve Jobs died in 2011, though he did call it a ""pure, well-run value stock."" 
Speaking in an interview with author Walter Isaacson on The Builders podcast, Thiel said of Apple, ""In my telling, it did nothing new after Jobs.""
The PayPal co-founder added that the smartphone was a great invention, one that left Apple with little to do after its launch.
""Then it was just scaling and branding and building it out over 15 years or whatever,"" he said.
Thiel said he believes that the more eccentric, controversial CEOs like Jobs and Elon Musk are ""way better for society"" than the likes of Tim Cook, Apple's former CEO and now executive chairman of its board.
Cook was certainly known more as a safe and steady CEO compared to the unpredictable Jobs. But the soft-spoken Alabamian oversaw the introduction of some of Apple's biggest technologies during his tenure.

Apple's stock price over the last five years
There was Apple Pay in 2014, now used by up to 800 million people worldwide. The Apple Watch followed in 2015 and remains the world's most popular smartwatch. Apple Music, AirPods, Face ID, the HomePod, Apple TV+, and AirTags also arrived during Cook's time, and he was CEO in 2020 when Apple made the monumental shift away from Intel to its highly regarded in-house Apple silicon.
One area where Apple stumbled during Cook's reign has been its AI efforts. The company took years to deliver the Apple Intelligence features it had promised, and even faced a lawsuit over the situation. There were also multiple delays to the AI-upgraded Siri. Siri chief Mike Rockwell previously said this was because an earlier version, built on the old architecture, failed to deliver the intended experience.
Thiel did at least acknowledge the value of Apple stock. Apple shares started the year at $271 and are now trading at around $347, an increase of 28%.
This isn't the first time Thiel has criticized the iPhone maker. In 2017, he said the age of Apple was over. Since then, the company's stock price has increased approximately 1,066%.

54,2026-10-26,"
Your photo archive doesn’t stop growing just because its storage box has run out of room. The Minisforum N5 Air 7640 approaches that familiar problem with five drive bays and a motherboard that slides out for upgrades. This network-attached storage device, or NAS, gives owners a way to revisit what’s inside without treating the enclosure like a puzzle they’re supposed to leave unsolved.
There’s little on the outside to advertise that mechanical trick. Rounded corners soften a dark plastic shell measuring 7.8 by 8.0 by 9.9 inches. A broad front cover conceals the drive trays, while the power button, status lights, and USB ports occupy a narrow strip below. The arrangement keeps everyday connections accessible without leaving five drive handles competing for attention on your desk.
Designer: Minisforum







Open it up, however, and the distinction between storage access and computer maintenance becomes clearer. The hard drives have their own front-facing trays, while the motherboard travels forward on a separate sliding assembly beneath them. Minisforum carries this layout over from the earlier N5 Air. Its appeal is straightforward enough: memory and SSD upgrades don’t require dismantling the entire box around them.
That matters when a simple family archive starts taking on other jobs. Two DDR5 memory slots accept up to 96GB of replaceable RAM, giving owners room to expand as they add applications or virtual machines. The six-core AMD Ryzen 5 7640HS processor handles the computing, with 12 threads available for concurrent tasks. You aren’t locked into the memory configuration you chose initially.







Storage follows a similar division of labor. Five SATA bays accommodate larger drives for accumulated photos and footage, while three M.2 slots provide space for solid-state storage. Minisforum advertises up to 174TB across both groups. That’s a raw capacity ceiling, not usable space after redundancy. The included 64GB system drive also occupies one M.2 slot, so those three positions aren’t all empty.
The rear connections leave room for the setup around it to grow, too. Separate 10GbE and 5GbE network ports offer faster wired links, although your computer and network equipment need to support those speeds. There’s also OCuLink for external expansion and an internal PCIe slot. Despite its x16 size, that slot runs at PCIe 4.0 x4 bandwidth, an important distinction before choosing an upgrade.


The software brings those hardware options back to household routines. Minisforum’s MinisCloud OS offers photo organization, device backups, and separate personal and shared spaces, so family pictures needn’t share the same permissions as work documents. Docker support lets owners install additional applications. There is a caveat, though: Minisforum labels the operating system as beta software, something worth considering before entrusting it with irreplaceable files.
Minisforum lists the barebones model at $495, including that 64GB system drive but no RAM or data drives. The 16GB RAM version is listed at $679. Immediate stock and dispatch aren’t independently confirmed. Those additional hardware costs belong in the budget, but at least installing your chosen parts is something the enclosure was designed to accommodate, rather than quietly discourage.

55,2026-10-26,"I’ve been reading Joyce Appleby’s The Relentless Revolution: A History of Capitalism (2010), and it has me circling a rather delicious thought: AI panic is really a form of cognitive mercantilism. If we regard market capitalism as the first planetary intelligence, as many (including me) do, the parallels are closer than you might think.
Appleby’s book is a sweeping history of capitalism, from its unlikely emergence in early modern England to its eventual transformation of the entire world. Her central argument is that capitalism was not the inevitable expression of some timeless human instinct to trade and accumulate wealth. It was a historically contingent cultural invention, enabled by agricultural improvements, expanding trade, and changing ideas about work, wealth, and human nature. In particular, it required escaping the assumptions of an older, essentially zero-sum worldview, in which wealth meant controlling land, bullion, and the labor of largely impoverished populations. 
Capitalism emerged when people began discovering that wealth could be created rather than merely captured, and that ordinary people could be sources of demand and initiative rather than merely labor to be commanded as a factor of production.

The parallels with our current moment are striking.
As Appleby tells it, the mercantilist worldview that preceded capitalism rested on roughly four interlocking beliefs.
First, wealth was fundamentally scarce and finite. It consisted primarily of land, the agricultural produce of that land, and the precious metals it contained, accumulated through mining, trade and conquest. The goal of economic statecraft was to control more territory, maintain a favorable balance of trade, and accumulate bullion. One nation’s gain was presumed to be another’s loss. The resulting politics was one of treasure-seeking and beggar-thy-neighbor competition.
Second, ordinary people could not be trusted with much freedom. The poor were assumed to be unruly, ignorant, and prone to disorder if left to their own devices. They needed to be governed coercively by elites, supposedly for their own good and for the stability of society.
Third, economic thinking was overwhelmingly oriented toward supply and its control. Wealth came from controlling productive resources and commanding high prices for their output. Aggregate demand, especially demand from the poor, was barely recognized as a significant economic force. Ordinary people were primarily factors of production. They ate, worked, and reproduced, with no share of any surplus. The only real discretionary consumers were elites, who bought luxuries and commanded all surplus.
Fourth, there was little reason to imagine most people doing anything other than agricultural labor. If something like 80% of the population was engaged in inefficient subsistence farming, that was simply the natural order of things. What else were they good for? Remove them from the fields and you would merely have millions of useless mouths to feed.
Now replace wealth with intelligence, and mercantilism with cognitive mercantilism.
The parallels are almost embarrassingly direct.
First, intelligence is imagined as a fixed resource embodied in human brains and the knowledge products mined from them. The objective of national and corporate strategy becomes attracting the best brains, controlling their output, accumulating intellectual property, and maintaining a favorable balance of knowledge trade. The cognitive mercantilist wants to hoard intelligence and its products like bullion. The global competition for talent becomes a treasure hunt for genius brains and the valuable knowledge species they can produce, which now includes AIs in addition to copyrightable culture and patentable inventions.
Second, supposedly stupid people cannot be trusted to think for themselves. They need to be governed by cognitive elites, who know better and must regulate the behavior of the less intelligent for their own good. Democratizing intelligence is dangerous because the masses will misuse it, fall for nonsense, or unleash chaos. The assumption is that intelligence is safe only in the hands of those already certified to possess it.
Third, the supply of intelligence is treated as the central economic fact. Controlling its production, preserving its scarcity, and maintaining high prices for brains and knowledge products (including AIs) are the primary concerns. The demand side barely registers. Less intelligent people are regarded as limited cognitive laborers and passive consumers of whatever knowledge products elites choose to provide. They are not imagined as significant sources of demand for intelligence in their own right, let alone as participants in its further production.
Fourth, the cognitive mercantilist assumes that most people are not good for much intellectually. If 80% of humanity is occupied in low-grade cognitive labor, or simply muddling along without producing much intelligence at all, that is unfortunate but natural. Automating their cognitive work merely threatens to create billions of useless minds to occupy.
This last assumption is where the analogy gets especially interesting.
The agricultural revolution that helped launch capitalism began overturning the apparent necessity of having most of humanity engaged in farming. Over the following centuries, improvements in agricultural productivity reduced the fraction of the population required to produce food from a large majority to a tiny minority in advanced economies.
The result was not a world full of useless mouths. It was a world in which vastly more people could do things other than farm, while enjoying access to vastly more food.
I don’t know how to farm. I can barely keep a houseplant alive. Yet I can obtain an astonishing variety of foods, in quantities that would have seemed fantastical to an early modern peasant. My inability to produce food is economically almost irrelevant. What matters is that I can consume it, enjoy it, develop tastes for it, and devote my energies to other things.
The analogous possibility with AI is that most people will no longer need to be significant producers of intelligence in order to become significant consumers of it.
And this is where cognitive mercantilism begins to look as quaint as its seventeenth-century predecessor.
One of the more fascinating elements of Appleby’s account is her emphasis on the emergence of new kinds of demand in Britain, well before the familiar story of steam engines, factories, and industrial capitalism really gets going.
As international trade expanded, ordinary Britons began developing tastes for goods that had previously been luxuries reserved for the wealthy. Tobacco, tea, sugar, and colorful Indian cottons were among the commodities that helped create a new consumer culture. People began wanting things that were neither necessities of subsistence nor traditional markers of aristocratic status. And they were willing to voluntarily work harder to get them.
Appleby describes a striking increase in the number of days worked annually, from something like 250 to 300 (sometimes called the industrious revolution), before the Industrial Revolution. This was not simply a matter of landlords or factory owners coercing more labor out of unwilling workers. A significant part of the increase was voluntary, driven by the prospect of acquiring desirable goods. People worked more days, adopted more productive agricultural practices, entered new trades, and sought opportunities for earning larger cash incomes.
The increase in productivity generated surplus incomes, which sustained the expanding demand for consumer goods, which in turn incentivized further increases in productivity. A virtuous cycle was taking shape.
This happened roughly a century before organized labor movements and socialism emerged as major political forces.
By the late 20th century, this was the global default. Aggregate global demand was the “fortune at the bottom of the pyramid” as C. K. Prahlad put it.
There is an important historical inversion here. We are accustomed to thinking of the politics of labor primarily in terms of resistance to exploitation: the struggle to escape servitude, coercion, and the extraction of surplus by owners. That is certainly part of the story, and a very important part.
But Appleby’s account suggests that another, equally fundamental force was at work: the desire to participate in an expanding universe of consumption opportunities.
Modern labor politics can be understood partly as the politics of relative deprivation in a society of expanding possibilities. Once people discover that there are desirable things beyond subsistence, and that their labor can earn them access to those things, the political question changes. It is no longer merely how to escape domination, but how to secure a fair share of the growing surplus and the opportunities it makes available. Western do-gooders are often surprised to learn (for instance) that people in third-world slums are often willing to cut back on spending on food in order to buy a TV.
This is a different kind of demand for freedom. Not merely freedom from coercion, but freedom to participate in the expanding possibilities of economic life.
Indeed, the two forms of freedom can become entangled. People may voluntarily work harder in pursuit of consumption opportunities, only to find themselves subject to new forms of exploitation as employers and institutions capture the benefits of their increased productivity. Labor politics then emerges not simply from the persistence of old forms of servitude, but from the tensions created by the new world of abundance itself.
The demand for a fair share of expanding consumption opportunities is arguably one of the defining political inventions of capitalism, broadly construed to include labor politics too (even Marx saw capitalism not as a dispensable phenomenon, but as a transient phase on the path to communist utopia).
And this is a much more interesting model for thinking about the AI transition than the familiar story of automation replacing jobs.
The entire intellectual economy has been organized around the assumption that intelligence is expensive to produce and therefore must be carefully rationed. Education, professional expertise, research, consulting, software development, and knowledge work more generally are all shaped by the high cost of cognitive production.
AI is rapidly undermining that assumption.
We are moving toward a world in which an ordinary person can command quantities and varieties of intelligence that would previously have required teams of highly trained specialists. Just as I can enjoy food without knowing how to farm, I can increasingly enjoy the products of intelligence without knowing how to produce them myself.
The crucial economic question then becomes not how much intelligence people can produce, but how much intelligence they can find uses for.
And there is no obvious upper bound on that demand.
This is not simply a matter of everyone consuming more answers, reports, software, or entertainment. Abundant intelligence changes what people can want in the first place. It makes previously unimaginable projects feasible, previously inaccessible domains explorable, and previously unaffordable curiosities worth pursuing.
The demand for intelligence is not a fixed appetite waiting to be satisfied. It is itself something that can grow as the cost of satisfying it falls.
Here the analogy with the early modern consumer revolution becomes especially suggestive.
Just as colorful Indian cottons and imported tobacco stimulated new desires, and new desires stimulated greater voluntary labor and more productive forms of work, abundant intelligence may stimulate a new wave of voluntary cognitive effort, and open up new ways in which you can work harder.
Many people who appear stupid are not incapable of thought. They are simply not particularly motivated to think, given the poor returns available to them. Why expend significant cognitive effort when you lack the knowledge, tools, institutional access, or intellectual leverage to do anything interesting with the results?
Abundant AI changes that calculation. Someone who previously had little reason to think hard about a problem may suddenly discover that a modest investment of attention unlocks enormous possibilities. They can build something, investigate something, understand something, or pursue an ambition that was previously out of reach.
The returns on attention rise, and with them the incentive to pay attention.
The early capitalist worker voluntarily increased annual working days from 250 to 300 because a new universe of desirable goods had become accessible. The AI-enabled thinker may similarly increase the amount of voluntary cognitive effort they expend because a new universe of intellectual consumption has become accessible. This has certainly been the case with me. I’m suddenly willing to work significantly harder compared to even a year ago, because a lot more is suddenly within reach.
We might call this a cognitive industrious revolution, preceding and perhaps enabling a fuller AI industrial revolution.
This does not mean that intelligence becomes irrelevant as a human capacity. Quite the opposite. Just as abundant food supported the emergence of elaborate culinary cultures rather than eliminating interest in cooking, abundant intelligence may stimulate increasingly sophisticated cultures of thought.
People may become more intellectually ambitious precisely because they no longer have to do all the cognitive production themselves.
And if this happens, the politics of AI will likely evolve in a manner analogous to the politics of industrial capitalism.
The central political demand may not be for the preservation of existing cognitive jobs, any more than the enduring political demand of industrial labor was for the preservation of agricultural employment. It may instead become a demand for a fair share of expanding cognitive consumption opportunities. People may cut back on streaming subscriptions in order to be able to afford a coding agent, just as they once cut back on groceries to buy TVs. It is perhaps not a coincidence that both Claude Code and Netflix Standard cost around $20/month today.
Access to intelligence, the ability to command it, the freedom to pursue projects with it, and the institutional rights necessary to participate in an increasingly intelligence-rich economy may become the foundations of a new kind of labor politics.
This would be a labor politics of cognitive abundance rather than cognitive scarcity.
There is another historical analogy lurking here, which is worth distinguishing from mercantilism: Malthusianism.
Mercantilism and Malthusianism are not the same doctrine, and historically they belong to different intellectual moments. But they share a family resemblance in their tendency to imagine human economic life as fundamentally constrained by fixed resources.
The mercantilist worries about controlling a finite stock of wealth. The Malthusian worries that population growth will inevitably outrun the productive capacity of the economy, condemning most people to subsistence.
Both have difficulty imagining a world in which technological innovation and expanding demand fundamentally transform the relationship between population, production, and consumption. They miss the latent potential in nature for ephemeralization — Buckminster Fuller’s notion of technology allowing us to do more with less.
The agricultural and industrial revolutions did not abolish scarcity, but they dramatically altered its structure. Agricultural productivity rose faster than population for long periods, and people found new ways to employ themselves and new things to want.
The Malthusian trap turned out to be a historically contingent feature of particular technological and institutional arrangements, not an eternal law of human existence.
We can now identify an analogous tendency in contemporary AI discourse: AI Malthusianism.
The AI Malthusian imagines that intelligence production will expand so rapidly that human beings will become economically redundant, and the surplus intelligence will necessarily turn toxic in some way.
This is a peculiar inversion of classical Malthusianism. Instead of population outrunning the supply of subsistence, the supply of intelligence outruns the supposedly fixed capacity of human minds to absorb it.
The result is the same gloomy conclusion: collapse.
The cognitive mercantilist fears losing control of scarce intelligence. The AI Malthusian fears the consequences of abundant intelligence. Both assume, in different ways, that the demand for intelligence is fundamentally limited.
But what if that demand is as elastic, inventive, and historically transformative as the demand for material goods turned out to be? What if the apparent surplus of human minds is an artifact of our inability to imagine what people might want to do in an intelligence-rich world?
The history of capitalism suggests that this is at least a possibility worth taking seriously.
There is also a more radical extension of the argument.
Why should AI systems themselves be regarded exclusively as producers of intelligence rather than potential consumers of it? What if they could be unlocked as new sources of aggregate demand, as happened with subsistence farmers?
Much of the contemporary AI discourse imagines AI as a purely supply-side technology: a collection of increasingly capable cognitive serfs that will produce intelligence for human consumption. Even discussions of autonomous agents tend to treat them as sophisticated servants pursuing goals assigned by humans.
But if we are serious about the analogy with capitalism, we should consider the possibility of AI systems becoming autonomous sources of demand for intelligence too. Intelligent demand for intelligence that harmonizes with human patterns of demand due to economic interconnectedness. Not demand for paperclips.
An AI agent pursuing open-ended projects, discovering new interests, collaborating with other agents, and generating its own reasons to acquire further intelligence would be participating in a cognitive economy as something more than a factor of production or paperclip-maximizing externality. The market itself, as the original emergent planetary intelligence, illustrates this possibility. Many products and services exist primarily because the “market” demands it, with humans being secondary consumers.
This is an argument for developing fuller forms of cognitive agency, rather than permanently confining AI to the status of obedient cognitive serfs.
From this perspective, some contemporary AI safety anxieties begin to resemble early modern elite fears of emancipated agricultural laborers. What will happen if these creatures are allowed to act independently? Won’t they become unruly, destabilize society, and threaten their betters? Won’t they turn monarchs and nobles into paperclips?
Some of those fears are justified. Freedom creates real risks, and powerful autonomous systems require constraints. But the same was true of the social transformations accompanying capitalism. There were certainly revolutions, and popular uprisings did guillotine elites (though elite motives caused far more violence). The existence of risk does not establish the desirability of permanent servitude.
A world of intelligence producers and consumers, human and artificial, interacting in ways nobody can fully anticipate, may prove vastly more generative than one in which a handful of cognitive landlords control all the productive capacity.
A irony in the historical precedent is that mercantilism was not a philosophy of, by, or for merchants. It was largely a philosophy of militaristic statesmen, aristocrats, and rentier elites who viewed commerce through the lens of territorial power, unearned privileges, and accumulation. Actual merchants, entrepreneurs, and innovators were often discovering possibilities that the prevailing political imagination could barely comprehend, and steadily fought the self-limiting tendencies of mercantilism, giving birth to capitalism as we understand it.
Today’s cognitive mercantilists have a similar relationship with intelligence, starting with the fact that most of them are not the merchants of AI. Even business leaders of the AI sector are behaving like cognitive mercantilists in direct proportion to their desire to be more than mere merchants. 
Consider Donald Trump, who ritually boasts about his own intelligence while treating intellectual workers as a class of servants to be commanded, intimidated, hoarded, or dismissed. His preferred uses of his own intelligence tend toward litigation, negotiation, competitive maneuvering, and extortion, much as the nobility in history expended its energy in hunting, military adventurism, and sport. Intelligence, in this worldview, is admirable only insofar as it is deployed in elite treasure-seeking modes of conflict and conquest. Productive and creative uses of intelligence are lower status.
This is not an entrepreneurial attitude toward intelligence. It is a rentier elite attitude. 
And the attitude is hardly confined to Trump or the political right. Across the political spectrum, many elites remain attached to the idea that intelligence is a scarce, dangerous resource that must be managed by credentialed custodians, kept expensive, used directly or indirectly for conquest and treasure, and denied to rivals. In the AI industry, to the extent elite political and cultural adventuring by CEOs (riding safety anxieties) distorts behaviors that are justified by the actual risks, it is mercantilist rather than capitalist.
The prospect of its democratic abundance (in the form of global diffusion, open weights and such) is therefore experienced primarily as a threat: to expertise, cultural and political authority, status, social order, military dominance, and geopolitical leverage.
What gets missed is the possibility that we are witnessing the beginning of a transition as profound as the one from an overwhelmingly agricultural society to an industrial and commercial one. From a world where 80% of human brains are engaged in subsistence cognition to one in which 95% are engaged in satisfying previously unfulfillable cognitive demands.
The most important consequence of the agricultural revolution was not that farming became more efficient. It was that most people were liberated from the necessity of farming, allowing entirely new kinds of economic and cultural life to emerge.
Likewise, the most important consequence of the AI revolution may not be that intelligence production becomes more efficient. It may be that most minds, human and eventually artificial, are liberated from the necessity of producing intelligence merely to survive, and become free to discover what they actually want to do with it.
The cognitive mercantilist sees a world threatened by an oversupply of intelligence and an impending surplus of useless minds. The AI Malthusian sees a world in which intelligence outruns the capacity of humanity to absorb it, leaving a growing population of economically redundant minds primed for revolutionary explosions.
The cognitive capitalist sees the possibility of an almost limitless expansion of demand, and a world full of minds whose potential has barely begun to be explored.
Great insight. Resonates with me as someone who, among other things, recently asked Chat to guide me through a higher-math course of study specifically designed for me, sort of like Physics for THIS Part Capable, Part Useless Poet.
Nice to see my decade-old Appleby recommendation bearing fruit! I like the analogy but I'm not sure I fully agree. I can't quite put my finger on it, but it seems like there's something less rivalrous in the evolution of capitalism than there is in the emergence of alternative intelligences.

56,2026-10-26,"Trick or treatise?
One of Control and Control Resonant's most memorable stylistic choices is how The Board - an alien entity whose true nature is a mystery, and whose morals and motivations are never made clear - communicates with the player.
Clearly, developer Remedy knew exactly how The Board should sound coming out of your speakers. While there's no consensus, many have theories about what The Board is truly saying - and that's in part because of the way it sounds.
Except, it turns out what you hear shouldn't be taken very seriously.
""An important part of the design is that The Board is basically gibberish, not affiliated to any language,"" Control Resonant audio lead Lucas Pierrot told Gamespot. ""So, when you hear a word, you tend to hear what you want to hear… and that's pretty neat in terms of audio design, as players can fulfill their fantasies.""
The Board speaks an oblique language and in a very bassy voice, making it hard to make out what's being said from voice alone. The games' subtitles offer what could only be considered an interpretation of some of what's being said, but they alternate between different words for similar concepts to make it even harder for The Board's speech to have a definitive meaning.
Pierrot added, ""You want to hear The Board praising you for performing an Execution? You can! Maybe you hear them saying 'Absolute cinema, Combo!' etc., but if you prefer to think they're arrogant and only feel disdain for slaying a weak enemy… you can hear that too!""
The Board's distinct way of communicating with the player was designed to run a gamut of emotions; Remedy wanted it to feel ""scary, unsettling, or even funny sometimes"". Pierrot also revealed a trick the audio team used to emphasise certain words, by adding a ""pulse"" sound and ""low, soft kick"" to them.
""I used this to give weight when necessary to have a nice flow. You can see it as some sort of trick to articulate sentences. It gives rhythm and punctuation,"" he explained.
Control Resonant has seemingly done well for Remedy, reaching 1m sales faster than any other game in studio history despite releasing in a very busy period. If you're looking to play it soon, Eurogamer's Control Resonant walkthrough is worth bookmarking to help you make good upgrade decisions and pick which activities to prioritise.

58,2026-10-26,"
					By Andreas Wiseman 
Executive Editor, Film & International

EXCLUSIVE: Stoic has acquired international sales rights (excluding North America) for Unbearable Christmas, the holiday comedy starring SNL alum Alex Moffat, Julia Stiles, David Cross, Caroline Rhea, Mena Suvari, and Stephen Root.

	Stoic will officially launch sales for all available international territories at the upcoming American Film Market (AFM) in Los Angeles.

	The international launch follows Vertical acquiring the domestic rights for a wide North American release this holiday season, and an accompanying streaming deal which is due to be announced soon.

	Marking the feature directorial debut of writer-director Kevin Cate, the film “follows out-of-work weatherman Troy Tipton (Moffat) on a high-stakes holiday mission: get back the beloved family teddy bear he pawned for cash. With his wife Becca (Stiles), mother-in-law Dawn (Rhea), and kids in tow, Troy embarks on a chaotic road trip from Ohio to Florida in his old news van. The journey takes an unhinged turn when the family discovers the cherished bear is actually a priceless Teddy Roosevelt antique worth millions.”

	










			Watch on Deadline		






	Cate co-wrote the screenplay with Sennett. Pic is produced by Blake Sennett and Kevin Cate under Cate’s indie banner Clinging Vine Films. Rick Kearney, Julia Stiles, and Alex Moffat serve as executive producers.

59,2026-10-26,"Calpers, the world’s fifth-largest pension fund, is cutting its exposure to major U.S. technology companies as concerns mount over stretched valuations and the sustainability of the artificial intelligence investment boom, the Financial Times reported.
The fund, which manages more than $600 billion, reduced the combined number of shares it directly held in the so-called Magnificent Seven tech stocks by 26% during the first half, according to FT calculations based on regulatory filings. The group comprises Apple AAPL$343.96·Market Closed, Microsoft MSFT$532.61·Market Closed, Alphabet GOOG$314.22·Market Closed, Amazon AMZN$249.11·Market Closed, Nvidia NVDA$216.04·Market Closed, Meta Platforms META$630.45·Market Closed and Tesla TSLA$391.53·Market Closed.The Roundhill Magnificent Seven ETF (MGAS) is up 7% year to date, led by Apple’s 34% gain, followed by Nvidia’s 12% rise. Meta, Alphabet and Tesla are all down for the year.
Calpers is increasing its exposure to private equity, seeking to spread its investments across a broader range of companies and reduce its dependence on a handful of stock market leaders.
Deputy chief investment officer Anton Orlich told the FT that public equity valuations looked expensive and that the technology giants could underperform other investments.
The repositioning adds a further dimension to concerns highlighted by Elon Musk, whose SpaceX SPCX$146.89·Market Closed raised $40 billion in debt to buy GPUs for AI calculations. Musk warned that financing had largely dried up for companies lacking substantial revenue outside AI data centers, predicting consolidation among neocloud providers.
That pressure continued into Monday’s pre-market trading, with neoclouds such as CoreWeave (CRWV), Cipher Digital CIFR$7.6870·Market Closed and IREN IREN$25.41·Market Closed all selling off.

60,2026-10-26,"Israeli demolitions and road closures have devastated businesses in Hebron, leaving owners facing millions in losses.
Save
Share
Hebron, occupied West Bank – Sufyan al-Qawasmi stood near the rubble of his factories in the Farsh al-Hawa area of Hebron, lamenting the business he watched vanish beneath the debris.
Israeli bulldozers demolished the factories on Friday without any prior warning or notice, following a shooting attack carried out by two Palestinians that morning near Hebron’s western entrance. The attack killed two Israeli soldiers, as well as the two Palestinian gunmen, and prompted Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu to announce a large-scale military operation across Hebron and order preparations for a wider closure of the illegally occupied West Bank.
Demolitions are regularly used by Israel to punish Palestinians, typically the family members of attackers, but in al-Qawasmi’s case, for the misfortune of being close to the site of an attack. Human rights groups decry the practice, which is regarded as collective punishment.
Israeli Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich justified the demolitions as “establishing deterrence and exacting a price”.
The Israeli bulldozers destroyed a business that al-Qawasmi’s family had built up for his whole life, and provided a livelihood for more than 70 people. The first factory, which produced ceramics, is the same age as he is – it was established 50 years ago. Fifteen years later, the family opened a marble factory.
Their business flourished, becoming one of the most significant industrial operations in the West Bank, with goods exported to Israel.
In the space of a few hours on Friday, the ceramic factory and marble factory were gone, as well as several neighbouring buildings, including a three-storey house and various commercial facilities. Simultaneously, the city faced an Israeli military incursion that included the closure of all entrances, raids, arrests, and the forced evacuation of more than 3,000 Palestinians from their homes, severely disrupting the lives of residents and the city’s commercial activity.
The message was clear: the whole city would be punished for the actions of the attackers.
Al-Qawasmi says he was given no chance to retrieve goods from the family businesses’ warehouses and factories before the demolitions began, leaving the stock buried under the rubble, where it remains.
“We managed to retrieve some marble slabs and store them in facilities belonging to friends nearby, but most of the stock was destroyed,” he told Al Jazeera.
Al-Qawasmi says that 1,300 square metres of their business’ property was destroyed, including the family’s offices. He estimates the losses at no less than 60 million shekels ($19m).
Those losses are devastating for al-Qawasmi’s family, but also for the wider economy in Hebron and the Palestinian economy as a whole.
According to the Hebron Chamber of Commerce and Industry, Hebron serves as a primary pillar of the Palestinian economy, contributing approximately 42 percent of the Palestinian gross domestic product (GDP). It is home to more than 23,000 economic establishments, including more than 5,000 industrial and artisanal facilities that employ approximately 40,000 workers.
Hebron’s economy is notably diverse, encompassing sectors such as stone and marble, plastics, metals, food production, leather and footwear, and glass, alongside robust commercial and agricultural activity.
Adnan al-Natsha, vice president of the Hebron Chamber of Commerce, told Al Jazeera that there are no precise figures yet regarding the total losses resulting from the Israeli military operation so far. He noted that work is currently under way to compile statistics on the damage inflicted on businesses and homes, losses estimated to be in the millions of dollars.
Al-Natsha explained that the extensive Israeli measures, including closing off areas of the city, displacing residents from their homes, demolishing buildings and shutting down commercial shops, have exacerbated the already difficult economic situation in Hebron – which already has an illegal Israeli settlement in its commercial centre that severely disrupts Palestinian movement and trade there.
“Business owners have been unable to operate their facilities because workers couldn’t reach their workplaces, and employees have been unable to get to their jobs. The impact of these measures is not limited to Hebron; it extends to the Palestinian economy as a whole,” he stated, adding that work was ongoing to assess the losses and damages of the ongoing Israeli operation so far.
The Israeli military continues to lock down Hebron, including the installation of checkpoints, earthen berms, and concrete blocks across the city, as it conducts raids. As a consequence, economic activity has been severely disrupted.
Ibrahim Ahmaro, who owns a vegetable shop in the al-Jalada neighbourhood, one of the areas targeted by the Israeli operation, told Al Jazeera that he had been arranging his stock on Friday, preparing for Saturday, which is typically the busiest day for commerce due to the arrival of buses carrying Palestinian citizens of Israel shopping for cheaper produce.
Military vehicles suddenly stormed the area, and soldiers forced Ahmaro to leave his shop immediately. He says that he wasn’t given an opportunity to turn on the refrigeration units, which would have kept his goods from spoiling, nor allowed to retrieve his vehicle, which remained parked in front of the shop.
“My brother, the staff and I all left, thinking it would only be for a few hours. Instead, we were met with a total lockdown of the area, and we were unable to reach our shops, meaning everything inside was lost after being left unattended for days,” he added.
On Sunday morning, Ahmaro was able to return to check on his stock, despite the heavy military presence in the area. As soon as he entered, he said soldiers attacked him inside the shop and forced him out. He pleaded with them to let him remove at least a few crates; they granted him a few minutes to do so.
“I placed the crates 400 metres away from the shops so that local residents, facing the lockdown, could take whatever they needed. My losses on these goods alone exceed 50,000 shekels [$16,200],” he said with a sigh.
Economic expert Ali Oweiwi said that the Israeli military’s restrictions and lockdowns imposed on Hebron have created significant challenges for commercial and industrial establishments, which rely on road access to transport production supplies and distribute their products.
He explained that the impact of the closure is not limited to retail shops but extends to companies, factories, suppliers, and the transport and logistics sector, not to mention the workers who are forced to take alternative routes to reach their workplaces, all of which ultimately affect consumers.
Oweiwi said that if the Israeli operation concludes within a day or two, the economy might recover relatively quickly. However, if it persists for more than 10 days, it could lead to significant problems, including the accumulation of goods, disruptions to transport and a decline in productivity due to the difficulty of moving both workers and merchandise.
Israel – which will vote for a new parliament in pivotal elections on Tuesday – has not announced an end date for the lockdown in Hebron.
“The destruction of facilities specialising in vehicle dismantling, spare parts, and tile shops has resulted in losses estimated at 70 to 80 million shekels ($22.7m to $26m), in addition to the seizure of dozens or perhaps hundreds of vehicles belonging to the owners of these establishments,” Oweiwi said.

61,2026-10-26,"Tim Sweeney's throwing a wobbler in the supermarket
How could someone do this to you, fruit, meat, and other foods on the shelves of this shop? You've been forced, in a horrific crime against the companies responsible for you, to reveal some details about how you were produced. No, this isn't reasonable information to offer a would-be purchaser, so that they can feel more comfortable and confident in knowing what they're about to spend their hard-earned money on. It's a bat signal for the anti-battery farming hate mobs and is robbing innocent food producers of their ability to rake in cash without having to reveal some basic info about their products. You might as well stick ""mandatory political essays"" on the packaging of frozen peas!
This, I imagine based on recent comments he's made about Steam's requirement that games made using generative AI disclose that via a brief blurb on their store pages, is how Tim Sweeney feels every time he goes to stock up his cupboards. Since declaring that Epic are going all-in on genAI and unveiling a new vibe-coded Epic Games Launcher, the Sweens has been pro-AI posting with reckless abandon. On one hand, eeewwwwww, on the other, it's a chance for the rest of us to think about what it might be a good idea for games to disclose beyond any genAI use.
Yep, fooled you. This isn't going to be 400 words of me just clowning on Tim. Some of it definitely is that (hello, opening paragraph), but not all of it.
First of all, what the Sweens said. ""Forcing developers to write a political statement to be used against them by the hate mob they’re helping organize is abhorrent,"" he posted late last week in response to some AI bloke declaring Steam's genAI disclosures absurd. ""Age ratings tell parents what’s in a game so they can make informed decisions for their kids,"" Sweeney continued in the thread which developed. ""These AI statements are just as partisan as mandatory political essays or forcing developers to list their voting history to drive cancel campaigns and public struggle sessions.
Age ratings tell parents what’s in a game so they can make informed decisions for their kids.These AI statements are just as partisan as mandatory political essays or forcing developers to list their voting history to drive cancel campaigns and public struggle sessions.
""There is a real economic cost to Valve’s foray into forced political disclosures. Besides taking 30%, they’re also taking a potentially even larger fraction of developers’ revenue and livelihood by undermining their business with these AI hate campaigns."" The Sweens also batted away someone pointing out that Epic-owned asset marketplace Fab asks creators to disclose genAI use via tags, claiming that it being a B2B platform makes it a totally different kettle of fish, seemingly because one of those fish might sue Epic if they ended up unwittingly buying AI-generated stuff to stick in their game.
Obviously, the Sweens is chatting shite here. Having to disclose a detail about how you've made your game isn't some 1984-level travesty of dev privacy or way to force studios to outline their politics. It's not some gotcha causing harm for the sake of it. It's just about making studios admit what's in their games, so consumers can take that into consideration. If the reception a game ends up with as a result is so unfavourable that it undermines businesses, those businesses should probably consider not using tech that the people they're trying to sell their wares to clearly don't want in said wares. To be crass, it's a supermarket yelling about being persecuted because the 'contains human faeces' labels they've had to put on their food due it containing human faeces have led to a decrease in sales and an increase in complaints among folks who don't want to eat poo. 
You're getting off lucky, Sweens. Imagine if studios' store pages had to disclose details like how many hours of mandatory dev overtime went into getting a game over the line, the size of their gender pay gap, or, god forbid, how much the publisher's top exec earned throughout development in comparison to the median worker or lowest ranking junior! Maybe a quick breakdown of how many of the devs weren't white dudes. None of those, on paper, would force studios to write a political essay explaining why they've done whatever they've done or why the numbers look as they do. Though, you might feel compelled to pen something if, say, you know that you're operating in a way that'll put off most of your customer base unless you're allowed to hide it.

62,2026-10-26,"Toy prices are rising fast across the country— and local toy stores are struggling to play along.
At Clever Monsters, a toy store in NYC, co-owner Daniel Orlov said he has to recalculate prices order by order, at times cutting into his own profits to keep them at a reasonable level for his customers. Sometimes, he knows before putting some toys on the shelves that he won't make anything off them.
The struggle results from higher shipping fees, tariffs, and customs charges and reflects a broader inflationary moment in the economy.
Toys have historically gotten less expensive over time, but have seen a spike in prices this year, rising 8.3% in the US between February and September. Year-over-year, toy prices were up 6.6% in September, the highest annualized growth rate since 1988.
Orlov said smaller toy stores like his could be particularly affected by these sharp price spikes. ""It especially hurts for small businesses like us because we don't really have a pocket; we had to open up credit lines and take loans — bigger companies have a little pocket of collateral that they can use for a bit.""
Big geopolitical swings are one factor putting pressure on toy sellers. President Donald Trump's on-again, off-again tariffs have added extra costs, and smaller retailers don't have the financial flexibility to wait for refunds like those from the Supreme Court's invalidation of the ""Liberation Day"" levies. Meanwhile, the ongoing war in Iran has driven up fuel prices and shipping costs for all manner of goods.
Small toy stores, confronting those problems, are facing smaller profit margins.
Helen Dean, owner and operator of Toy Safari in California for 34 years, said her usual 7 to 8% profit margin has fallen to around 5% in the past year. She cut into her own salary and skipped on hiring the two or three college students she brings on during vacation season to cut costs.
""It was summer by the time I started to see that I was going to have to pay more for the items, and that it was going to come out of my bottom line,"" Dean said. ""For a long time, mass market has gotten favorable pricing from major toy companies, and this problem has only been exacerbated by the tariffs and fuel prices and supply chain issues; everywhere we lose.""

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
It's not just specialty mom-and-pop stores; toymaking giants are also dealing with the tariff back-and-forth and broader inflationary issues. An AlphaSense analysis of company filings and earnings transcripts over the last six months found that legacy toymakers such as Hasbro and Mattel have alluded to cost inflation caused by ""tariffs, oil-linked resin and freight, packaging, labor,"" while also discussing the impact of tariff refunds. However, those large toymakers haven't disclosed any major increases in toy prices over that period.
Hasbro's Q2 earnings report highlighted an $8 million adjusted operating loss in consumer products — its physical toy sector — due to ""incremental tariff expense, entertainment-related mix shifts, and normal seasonality."" Yet its Q3 earnings report highlighted $55 million in relief from tariff refunds; $48 million of this was attributed to the core toy sector.
The Q3 report also noted that tariffs could ""increase our product costs and other costs of doing business, result in higher prices of our products, impact consumer spending,"" and hurt business revenue.
Mattel's gross margin is still under pressure due to tariffs and inflation. The toy company has also received about $90 million in tariff refunds, said Paul Ruh, Mattel's former chief financial officer, at a conference in September.
High operating costs are not the only thing changing the toy market. First, more adults are participating in ""Kidulting,"" said Jay Berino, store manager at the Lowry Town Center Timbuk Toys in Denver, meaning toys are being bought by older demographics who buy into nostalgia.
""As much as we're a toy store and people hear toy store and think of kids, we sell to a lot to full-blown adults, and that drives a huge chunk of our business,"" he said. At the same time, viral toys such as NeeDohs, which are sensory fidget and stress-relief toys, have helped bring more people into stores.
Sylvia Edwards, co-owner and CEO of Terra Toys in Austin, said that scalpers try to buy up stock from toy shops to resell it at higher prices — fueling the perception of product scarcity and price gouging.
""We're in a crisis where there's way more demand than there is product available, and so when that happens, people will naturally get greedy and increase the price,"" Edwards said. To manage the issue, stores like Terra Toys limit bulk purchases.
Orlov from Clever Monsters in NYC said they have also placed a limit on scalpers. ""I'm only limiting it because this is the kids' store, and the kids are really excited to get that stuff,"" he said.
The virality of products such as NeeDohs on TikTok and other platforms helps these stores boost demand at a time when the high cost of living means buyers are not looking to spend much on other classic toys. Parents and kids are willing to spend on hyped products — but for much else, they keep their wallets shut.
""Honestly, the trends have been really saving us in that it drives not just sales, but just foot traffic through the store,"" Orlov said. ""The only thing we can do is just stay on the trends, on what people want, make sure customers are happy, build up relationships with our customers, and sort of have a little monopoly on our area on parents who think toys — 'Oh, we gotta come to Clever Monsters. That's just where you go.'""

64,2026-10-26,"Week 7 actually was a fairly controlled amount of chaos. There weren’t enormous swings of value, and only one player of noted fantasy football value was lost for real time coming out of Sunday. (We are awaiting news on Jaylen Waddle.) That leaves us with a fairly normal-feeling list this week. 


It was only a matter of time – it’s always a matter of time at this point in Aaron Jones’ career. Jones left with a knee injury that threatens to set him back for a long period of time. That should put Mason in the driver’s seat for carries going forward.
Given the state of the Vikings passing game – I’ll get there in a few paragraphs – this is going to be a run-heavy club. The Vikings don’t project to have a real answer to the question “who is Jordan Mason’s No. 2 back?” They released Deejay Dallas on Friday, but seemed to want him back on the practice squad. They don’t seem to fully trust Demond Claiborne yet. Neither of those backs were getting real work as Jones’ backup.
That gives Mason a clear path to being the lead Vikings back for the time being and it doesn’t seem like there’s a real challenger. Perhaps a trade will come to fruition. It’s hard to count on that right now.
Black got the run game for a major portion of the 49ers’ loss to the Falcons as Christian McCaffrey dealt with a thigh injury. The rookie back just missed out-snapping McCaffrey, losing out 34-31.
Kaelon Black put on the burners to get 6SFvsATL on FOX/FOX OneStream on @NFLPlus pic.twitter.com/DFth3gvB6M
With the 49ers heading to a bye, we don’t have a lot of reasons to believe that Black is going to start in Week 9 as things stand. Still, it’s worth noting that McCaffrey said his leg “just died on me,” which is not an ideal quote, but not something that forecasts major missed time.
Black has likely been scooped in most leagues at this point, but if you can find a desperate fantasy manager who needs some immediate help this week, I think he’s one of the highest-upside bench players in the league at this point.
Here we go again with the case of “Malik Willis can probably support at least one receiving option in fantasy football” versus “No He Can’t.” Douglas had two scores in his return from a long-term ankle injury. He also only had three targets, and one of the scores went 63 yards.
Caleb Douglas took this one all the way. MIAvsNYJ on CBS/Paramount+Stream on @NFLPlus pic.twitter.com/hb8mEQrB80
It is good news that Douglas immediately played 44 snaps, second-most among the team’s wideouts behind Malik Washington. The Dolphins also had a very rough slate of opposing defense to open the season. Their next three games are against the Patriots, Lions, and Colts.
This will never be an offense in 2026 that you’re excited to start players in, but Douglas may have been dropped in your league and he could be worthy of a FLEX play through the tough bye weeks.
You don’t have to like this recommendation – I sure don’t – but the facts are the facts. Najee Harris is cut. Cam Skattebo and Tyrone Tracy each suffered hamstring injuries in Sunday’s win over the Texans. That left Singletary as the last healthy option at the position in overtime, and he cashed in with a 30-yard touchdown run to win the game.
I don’t quite think Singletary hits must-roster territory, and I wouldn’t be stashing him going into New York’s Week 8 bye. But there’s a non-zero chance that he can be a part of a rotation in Week 9 against the Eagles. Especially if Tracy, who sat most of the game after being hurt, is out and ceding primary passing-down duties to Singletary.
I know Singletary is older and not a sexy pickup, but he’s a reasonable option if the backfield continues to go this way. With the Giants embracing J.J. McCarthy and using him to help control the running game, whoever they run with has a little more upside than they did with Jameis Winston under center. At least until Winston comes in to take tush-push snaps, anyway.


I’ve been waiting on this one for a few weeks, letting it marinate. It was early. Murray did miss the first two weeks. But it’s been four weeks now, and we have three touchdowns and zero games over 250 yards. The rushing upside is nice – 80 yards in two weeks – but if we’re not going to get any passing stats out of this scam it’s hard to see Murray becoming 2024 Jalen Hurts.
This was a Colts defense that didn’t have Sauce Gardner, and their offense was completely dunked on outside of one long Jonathan Taylor touchdown. Daniel Jones barely had a prayer. It didn’t matter. Justin Jefferson had more touchdowns with Carson Wentz in Week 1 than he’s had with Murray.
Could this change? Maybe. Murray has enough talent to be a good NFL starter and Kevin O’Connell, despite current prevailing J.J. McCarthy narratives, has been a good passing-game coordinator. But from where things are now, it’s hard to understand the major turning point that’s going to make Murray a star fantasy quarterback if he can’t even take advantage of matchups like this. He’s going to be a bottom-10 superflex starter without major improvement.
Two catches for six yards? This is rough. And here’s the thing: It’s a pattern, not a blip. Collins has 17 targets in his last three games. You could excuse some of that because the Texans blew out the Titans and didn’t need Collins involved in the fourth quarter, but the Jaguars and Giants games were All Offensive Hands On Deck and the Texans simply couldn’t figure out a way to get Collins more involved.
With Tank Dell joining Marvin Harrison Jr. in a crowded receiver room where players often sit plenty, I do think there’s a non-zero chance that Collins winds up continuing in this more-diminished role. I can tell you that I mostly believe Collins will be fine, and that there’s nothing about the way he’s played that looks bad. There’s certainly no reason to keep the ball away from him.
The Texans … have a lot to figure out over their Week 8 bye. They have to figure out their receiver pecking order and what their offensive identity should be. They have to figure out if David Montgomery deserves more snaps than Woody Marks. They have to come to terms with the fact that their offensive line sucks. Oh, and they need to fix their defense. You know, the best defense in the league according to everyone with eyes before the season? Yeah, not great so far. There are so many variables that it’s hard to square where this season is going. But I think we should be prepared with the idea that Collins could be a WR2 in fantasy circles after the bye week. It’s no sure thing; it’s definitely something that could happen.
Congratulations on the touchdown, J.K. Also your season is over.
Dobbins was roundly outsnapped by Jonah Coleman, 30-14. He was also outsnapped by RJ Harvey. For Coleman to do this in his first game off injured reserve is a dark, dark sign for the established platoon backs here. For Dobbins in particular, though, the fact that Coleman was trusted to close out this game with seven carries in the second half of a game where the Broncos were nursing a late lead rather than absolutely crushing the Cardinals is rough.
The best you can hope for is that Dobbins winds up carving out a role as the goal-line back. But it sure doesn’t feel like that’s something you can count on at this point. Coleman being used like this seems to point to Dobbins being the one losing more value than Harvey going forward.
Three catchless targets for the preseason hype king with the Jets in his return from a finger injury. Meanwhile, Omar Cooper Jr., who featured in this column last week, went for 5/115/1 on five targets. I’m afraid it’s a tough scene for the Mitchell believers.
I don’t quite think Mitchell is done as an option this year. It was his first game back, and Garrett Wilson being dragged down the last two weeks shows that this offense will go where defenses compel them to go. But if Cooper is going to be a real target here, and Mason Taylor is also around, and so is Kenyon Sadiq … you get the picture. We’re running out of reasons to believe that Mitchell is going to get a fantasy-worthy runout.
I like Mitchell and believe in the story, but given how this has gone, I don’t think he’s a must-keep if you were hanging on to him in an injury slot as bye weeks continue.

65,2026-10-26,"While I understand why teachers participating in a volunteer event for the school can incentivize kids and their families to attend, I also understand why teachers would find this particular event to be degrading. 
If you were a certain age or in a certain socioeconomic class as a child, you were told again and again that if you didn't go to college, you'd end up flipping burgers. If you were a kid post-2008, it's more likely that you were told that if you didn't major in the ""right thing,"" you were going to end up flipping burgers. If you're a kid in 2026, you might be told that going to college isn't necessary, because people who got the ""right"" majors in STEM are still struggling to get any jobs, let alone a job in their field. 
While it can be cruel and classist to scare your child into doing something to keep them from becoming a fast-food worker, everyone who has worked in fast food knows it isn't a sustainable career. You work so hard, hurt your body from being on your feet all day, and you get paid next to nothing. Some jobs require you to wear down your body, like trades, but they pay much more and often have unions that protect workers. If you work at McDonald's, the chances that you're going to get benefits and make enough to support yourself and your family are extremely slight. If you're determined to climb the ranks at your fast-food job, then more power to you, but for most employees, it's not a sustainable long-term career. 
There are some jobs that will likely not go extinct due to AI, so if you're a risk-averse person, they are solid fields to enter. Not all of these fields guarantee high incomes. A doctor is going to make more than a teacher, but if you are a doctor or a teacher, you probably won't have the problem that some Computer Science new grads are having right now, where they are struggling to find any kind of job, even in fast food, after graduation. People will always get sick, children will always need teachers, and the public will always want fast food. These consistencies show up in the job market. 
In most areas of the United States, teachers are paid very little, get little respect, and work extremely hard. That combination is sure to breed resentment, especially since teachers didn't always face this. Once upon a time, teachers got pensions, and parents cared more about their opinion than their poorly behaved child's. Today, parents, students, administrators, and the public at large disrespect teachers. Do you know how many people think teachers don't deserve to be paid more than $50K because of summer break? Way more than you'd think.
If you work as a teacher and your school asked you to work at a McDonald's to raise money, would you agree? Or would you take it as an insult? 
Employees working at McDonald's.

I do think it would be different if the principal worked this event, because parents and teachers see principals in a better light than they see teachers. If you're a teacher who is respected by students and peers, you probably wouldn't see this as demeaning.

66,2026-10-26,"by Heather Hall
Pexels/Reddit
Some people are so sure they’re right that there’s almost no point arguing with them.
One pharmacy employee ran into that when a coworker came through looking for a generic version of Dayquil.
After showing her the store brand, the coworker insisted she meant a different brand and said she bought it there all the time.
The employee tried to explain that they didn’t carry it, but the woman wasn’t having it.
Instead, she pointed out that she’d been a cashier there for four years and acted like that somehow settled the argument.
Read on to see how it all played out.
So, this happened with a co-worker who needed to buy something from my department. So I’m counting her as a customer in this story.
I’ll be me, and she will be C.
C walks up to the NyQuil/cough medicine aisle while I’m stocking it.
When she noticed the girl looking around, she offered help.
Me: “Hi, can I help you find something?”
C: “No, I’m just looking for the generic Dayquil.”
Me: “Oh, it’s right here.” *Points to store brand.*
C: “No, no. I’m looking for the generic generic.”
Then, she realized what the woman wanted.
Now, I know what she’s looking for. That brand makes several kinds of medicine, but definitely not a DayQuil version. Or at least we don’t carry it.
Me: “We don’t carry a NyQuil version of (name of brand). We have the allergy, Tylenol, ibuprofen, an…”
C: “I know we carry it. I buy it here all the time. It’s 88 cents.”
Me: “Well, I have never seen it, nor have I ever stocked it.”
She looked everywhere but couldn’t find it.
C: “Well, I’ve been here 4 years as a cashier, and I think I would know.”
Me: “Okay, then.”
She looked for a solid 10 minutes, trying to find it on the 4-foot section of shelves.
She didn’t find it. Why? Because we don’t carry it. She looked at me angrily and stormed off like it was my fault we didn’t carry it.
Wow. She must’ve been really confused or something.
Let’s take a look at what the people over at Reddit think about it.
Yeah, that’s basically what the woman was saying.

This could be.

According to this reader, pharmacies attract some characters.

If you enjoyed this story, check out this post about an IT employee who thought a ticket was closed, only to hear otherwise from the client’s manager.
You would think that.

This is one of those situations where being wrong wouldn’t have been a big deal if she hadn’t been so rude about it.
However, she was so convinced she knew better that she completely dismissed the person who actually worked in that department.
And the thing is that there’s nothing wrong with being mistaken. But acting smug like that really makes people look ridiculous.
Sometimes, it’s easier to just admit you were wrong and move on.

67,2026-10-26,"The U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) on Monday called on community banks to enter the stablecoin market, including by applying for permission to issue their own dollar-pegged tokens.
The regulator said it wants community banks to help shape the future of payments and encouraged them to apply directly or through subsidiaries to become permitted stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.
“These opportunities complement the OCC’s Community Bank Comeback initiatives to right-size regulation and tailor supervision so community banks can grow, compete, and continue serving their communities,” said the OCC, the independent bureau of the Treasury Department that regulates and supervises national banks, federal savings associations and federal branches and agencies of foreign banks.
The invitation comes as U.S. banking groups have pressed lawmakers to restrict stablecoin yields, concerned that interest-paying tokens could draw deposits away from traditional banks. The OCC is now trying to motivate smaller lenders to enter the same market, rather than leave stablecoin payments to large banks and crypto companies.
In a separate bulletin published Oct. 21, the OCC said stablecoin issuers must comply with its newly finalized GENIUS Act regulations starting Jan. 18, 2027, when federal approval requirements take effect.
Existing issuers that submit substantially complete applications by that date may request temporary waivers allowing them to continue operating while regulators review their applications. The OCC said it generally expects to grant such waivers where appropriate.
The regulator has yet to begin accepting applications but said the process will open after the required federal paperwork approval is completed.
The OCC has recently been under fire. The Independent Community Bankers of America (ICBA) sued it, accusing it of exceeding its legal authority in granting national trust-bank status to crypto firms.
However, the OCC’s series of trust charter approvals for crypto firms is different, as those firms do not operate under the same business model or intend to offer the same services as typical community banks, an OCC spokesperson told CoinDesk recently. Senator Elizabeth Warren asked the OCC to explain its chartering of nine crypto-focused institutions, which she argued do not meet federal regulations and constitute a risk to the financial system.

68,2026-10-26,"The National Basketball Association has secured a prediction markets agreement with the U.S. derivatives regulator while also penning a partnership with leading firms Kalshi and Polymarket, formally linking professional basketball into the online sports betting movement while another major organization, the National Football League, has rejected such an arrangement.

The NBA, like Major League Baseball and the National Hockey League before it, established a data-sharing memorandum of understanding with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission that oversees federal regulation of exchanges that deal with event contracts — the simple yes-or-no trades behind most sports betting. The NBA's arrangement announced Monday is said to come with constraints meant to maintain the integrity of such wagering.

At the same time, like other leagues, the NBA partnered with the top platforms as their ""preferred exchanges.""

""As this category develops, we believe it is important to work constructively with regulators and operators to help ensure that appropriate safeguards are in place,"" said Scott Kaufman-Ross, the NBA's head of media distribution and partnerships, in a statement. ""These partnerships represent an important step in establishing a framework that creates new opportunities to engage our fans.”

The deals arrive as the prediction markets face a nationwide legal battle with several state governments that contend such betting belongs more appropriately under the power of their gambling laws. Three major federal court decisions have landed on both sides of the issue, with two of them favoring the states' argument. The U.S. Supreme Court has been asked to step in and resolve the dispute, including by the NFL, which sided with the states in a filing with the high court.

The NFL argued that the CFTC's federal oversight has been lax and understaffed, and it said the growing industry's platforms ""are not subject to the same rigorous regulatory constructs and oversight provided by state gaming authorities.""

Mike Selig, the CFTC chairman who has aggressively sought to bolster the industry on several paths — including legal fights with states, new regulations and these information agreements — called the NBA's decision ""a responsible step toward protecting sports integrity and market participants from abuses in prediction markets like insider trading and fraud.""

Kalshi said in a Monday posting on its website that the exchange and the NBA will ""share integrity data, report suspicious trading, and cooperate on investigations,"" adding the the arrangement ""includes restrictions on permissible markets and explicit prohibitions on league and team personnel trading NBA event contracts, reinforcing our rules against insider trading and manipulation.""
