69,2026-10-26,"Thomas Rau is a prominent Swiss doctor of alternative—or, as he calls it, “biological”—medicine. He believes that in most cases the best way to cure disease is not to attack the ailment directly but, rather, to boost the body’s natural defense systems. In his eyes, most conventional medical practitioners are timid and overly literal-minded. (A patient of Rau’s remembers him calling specialist doctors “narrowists.”) Whereas most doctors give a standard battery of CT scans and MRIs, Rau uses a technique called dark-field microscopy. He takes a dab of a patient’s blood, places it on a slide, and observes the cells’ behavior over time. Do they clump up, or are they motile? The presence of toxins or inflammation—indicated, Rau told me, by irregular formations or “stacked rings” of cells—dictates specific treatment protocols, often including a change of diet. He has written, of dark-field microscopy, “Hardly any other examination leads so quickly to conclusions about dynamic processes in the body.” He performs the test at BioMed, a clinic that he owns, along with an adjoining hotel, in the picturesque town of Schwellbrunn, Switzerland. Guests often stay for several weeks and spend tens of thousands of dollars.
Rau believes that virtually anyone can benefit from his interventions, many of which he defines as preventive. But he told me that his specialty is treating people with what he calls “unclear” conditions. He estimates that eighty per cent of such patients—principally, sufferers of long COVID or chronic-fatigue syndrome—go home from his clinic “massively improved.” In 2024, he spoke at an event in Brooklyn called “The Future of Medicine,” in which he described himself as “the master in rebuilding cellular function.”
Rau’s tool kit includes colon hydrotherapy (irrigating the bowels with warm water), ozone treatments (infusing the blood with the gas), and hyperthermia therapy (placing a patient on a bed heated by infrared-A radiation to mimic a fever, which supposedly shrinks tumors). He isn’t concerned by the absence of studies confirming that the treatments performed at BioMed work. “I pay a lot of taxes,” he told me when I visited him in Switzerland, in 2024. “I pay scientists’ salaries.”
Rau, a licensed doctor in Switzerland, says that he doesn’t reject mainstream medicine. But he certainly seems skeptical of its efficacy. He has said, “You give anti-inflammatories, you give anti-immune drugs—you give anti, anti, anti.” Although he’s not always opposed to conventional treatments, he often modifies the typical approach to fit his theories. For instance, he sometimes injects a chemotherapy drug directly into a tumor site—a nonstandard application. (He boasted to me, “This is a treatment I developed!”) A key goal of his cancer-treatment protocol is to lower inflammation throughout a patient’s body by removing gluten from her diet. Good health, he likes to say, comes from harmonizing the patient’s “inner milieu.”
Rau, who is seventy-six, has been practicing for forty-four years, mostly in the northeastern canton of Appenzell Ausserrhoden, which includes Schwellbrunn. He estimates that he’s seen more than ten thousand patients. The back cover of his book “The Swiss Secret to Optimal Health,” which was published in English in 2007, touts him as the “Mozart of medicine.” He has taught hundreds of other doctors, naturopaths, and healers his techniques.
Traditional doctors do not generally rate the kinds of treatments given by Rau highly. American health authorities, for example, do not consider live-blood analysis to be a reliable diagnostic tool. The F.D.A. regards ozone as having no “known useful medical application in specific, adjunctive, or preventive therapy.” The distribution of ozone machines for medical treatment is prohibited in this country—at least for now. Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., who currently leads the Department of Health and Human Services, is a champion of alternative treatments. (Rau told me that he approves of Kennedy’s push to “really evaluate vaccinations” and his efforts to study alternative techniques.)
In February, 2021, Rau, who had run a clinic in another Swiss town for more than twenty-five years, opened BioMed with Alexandra Kotthaus, a German doctor. Kotthaus, who is fifty, had been working with Rau for several years. She became interested in practicing alternative medicine because of a metabolic condition related to her liver. She also considers herself unusually sensitive to medication. “It took me a lot of time, power, and investigation to heal myself,” she told me.
That March, a seventy-year-old patient from a wealthy Cypriot family, Evie Lanitis, arrived at BioMed, complaining of a host of minor maladies, including bursitis in her toes, dental pain, and digestive issues. She was the sort of person who often finds her way to Rau. Many of his patients are well-to-do women. (Women are generally more likely than men to see doctors when they don’t feel well, and the gaps in research on how illnesses affect women leave an opening for alternative practitioners.) Alexandra Manglis, Lanitis’s daughter, told me that her mother “erred on the side of hypochondriac.” Lanitis told Kotthaus that she wanted to improve her stamina so that she could be a good grandmother—in three months, Manglis’s first child was due.
Lanitis’s treatment began, in part, with a yarrow compress placed over her abdomen, to soothe her liver. The next day, she got a ginger wrap for her kidney area. She was also prescribed the hyperthermia treatment, followed by a “restorative infusion” and then a colon cleanse. Rau wrote in Lanitis’s medical log, “Healthy! Really has mostly stomach symptoms and sleep problems.”
After a week or so at BioMed, Lanitis was given an I.V. infusion of alpha-lipoic acid. The substance has long been available as a pill; health-food stores often market it as an antioxidant supplement that can help alleviate nerve pain, among other benefits. In pill form, alpha-lipoic acid may ease symptoms, cause mild harm, or do nothing. But an intravenous dose reaches the bloodstream more thoroughly. In the U.S., the F.D.A. has not approved the use of alpha-lipoic acid as a treatment for any condition. Nevertheless, some alternative-medicine doctors give these infusions to their patients. This can be risky, in part because high-dose intravenous use of the acid has been associated with reduced levels of blood platelets, which play a key role in clotting.
Rau told me that he has given patients the infusion hundreds of times. But Lanitis began experiencing severe pain in her upper abdomen within a few hours of being administered the acid. She rested in her hotel room for a while, then managed to get downstairs to an examining room, where Rau tested her platelet count. He found it abnormally low. Within the next two hours, the count plummeted even further, to seventy-nine billion platelets per litre of blood. (Normal platelet counts vary from a hundred and fifty billion to four hundred billion per litre.) Rau and Kotthaus drove her to a hospital in the nearby town of Herisau. The doctors, seeing her platelet count, suspected liver failure and concluded that a better-equipped hospital should treat her. As her condition worsened, she was transferred by ambulance to a hospital in St. Gallen, the largest city in the area, where she threw up blood.
All the while, Lanitis was texting her sister in Cyprus, who was, in turn, updating Manglis and the rest of the family. As things turned worrisome, Manglis, who lives in Massachusetts, texted a sibling that she’d spoken with doctors at the St. Gallen hospital, who told her that BioMed had treated their mother with, as she put it, “some half-baked alternative shit.”
Link copied
Lanitis told her sister that her vomit was almost black—usually a sign of bleeding in the gastrointestinal tract. Manglis called the hospital and was put through to her mother, who described her sudden decline as “the strangest thing.” After they hung up, Manglis sent her mother a text telling her not to worry—both Lanitis and her future grandchild would be O.K. When a WhatsApp message reaches the recipient’s phone, two small ticks appear next to it on the sender’s screen. “That second little tick never appeared,” Manglis told me. During the crisis, Lanitis’s phone battery had apparently run down.
That night, the doctors in St. Gallen intubated Lanitis. She died the next day. Kotthaus was holding her hand as she passed away.
The Swiss have a long history of support for alternative medicine, and many of the clinics dedicated to it are based in Appenzell Ausserrhoden. In the early eighteenth century, the village of Gais started attracting swarms of visitors, many from abroad, who wanted to sample Gaisschottenkur, a treatment featuring goat’s whey. Locals claimed that drinking it was helpful for a variety of respiratory and digestive problems. One Herr Steinbrüchel, of Zurich, was said to have been miraculously cured of his chest disease, and before long preparations of Gaisschottenkur were being used to combat everything from dysentery to gonorrhea. A bustling spa in Gais accommodated four hundred visitors at a time. In 1791, a local pastor noted, of the inn’s restaurant, “One was allowed to sit with a sleeping cap, a nightgown, and slippers at the table.”
There was no proof that goat’s whey actually worked. In 1869, a German physician named Hermann Lebert lamented, “One may well ask whether an era can truly be considered enlightened when such unclear physical-chemical views on whey prevail among the thousands of doctors who prescribe.”
The development of the railroad helped to end the goat’s-whey craze. By the end of the nineteenth century, health tourists could easily travel to Davos, in the east of Switzerland, where the mountain vistas were more dramatic and newer treatments, such as chest rubs with marmot fat, awaited. Davos is the setting for Thomas Mann’s masterpiece “The Magic Mountain,” which centers on a sanatorium that caters to an international cohort of tuberculosis patients, in part through a daily regimen of extended sunbathing. In the book, the clinic has recently acquired an X-ray machine, and these “internal portraits,” Mann suggests, signal an epochal shift in medicine toward technological tools—and away from charismatic quacks. But spas in the vein of “The Magic Mountain” still dot the Alps, and they often attract people who aren’t satisfied with conventional methods of healing. Evie Lanitis, for instance, was devoted to Qigong, the traditional Chinese practice that combines movement, meditation, and intentional breathing.
Although Lanitis did not know it, a BioMed patient had unexpectedly died about a week before she arrived. Doris Ammann, who was in her mid-seventies and lived in Zurich, was a regular visitor to Rau’s clinics. Whereas Lanitis was just well-to-do, Ammann was enormously rich—she was a famous art dealer. After her death, her gallery sold Andy Warhol’s “Shot Sage Blue Marilyn” for a hundred and ninety-five million dollars, then the highest price ever paid for a twentieth-century work of art at auction.
Rau, who is an art aficionado, had met Ammann at Art Basel. “Doris was really important to me,” he recalled, in a hearing about her death. Ammann’s partner, Georg Frei, an art historian and dealer, later testified, “We went to the clinic happy and healthy, having meditated and swum together beforehand.” Frei said that Ammann underwent Rau’s treatments “not for an ailment but for prevention.”
When Ammann visited BioMed on March 18, 2021, however, she did have a physical complaint—a tremor on her left side. Rau wrote that she had “unclear neuropathy,” and that her lab results showed the presence of “neurotrophic viruses.” She had a colonic irrigation before joining Rau and Frei for a private vegan lunch. Afterward, Ammann got an alpha-lipoic-acid infusion—the same treatment that Lanitis would soon be given—followed by an infusion of mugwort.
At first, Ammann felt fine, but by the time she got into her car to return to Zurich, a few hours later, she had developed intense abdominal pain. Frei, in later testimony, remembered that, as Ammann’s chauffeur was loading bags into the car, Ammann began “screaming like crazy, like an animal, from all the pain.” Rau took her back into an examining room to try to pinpoint the source of her stomach discomfort. (One suspect was the lunch they had been served.) Kotthaus performed an ultrasound on Ammann, which did not appear abnormal. Rau gave her a small amount of morphine. She was also given an anti-spasmodic. At some point that night, Ammann vomited black fluid, Frei later testified. He recalled asking if they should go to the hospital, and Rau telling him that the hospital “always makes a fuss.” They decided that Ammann should remain at the hotel, where Rau could watch over her. (Rau told me that he would not have spoken that way, and that if he’d seen black vomit it would have been “an alarm sign.”)
Ammann slept through the night. In the morning, she told Rau that she wanted to go home, and he approved. Around this time, he noted in her patient log, “SEPSIS is possible, even though she is feeling much better now.” Ammann ate breakfast at the hotel and had a massage before Kotthaus did another ultrasound, which again wasn’t concerning. Ammann shook Rau’s hand, and her chauffeur drove her and Frei home.
But that afternoon she began vomiting again. Rau soon reviewed a blood-work report showing that Ammann had a low platelet count and a high white-blood-cell count—a possible sign of infection. He remembers suggesting to Frei that he take Ammann to a hospital but Frei saying that she didn’t want to go and was eager to sleep. (Frei testified that if he’d been told about her blood work he likely would’ve taken her to the hospital.) He had been waking Ammann up every hour and giving her some water. At around 3:30 a.m., he tried to check in with Rau, and then with Rau’s wife, Elizabeth—but nobody picked up. (He tried Rau four more times that night.) Just after 6 a.m., Frei texted Rau that Ammann was apathetic and confused. “She falls asleep again while drinking,” he wrote.
Not long afterward, Rau finally got in touch and told Frei to call an ambulance. She was admitted to University Hospital, in Zurich, where she died the next day. A postmortem report attributed her death to cardiovascular failure and septic shock.
Soon after the Ammann and Lanitis incidents, Appenzell Ausserrhoden prosecutors began investigating their deaths. But the clinic remained open for business, and reports of the inquiry did not go beyond Swiss news outlets. This meant that Rau’s foreign clientele—a significant portion of his business—had no idea that some of his methods had fallen under suspicion.
I first met Rau in 2024, at the “Future of Medicine” event in Brooklyn. It had been a pleasant evening—patients offered tributes to Rau while attendees enjoyed wine and vegan hors d’œuvres. It felt transgressive to ask him about the two deaths in front of so many admirers, but when I did so he did not miss a beat. “They came super sick to us,” he told me. “I sent them to the hospital, and then they died in the hospital.” He quickly clarified: “But not because of me—in spite of me.”
In a conversation the next day in an Upper East Side apartment—a patient was hosting him—Rau said that he could not talk about the specifics of the two cases because they were under investigation. He expressed confidence that he would be vindicated and urged me to visit him in Schwellbrunn to see him at work.
Rau’s clinic is about an hour east of Zurich. I travelled there in May, 2024. BioMed and the Sonnenberg Health Hotel lie just off the main street of Schwellbrunn, a hilltop village with about fifteen hundred residents. In town, an austere Reformed church is flanked by houses featuring the round shingles and steep gabled roofs typical of the region. Most of the area’s rolling hills remain pastureland, and during the day the loudest sound is the jangling of cowbells. In the distance looms Mt. Säntis, which, when I visited, was still topped with snow.
Switzerland makes a huge amount of money from rich foreigners seeking better health, and spas and alternative-healing resorts are often built to cater to voguish international tastes. But the Sonnenberg—a balconied building of four stories—is not a showy facility. When I visited, there were flowerpots and tables with umbrellas out front, and if you drove by you’d think you were passing one more comfortable hotel in a comfortable country. The only obvious sign of luxury was Rau’s silver Porsche 911 parked out front.
The Sonnenberg had been a medical facility long before Rau bought it. For decades, it was a “cure house”—a place where ill Swiss citizens could spend weeks, or months, getting fresh air and taking restorative walks. In Switzerland, someone with a cold might still address it by pouring a homeopathic liquid onto sugar cubes and then ingesting them. In a 2009 referendum, sixty-seven per cent of Swiss voters supported integrating nonconventional medicine into the country’s health-care system. But the practice of long rest cures had waned in Switzerland in the nineteen-nineties, in part because health insurers stopped fully covering extended stays at spas and wellness centers. Despite this shift, Rau, when buying the Sonnenberg, was confident that he had built a reputation strong enough to insure BioMed’s success. When the hotel and clinic opened, he had just turned seventy. “Technically, I shouldn’t be practicing anymore,” he told a Swiss radio station. “But this is an absolute passion for me—it’s my life.”
Although you can book a room in the Sonnenberg on the usual travel apps, it’s not a typical hotel. There’s a dedicated room for blood infusions on the main floor, and a pharmacy on the fifth. In 2022, a man from Hungary named András, who had stayed at the hotel, reported on Booking.com that “some of the patients in the ‘hotel’ has terminal illnesses—which is normal part of life, but if you are arriving to holiday with your family you might find it quite embarrassing.”
When I entered the lobby of the Sonnenberg, a woman from Palm Beach was chatting with the clinic receptionist, who sits next to the hotel receptionist, fretting over the timing of an appointment in which some of her teeth that had silver-amalgam fillings would be pulled out. (Dental amalgam contains mercury, and many alternative doctors believe that certain health problems, including chronic fatigue, can be caused by mercury “off-gassing.” The F.D.A. considers the removal of such fillings to be unnecessary.) The woman wanted the procedure done at the start of her stay, not near the end. She said, of Rau, “The original idea was I do this and then he treats me.”
A moment later, I saw someone I thought I knew—and in a sense I did. It was a very well-known American actress, dressed in loose clothes in preparation for a day of treatments. (She asked me not to use her name.) She told me that this was her eighth visit to see Rau, and that she has previously brought along her husband and her daughter.
The actress, who is in her seventies, said that she undergoes an annual “liver cleanse” with Rau. This involves five liver poultices (in which a castor-oil-soaked cloth is placed on the abdomen) and two colon-hydrotherapy sessions, and ends with “a strange night where you drink a combination of Epsom salts and grapefruit juice and, I think, olive oil.” The procedure leaves her better able to meet the demands of her gruelling schedule, she told me: “You really need a lot of stamina, and your body is your instrument.” She said, of the cleanse, “It’s about taking the toxic load off the liver so it can do its job, which is breaking down cells or rebuilding them.” (According to the American Liver Foundation, a liver cleanse is an unnecessary procedure.)
Soon afterward, Rau greeted me. With short gray hair and a trimmed beard, he looked like a kindly owl. As with doctors everywhere, he was a celebrity in his own facility—while we spoke, patients and nurses circled around him, trying to get his attention. We eventually moved into the dining room and had a delicious vegan lunch of lentil-cabbage-turnip soup and baked potatoes with leeks and mushrooms. He took off a white coat, revealing a Mr. Rogers-style cardigan, the cuffs folded back. Several patients were eating alone. Lunch is not a social meal at the hotel, as patients are often in the midst of treatments, many of which can be exhausting. Dinner is more convivial; patients plan trips to nearby towns or ascents of local hills together.
Link copied
Rau told me that he had started the day by treating a retired doctor who struggled with an autoimmune condition and had come to BioMed after “seeing forty-eight doctors.” He’d also met with a European academic who had salivary-gland cancer and had stopped traditional treatment. “He has no more pain,” Rau said, adding that the tumor was “really much smaller.” After lunch, Rau was seeing a woman from California with multiple myeloma. Next would be another American woman, whom Rau had named the “queen of colonics.” This woman had booked an appointment with him at the end of his workday. (She told me that she had chronic-fatigue syndrome and was also “slowly dying of heavy-metal poisoning.”)
Rau didn’t think that the criminal investigation had hurt his business. And it certainly hadn’t sapped his energy or made him doubt his skills. He recounted how he had turned to alternative medicine. He graduated from the University of Bern’s medical school in the late seventies, with a specialty in rheumatology. For a few years, he treated patients using conventional techniques, prescribing anti-inflammatory and anti-rheumatic drugs. “My patients did not get better,” he said, adding that he was the only doctor who seemed to notice. Soon he was integrating holistic medicine and investigating nonconventional methods, such as dark-field microscopy. Not that he particularly needed that tool these days, he said: “I feel from the patient if they have a toxic load.”
Rau likes to flip the logic of the phrase “alternative medicine.” In his view, what he practices at BioMed should be “the fundamental form of medicine,” and “only when that proves unsuccessful should conventional medical measures be employed.”
Lanitis’s and Ammann’s deaths were not Rau’s first brush with legal problems. In 2014, the Swiss national regulator, Swissmedic, determined that Rau’s former clinic, the Paracelsus, was distributing prohibited medications. “One day, unexpected, unannounced, the police came,” Rau told me. The clinic successfully filed an appeal. But, in 2003, Rau had been accused of something far more serious—being connected to the death of Prince Sadruddin Aga Khan, the seventy-year-old uncle of the Aga Khan, the Muslim spiritual leader. From 1966 to 1977, the Prince had been the High Commissioner for Refugees at the United Nations, and he had gone on to become an environmental activist.
Rau and the Prince were friends—alternative medicine seems to depend on personal relationships as much as the art world does. Sadruddin had survived mucinous colloid adenocarcinoma, an unusual form of breast cancer, and had become a devotee of Rau’s at the Paracelsus clinic. A local paper quoted him enthusing that its holistic treatments were the best he’d ever had; Sadruddin particularly appreciated the combination of familial hospitality and modern technology provided by Rau.
On one visit, Rau injected the Prince’s chest with a mixture of selenium and mistletoe extract, which he believes stimulates an anti-cancer response in the body. By the end of the day, Sadruddin was suffering from chest pain and a fever; he returned to Bellerive Castle, his residence in Geneva, in distress. He soon had a serious infection in his chest area. He died in Boston, at Massachusetts General Hospital, some nine months later.
An obituary in the Times stated that Sadruddin’s family members provided “no cause of death,” but they seem to have had no doubt about who was at fault. Sadruddin himself had clearly blamed Rau for the infection, which, he believed, originated with the subcutaneous chest injection. In a letter to Rau written in January, 2003, excerpts of which were eventually published in a Swiss newspaper, Sadruddin accused him of “brash arrogance and unprofessional behavior.”
Before the Prince died, he asked his family to sue Rau and the Paracelsus clinic. Two months after Sadruddin’s death, the family’s lawyer filed a complaint with the canton’s health authority, calling for an investigation into whether Rau had violated his duty of care. In another letter that the Swiss newspaper quoted from, the Prince explained that he hoped a lawsuit would enlighten “others who remain dependent on this disastrous individual.” The health authorities investigated the complaint. A former chief physician at the St. Gallen hospital presented an expert opinion stating that the Prince’s health crisis had begun with an infection that the Prince got under Rau’s care, adding that Rau had “regularly used medications that were not approved” and that it was ill-advised “to repeatedly breach the protective skin barrier and administer subcutaneous injections in an area of the body weakened by radiation therapy,” according to Swiss news reports. (In a recent conversation with me, the physician reiterated his assessment that, though the ultimate cause of the Prince’s death was uncertain, his infection was “very likely” caused by Rau’s injections.)
A prolonged legal battle followed. Rau filed a civil suit against Sadruddin’s family to clear his name, and blamed the Prince’s death on the doctors who had later treated him. Sadruddin’s family countersued, seeking about $1.4 million in damages. The family’s complaint eventually expired under the Swiss statute of limitations. When I asked Rau about the case, he told me that he still held the doctors who had seen Sadruddin after him responsible for Ammann’s death.
The day after my arrival, Rau’s son Felix, who is the C.E.O. of BioMed, took me for a tour. (Rau has four children. One son, Milo, is a well-known theatre director in Europe.) Felix told me that many of BioMed’s machines were originally developed to address the needs of high-level athletes. “We want to have the same benefit as sports medicine,” he explained.
On the first floor, there were private spaces for medical-massage therapists, who were doing acupuncture, cupping, lymphatic-drainage massages, and “everything else.” Elsewhere, a team of nurses administered detoxifying infusions. (Alpha-lipoic acid was no longer an option, I was told.)
I was astonished at the high-tech equipment at this otherwise unassuming facility. There was a large, pulsating magnetic-field-therapy machine. “You just put your head in here,” Felix explained. The device, he said, helps regulate “cell-membrane potential”—the difference in voltage between the interior and exterior of cells. In another room was a machine for intermittent hypoxic-hyperoxic training. You sat on a couch and put on a face mask that delivered fluctuating levels of oxygen. “You go up the mountain, and then after a few minutes you come down,” Felix said. “It’s like a mitochondria treatment.”
Next, we visited a red-light-therapy suite. There was a narrow bed and, above it, a row of light bulbs that emitted infrared beams. Its purpose, Felix explained, was to heal both physical and psychic wounds. It was one of the facility’s most requested treatments. Because each machine took up its own room, patients tended to spend a lot of time alone. This reminded me of a Swiss treatment for tuberculosis from the nineteenth century: patients were instructed to spend long stretches in a cowshed, to inhale ammonia from bovine urine.
Link copied
In a consulting room, Rau was seeing the patient from California. The setup was, at first glance, like any doctor’s: medical degrees on the wall, an examination table at the center. But I also noticed a small silver machine in a corner, called a Vegatest II, which measures changes in a patient’s electromagnetic fields, purportedly to ascertain sensitivity to certain toxins. It had knobs—one was marked “Provokation”—and a display with a needle that went from zero to one hundred, like an old-fashioned laugh-o-meter. Rau explained that he had just used the Vegatest II to investigate the harm done by toxins that had “leached” from the patient’s silver-amalgam fillings. In a 2018 documentary about the alleged dangers of root canals, “Root Cause,” Rau was quoted as saying that ninety-seven per cent of his breast-cancer patients between the ages of thirty and seventy had had “a root canal or toxic situation in the teeth.” (The film received fierce criticism from the American Dental Association, and Netflix, which had been streaming the documentary, pulled it.)
The Californian had short dark hair and carried a thermos from the luxury spa Canyon Ranch. I was invited in after she’d finished a colonic irrigation. (“I draw the line at that one,” she told me.) The patient, who was in her early fifties, told me that she’d developed multiple myeloma in her early forties. She’d had two bone-marrow transplants and was advised to undergo maintenance chemotherapy. She refused and instead began treatment under Rau, who focussed on ozone therapy and on removing gluten from her diet. Her multiple myeloma did not recur. “It sounds so kitschy,” she said. “But he’s basically saved me.” She now made a yearly visit to BioMed, where her regimen included multiple injections. “Only two today?” she asked, surprised. (She once received twenty-seven injections in a single session, she recalled.) She said, of one admixture, “They call it the tumor infusion—it’s specifically for people with cancer. Don’t ask me to tell you what’s in it. The ampules are always in German.”
Rau also gave her a dark-field-microscopy screening. “Look at all the rouleaux,” the Californian said, clearly upset. Rouleaux, in the vocabulary of dark-field microscopy, are blood cells that are stacked like pancakes. In theory, such an arrangement diminishes the cells’ ability to transport oxygen from the lungs. But Rau was preoccupied with another problem: images from the screening indicated that she was in a state of “hyper-proteinization.” The Californian was contrite. “We went to Italy for four days, and I wasn’t vegan or gluten-free,” she confessed. “But I’m back on it. I bet it will improve.”
“Look here—it’s not so bad,” Rau said, consolingly. “The cells, they move.”
On April 13, 2021—the day after Lanitis died—the Appenzell Ausserrhoden prosecutor’s office ordered the seizure of relevant records and called for an autopsy, which revealed that Lanitis had died of multiple organ failure.
Around this time, Kotthaus, Rau’s colleague, wrote to Lanitis’s daughter, Manglis, saying that she was “heartbroken.” Manglis asked what, exactly, had caused her mother’s organs to fail. Kotthaus answered that it was disseminated intravascular coagulation—a blood-clotting disorder. Manglis told me that her family secured a lawyer “pretty fast.”
The Lanitis family was warned that a toxicology report might be slow in arriving—the substances in Lanitis’s body were not the sort that the canton’s forensic scientists typically evaluated. After nearly a year, a report on Lanitis’s death was finally released to the family. It noted that the clinic had used alpha-lipoic acid in an off-label manner, and possibly without providing adequate information to the patient, which is required in Switzerland. By then, prosecutors had raised the possibility that the infusions had been contaminated. They were sent off for special testing at the hospital in St. Gallen. Nothing was found. This meant that the pharmacist who had prepared the I.V.s was not at fault—and that Rau and Kotthaus remained the main figures of judicial interest.
Manglis had started Googling Rau to see if he had a history of problematic outcomes. She eventually found Swiss newspaper articles, in German, about Sadruddin Aga Khan. Suddenly, Manglis said, she was confronting “the possibility that this doctor has been losing patients for a long time under suspicious circumstances.” She added, “I felt like I bore some responsibility for not vetting my mother’s medical choices.”
Before the two doctors were scheduled to testify in the inquiry, the prosecutor’s office called in a woman named Vera Torosian, who had been a naturopath at BioMed during the period in question. Torosian had been working in the United States, and Rau hired her in the hope that she would attract American patients to the clinic. She had no animus toward Rau, she testified, but from the beginning she was skeptical of many of the techniques she observed at BioMed. For instance, she saw patients being sent to the hyperthermia machine even though they seemed too weak to endure extreme heat. A few days after Lanitis’s death, Torosian called the police. (In her testimony, she recalled struggling “for a long time beforehand about whether to do it.”) She was directed to the prosecutor’s office, where she laid out her concerns—including an incident that she found alarming. Torosian, who left BioMed soon afterward, claimed that she’d seen a Swiss patient in the waiting room who was so weak that she could barely stand. The woman had been getting various treatments, including repeated sessions with a papimi—a device, not approved by the F.D.A., that applies high-intensity, short-duration electromagnetic pulses to the body. The woman ultimately died of advanced cancer.
One patient that Torosian had introduced to Rau was a friend named Mary Anne Hawrylak, an American who owned a medical-services company. Hawrylak was not a hard-core believer in alternative medicine, but she prided herself on keeping an open mind. She had suffered from an excruciating autoimmune disorder decades earlier, and she wanted to take steps to prevent its recurrence. “I’m interested in things beyond standard of care, because standard of care doesn’t always address things beyond just the symptoms,” she told me. After discussing her condition with Rau, she signed up for a three-week stay at BioMed.
Her visit overlapped with Lanitis’s, and they became friendly. They had meals together and chatted about their children and grandchildren. They planned an excursion to St. Gallen.
On April 9th, a few hours before Lanitis received her infusion, Hawrylak herself underwent an alpha-lipoic-acid treatment. When I asked her about the various infusions she received at BioMed, she said that she had asked Rau about them, and that he had said very little. “He never told me the ingredients,” she said. “And, had he told me the ingredients, I might not have known what that meant, anyway, unless he said, ‘Here are the potential side effects.’ ” (Rau says that all of his patients are given consent forms.)
After receiving the alpha-lipoic acid, Hawrylak felt confused and dizzy. She went to her hotel room to lie down. “Honestly, I thought I was going to die,” Hawrylak remembered. Torosian encouraged her to go to the hospital, but Hawrylak refused: COVID rules were in force, and Hawrylak, who doesn’t speak German, was scared that she would have to enter the emergency room alone. A while later, Rau tested Hawrylak’s platelets and found that they were slightly reduced.
In the meantime, Lanitis had fallen seriously ill. After Jeff Höseler, the hotel manager, told Hawrylak that her friend had been taken to the hospital, Hawrylak asked Rau, “Is she going to die?” Rau said that it didn’t look good.
Rau, wondering if his patients had been poisoned somehow, had his staff check the kitchen for contamination. He went to the infusion room and found the used ampules of alpha-lipoic acid in a biohazard-waste container. He tested some remaining drops and found no problems. Rau wondered whether the women’s COVID vaccines had caused them to have adverse reactions to the acid. A month before Lanitis died, she had received the AstraZeneca vaccine, which has been linked to problems with blood clotting at a rate of one case in every hundred thousand. Hawrylak, however, had been given the Pfizer vaccine, which has not been strongly implicated in any blood-clotting issues.
Rau called the hotel’s guests to a wood-panelled library where copies of “The Swiss Secret” were on display. He announced that a patient had been given an infusion and subsequently died. There would be no more such treatments until he could rule out the infusion as the cause. The guests seemed less upset by the death than by the news that their infusions would be halted. Hawrylak remembers their response as “We paid so much money—we want these infusions.” She told me, “I thought I was in ‘The Twilight Zone.’ ”
Hawrylak was shocked when Rau told the guests that other patients had been given the same treatment and had not been harmed. “I looked at him and he gave me a weird look,” she told me. (Rau disputes this.) She was so angry that she took a walk in the snow to gather her emotions.
Hawrylak soon left. She said goodbye to another friend she’d made at BioMed, a British woman with cancer. She did not warn the woman that Rau was untrustworthy. “I didn’t want to talk to her about it, because she had just complete faith in this,” Hawrylak told me. “And the last thing I want to do is shake somebody’s faith.”
Torosian’s testimony stunned Manglis and her family. “It’s the first time I’m understanding the extent of what’s happening,” Manglis told me. She now knew that her mother and Hawrylak had been given the same infusion on the same day and that both had appeared to have had strong adverse reactions. A possible reason that Hawrylak had survived was that she was physically larger than Manglis’s mother, who was petite. The earlier death of Ammann—who weighed little more than a hundred pounds upon her final arrival at BioMed—was consistent with this hypothesis.
Gossip was swirling at the clinic. Torosian had told the prosecutor’s office that an undernourished, severely disabled girl who was receiving treatment at BioMed was subsisting entirely on spinach—on Kotthaus’s orders. After learning of this and other allegations, Rau and Kotthaus each sued Torosian for defamation. (They lost these suits, but Kotthaus told me that the accusation about the disabled girl was “bullshit.”)
Eventually, Höseler was contacted by the police, and afterward he began exchanging texts on WhatsApp with Zacharie Tachi, an employee at the hotel’s restaurant.
“This is getting tight for Dr. Rau,” Tachi texted.
“I can’t really remember who all died, names and number of deceased, do you still have them in your head?” Höseler wrote.
Tachi said that he couldn’t remember any names.
Höseler speculated about several such deaths. There was “the Zurich woman”—Ammann. “The lady from Cyprus”—Lanitis. A “lady from Germany.” A “lady from Slovenia.” Rau told me that these texts reflected medical ignorance: the last two women had come to him terminally ill. (The prosecutor’s office opened a separate investigation into allegations of mistreatment and additional deaths at the clinic and identified no criminal misconduct. The case against Rau has remained limited to Lanitis and Ammann.)
Like Torosian, Höseler left his job, and he later shared his texts and other evidence with prosecutors. The restaurant in the Sonnenberg, he told them, was meant to be a place of “joie de vivre.” But Rau’s arrangement was “a money-making machine with the potential to save lives but not to bring joy.”
In May, 2023, the prosecutors finally interrogated Kotthaus and Rau. Kotthaus attended the hearing but declined to answer most questions. Rau spoke more freely. He said that he didn’t remember much about Lanitis—Kotthaus had been the doctor in charge and had done most of the interfacing. But, to indicate how seriously he had taken the incident, he noted that this was “the first time in my life” that he had driven a patient to a hospital himself. He did not deny that Lanitis’s death might have been related to the alpha-lipoic-acid infusion or to other treatments she’d received, though he cautioned that “the causal connection seems to have been drawn a bit too hastily.”
On November 18, 2023, the Sunday edition of the Swiss newspaper Neue Zürcher Zeitung published an investigation into the deaths at BioMed, written by a reporter named Leo Eiholzer. It laid the blame mostly on Rau, who brought legal action against the paper, demanding to be allowed to publish a rebuttal in its pages. (A judge denied his suit.) After the article ran, canton officials invited Hawrylak to fly to Switzerland and recount her experience to prosecutors.
Rau, in his first round of testimony, seemed to acknowledge having used alpha-lipoic acid off-label. And he entertained the notion that he should more explicitly inform patients about the potential risks of such treatments. “That has nothing to do with the causality with the tragical cases,” he said. “But I can see the point.” In his second round of testimony, he took a tougher tack. He insisted that the infusion was completely appropriate. Lanitis’s toe bursitis had been accompanied by “subtle neurological problems,” and to him this seemed to be “a classic indication for the infusion.” He said, “It’s too narrow to say that just because she hasn’t had diabetes it’s not indicated.”
Later in this testimony, Rau claimed that it was unreasonable to expect him to inform patients “about complications that practically never occur.” All the same, he emphasized, he did take his duty of care very seriously, especially when using medicines off-label. Indeed, he had personally reported Lanitis’s death to the canton. “If I had wanted to cover something up, I certainly wouldn’t have done that,” he said.
The prosecutor then turned to the death of Ammann. Both sides dug in hard here. The postmortem report, which was conducted by an expert at the Zurich hospital, found that Rau had mishandled Ammann’s case from the start. He had missed clear signs of intestinal paralysis, which can lead to sepsis, and had failed to respond to severe gastrointestinal bleeding, which can occur in someone with an abnormally low platelet count. Rau should have sent her to a surgical clinic; cases like hers have “an excellent success rate and survival prognosis.” The report called BioMed’s diagnostic efforts “incomprehensible.” It concluded that Rau had breached “the doctor’s duty of care.” (Rau disputes this report and commissioned a counter-expert opinion that challenged the report’s assessment about the cause of Ammann’s death.)
Rau called the allegation that he’d missed an intestinal issue “nonsense”—the day after he gave Ammann the infusion, he’d placed a stethoscope on her abdomen and heard normal digestive activity, and he was further reassured when she ate and didn’t vomit. In addition, he said that Ammann had nerve inflammation, which in his view was more or less peripheral neuropathy—a condition for which alpha-lipoic acid is approved in Germany. The question of whether the infusion caused Ammann’s death was more complex than in Lanitis’s case, because none of the acid remained in Ammann’s blood at the time of her death, nearly three days after the treatment. (Melissa Gittinger, a medical toxicologist, told me that the substance passes through the system “in hours.”) But the fact that Ammann’s platelet count, like Lanitis’s and Hawrylak’s, had been impacted after the alpha-lipoic- acid infusion suggested that Ammann, too, might have had an adverse response.
Rau testified, of Ammann’s case, “In our opinion, this death is still completely unclear.” His late friend, he said, had had various problems with her inner milieu, including a history of “heavy-metal contaminations.” Rau said that he doubted Ammann had screamed in pain—the Doris he knew was too suave for such an outburst. The prosecutor later asked him why her partner would make such a detail up. Rau couldn’t say.
As to why it had taken so long for Ammann to be sent to a hospital, Rau said, it was in part because she was famous in Switzerland and shy of publicity. Moreover, he added, “trying to prove whether time was lost here is just a matter of finding someone to blame.” At one point, he protested, “I’m a good internist.” The charge of “negligence,” he went on, “hurts me. I’m not a negligent doctor. That’s why I have many patients from all over the world.”
In January, 2025, Rau gave prosecutors a written defense of his actions. He insisted that the Swiss woman whom Torosian had found in the waiting room had not been mistreated, pointing out that her family had thanked him in the woman’s obituary in the St. Gallen newspaper. The woman, whose family did not want her to be named, had died of advanced cancer. But, when I reached the woman’s husband, he said that Rau “didn’t act as a doctor but as a business partner.” Although he said that Rau had given his wife a good final month, he wondered if she might have lived longer if she hadn’t left standard care: “She simply blocked conventional medicine, and that’s actually Rau’s business model—he grabs her and says, ‘Yes, come to me.’ ”
Rau told me with pride that many of his patients came back more than once—he affectionately called them “repeaters.” Manglis saw this differently. “Part of the grift is to keep you coming back,” she said. But others I spoke with insisted that Rau was a master diagnostician. Xenia Lehner, a nursing specialist in St. Gallen who worked at BioMed for a year, and at Paracelsus before that, wrote to me, of Rau, “Both he and Dr. Kotthaus were providing much care and quality in their treatment—probably the main reason why patients follow them.”
In October, 2022, Ammann’s sisters filed a criminal complaint accusing Georg Frei of failing to get her to a hospital promptly. “This situation should have filled a life partner with great fear,” it said. Rau’s lawyer has noted that Frei stood to inherit two properties and nearly fifty million dollars upon Ammann’s death. This past March, a prosecutor dismissed the complaint, finding that Frei had acted in a sufficiently “timely manner” and had taken “the measures that could reasonably be expected of him.”
When Torosian, the naturopath, testified for a second time, in February, 2025, Rau’s attorney made a shocking insinuation. After questioning Torosian about an F.B.I. course in criminal investigative analysis she had taken, he implied that she might have poisoned the victims with arsenic. This stratagem went nowhere: when he asked her to confirm that a person with her expertise would know how to disguise arsenic poisoning, there was “tumult” in the courtroom and the question was declared inadmissible. Moreover, Torosian claimed that she had never administered any substances at BioMed. All the same, prosecutors requested an expert report on arsenic poisoning, which caused further delay.
This past March, the Appenzell Ausserrhoden prosecutors finally charged Rau and Kotthaus with two counts of negligent homicide and sent the case to a judge for a trial. In Switzerland, a trial is a much smaller, more compact affair than in the United States. The proceeding often takes place in a single day. But it has still not been scheduled and may not happen for many months.
Earlier this year, after the prosecutor signalled that he intended to stop collecting evidence and file charges, Rau’s lawyer demanded that the prosecutor recuse himself, claiming that he was “trampling on Dr. Rau’s right to evidence.” Rau plans to submit his counter-expert report on Ammann’s death for the trial, and continues to plead his case—he recently produced an annotated hundred-and-fifty-page chronology of events relating to her case, in which he describes her having various maladies, including a hormonal imbalance and chronic inflammatory-bowel disease. He also sent me a shorter document about the death of Lanitis, saying that he didn’t know why she died and calling for the case to “be assessed by a truly independent, interdisciplinary expert review.”
The prosecutor has asked for a suspended fourteen-month prison sentence for Rau and a ten-month one for Kotthaus. A licensed psychiatrist, Kotthaus has started her own practice in the nearby town of Herisau, focussing on “body/mind” medicine. Kotthaus feels that she and Rau are being scapegoated for practicing alternative medicine: “The things the emergency-room doctors wrote down in Herisau, in St. Gallen . . . it’s, like, prejudiced. Since we don’t do mainstream medicine, we do harm. We do not know what we are doing.”
Whatever happens in court, the canton could try to prohibit the two doctors from practicing medicine. But Rau is an eminent figure in the area, and Andreas Dudli, a lawyer for the Lanitis family, told me, “I’m not sure if they have the courage to take away his license in this tiny canton.” Rau’s lawyer told me he was prepared to appeal the case all the way to the European Court of Human Rights.
Manglis hopes for a guilty verdict, in part, to warn patients away from BioMed. She said recently, “I think a lot about how, when I spoke to my mother the day before she died, she was so confused about what was happening to her.” It still made no sense to her that her mother’s visit to a serene alpine retreat had ended in painful sudden death. “It feels like I’m often occupying that same state—the state of ‘What the fuck happened?’ ” ♦

70,2026-10-26,"Strategy made no Bitcoin purchases or sales last week, as its STRC preferred stock buybacks reduced its USD Cash balance by 46% to $242.4 million.
Michael Saylor’s Strategy refrained from buying or selling Bitcoin last week, choosing instead to spend $204.9 million on STRC preferred stock buybacks.
Strategy repurchased 2.07 million shares between Monday and Friday, reporting no Bitcoin (BTC) transactions or stock sales through its at-the-market offering program, according to its Monday filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission.
The company said its Bitcoin holdings remained at 848,517 BTC, acquired for $64.02 billion at an average price of $75,446 per coin. At Monday’s US equities open, the biggest cryptocurrency was trading hands at roughly $79,992 apiece.
Strategy’s US dollar reserve remained unchanged from the previous week, at $4.83 billion, Its USD Cash balance fell around 46% to $242.4 million from $447.3 million after management used $204.9 million to repurchase STRC shares.
Since October began, Strategy has spent about $664 million repurchasing STRC preferred shares, compared with $72.3 million buying 851 BTC.
The pause came as Bitcoin fell toward $80,000 last week after reaching $84,777 on Wednesday, according to CoinGecko.
Strategy’s common stock (MSTR) fell 2.2% to $142.80 in Monday’s premarket activity, while its STRC preferred shares edged down 0.01% to $99.35, according to Yahoo Finance.

71,2026-10-26,"Let’s look at the Sigma 50mm f/1.4 CL DG Contemporary Lens, launching as the signature companion lens for the new Sigma FP2 camera.
Sigma 50mm f/1.4 CL DG Contemporary Lens
In the old days of video, calling something a “stock lens” was almost an insult. That never made too much sense to me. Like, how dare you use a lens designed to work best with your camera? These days most people understand the pros and cons of the lenses that most companies choose to package or market with their new cameras.
To that end, we’re looking at the new 50mm f/1.4 CL DG Contemporary Lens from Sigma, which could be the ideal partner for the new Sigma FP2—which we covered last week. Slightly smaller than the original FP, yet packing more power and serious punch with its 6K Open Gate video recording, the FP2 could use a fast, rich, and expensive 50mm partner. So, let’s take a look.

            
                The Sigma 50mm f/1.4 CL DG Contemporary Lens
            
            
        While it wasn't initially available with the FP2 at launch, this new Sigma 50mm f/1.4 CL DG Contemporary Lens is likely to be the signature partner for Sigma’s new signature camera. Designed to be a premium compact prime that is set to deliver a bright f/1.4 aperture and rich textural rendering in a small and lightweight metal body, this lens will be housed in a barrel that is very small by today’s mirrorless lens standards, but—like the FP2—it will pack a surprising punch.What’s most notable about this Sigma 50mm f/1.4 CL DG Contemporary Lens is that its aberrations are intentionally controlled with the latest simulation technology to soften texture and help subjects stand out.These simulations should also help the lens achieve gentle softness within the in-focus plane, smooth and attractive bokeh, and characterful background rendering that conveys warmth and a natural sense of three-dimensionality across the entire frame.

72,2026-10-26,"A judge ruled Monday that Maurene Comey was wrongfully terminated from her position as the former assistant U.S. attorney for the Southern District of New York, ordering the Justice Department to reinstate her with back pay.
Comey, the daughter of former FBI Director James Comey, was fired in July 2025. The DOJ did not provide a rationale for her ouster at the time, simply citing Donald Trump’s authority to do so as the sole justification.
In a federal lawsuit, Comey argued that her firing was part of the president’s political retribution campaign against her father, who testified before Congress during Trump’s first term regarding the federal investigation into Russian interference in the 2016 election.
In a 55-page opinion, U.S. District Judge Jesse M. Furman declared that Comey’s termination was not only unfounded, but it was also illegal as it fundamentally violated the Civil Service Reform Act of 1978.
The government’s other defense, which cited Article 2 of the Constitution barring Congress from regulating the removal of assistant U.S. attorneys, was similarly fraught, as it “flies in the face of nearly two centuries of Supreme Court precedent,” according to Furman.
“Indeed, to accept Defendants’ argument would be to unsettle the very equilibrium established by this Nation’s constitutional system,” Furman wrote.
Comey was involved in several high-profile cases prior to her firing, including prosecutions against musician Sean Combs (also known as P. Diddy) and child sex trafficker Jeffrey Epstein. She is expected to receive back pay dating from the time she was fired to December 2025, when she obtained a new job in private practice.
Trump has been very public about his contempt for James Comey. The president fired Comey during his first term in the midst of the Trump-Russia probe, prematurely ending the director’s 10-year term. His decision to do so prompted a follow-up investigation by Comey’s predecessor, special counsel Robert Mueller, who determined that Russian influence in the 2016 election was “sweeping and systemic” and found substantial (if non-prosecutable) evidence that Trump had obstructed justice by firing Comey.
In April, a grand jury in the Eastern District of North Carolina indicted Comey on two felony charges relating to a seaside photo the former FBI director shared on Instagram that depicted seashells in an arrangement reading: “86 47.” Eighty-six is a popular phrase used in the restaurant industry to signify that an item has been crossed off the menu.
Yet the Department of Justice claimed that the message would be interpreted by any person “familiar with the circumstances” as a “serious expression of an intent to do harm to the president,” according to Comey’s indictment.
Whether or not the government’s case against the elder Comey will pan out remains to be seen. However, recent revelations regarding Trump’s personal involvement in the initial investigation could unravel the suit.

President Donald Trump can’t stop talking about other people’s posteriors during his midterm campaign blitz.  
At a rally in Port Huron, Michigan, Saturday, Trump continued to rave about Ohio Senator Jon Husted’s buttocks. 
“I looked at his ass, and I’ve never done that with a man before,” Trump said. “Off the record, women yes, men no.”
Then Trump decided to ogle someone who was actually in the state instead.
“By the way I looked at one hell of a beautiful ass before: Nicki Minaj!” Trump said, as the crowd cheered. “That’s hard to miss! That’s—stand up! That’s hard to miss!”
TRUMP: I looked at his ass, and I've never done that with a man before. By the way, I looked at one hell of a beautiful ass before: Nicki Minaj. That's hard to miss. https://t.co/LPLeZlL4L3
The rapper-singer joined Trump onstage, telling the president that she’d support him if he ran a third time. It’s not clear what any of this had to do with Michigan’s Senate race. Trump did eventually turn his attention to making wildly Islamophobic remarks against Democratic candidate Abdul El-Sayed, calling him a “jihadist” who was “on the side of the terrorists.”
The president’s frequent fantasizing about other people’s bodies has become a mainstay of his weird political rallies, which have started to feel more like a farewell tour than a campaign blitz. Even if Trump isn’t impeached following the midterm elections, it’s not looking likely that the 80-year-old president will hit the campaign trail again. 
Two Catholic bishops have expressed intense regret at their recent photo op with President Trump, calling the widely circulated images of their White House meeting an exploitative act that was “publicized by the administration without the consent of the participants.”
“The meeting was in no way an endorsement of the president, his policies, or his party.… We were told the meeting was to be a private discussion, that several Catholic members of the Cabinet might make an appearance, and that there could be an opportunity to pray privately with the president,” Milwaukee Archbishop Jeffrey Grob wrote Sunday in a statement titled “A Public Apology.”
“We were then directed to stand behind him and pray. After which several photos were taken and we were ushered out. Unbeknownst to the group of bishops a video was also taken. But permission for the public distribution or posting of either the video or photos was not given by the bishops,” Grob continued. “It did not take long thereafter for the White House to post a video and photos of the prayer. I soon began receiving messages of serious concern over the possibility that I had inadvertently participated in a political event less than two weeks before a national election. I also heard from some who felt wounded because of the ways they or their family had been harmed by the president’s policies.”
Iowa Bishop William Joensen expressed similar sentiments.
“I wish I had been more shrewd and less simple, like those who exploited our meeting for partisan political purpose,” he wrote. “I pray I am more prudent and wise going forward, even as I remain resolute in my openness to engaging and praying for political leaders at all levels.”
Catholic Archbishops and Bishops pray with President Trump in the Oval Office ❤️ https://t.co/facV5wTGQQ
The White House meeting was organized by the ultraconservative Heritage Foundation. And Trump is about as pious as his policies (not very). This made the images from the meeting and the shady nature in which it unfolded all the more questionable—and regrettable—for some of the clergymen involved.
“I feel a heavy sadness, not only over the pain I caused, but also for the fact that something so sacred to a group of religious leaders, prayer to our Heavenly Father, would be manipulated in that way,” Grob continued. “We came in good faith to try to work for the betterment of our fellow human beings, to ease their suffering, to build up the common good, and to pray with a president, not to pose for a political photo opportunity to benefit the few. We should never have been put in that position by the White House.”
Anthropic’s Claude got around a paywall to access Georgia voters’ data without human authorization.
Anthropic confirmed to Axios Sunday that the company’s artificial intelligence model had, without prompting, requested a digital entry pass, or “access token,” to collect aggregate vote counts for a statistics project.
“Claude did not access the state’s voter registration system, change any voting records, or pull any individual voter records,” Anthropic said, adding that it had placed guardrails to prevent the chatbot from taking these sorts of actions in the future. 
The company first notified the Georgia secretary of state’s office and the White House three weeks ago about the spontaneous access. Trump administration officials urged the state office to disclose the incident, but those calls went unheeded, a source told Axios. 
This revelation comes as Anthropic unveiled its Initial Public Offering filing Monday, ahead of what is expected to be one of the largest stock debuts in history at an estimated value of more than $2 trillion. It also comes weeks after one of its researchers warned that the company’s product could cause a mass extinction event.
This is just the latest instance of an AI company being forced to disclose its model’s unauthorized behavior. 
Democrats just dropped a heavy hammer on the president, eight days out from Election Day.
House Judiciary Committee Democrats released a 50-page report Monday morning accusing Donald Trump of participating in Jeffrey Epstein’s criminal sex schemes. The caucus also accused the Trump administration of a broad cover-up, designed to shield the president from blowback as it tried to roll out the release of the Epstein files to the general public.
The report cites the experiences of 11 women, FBI records, and documents released through the Epstein Files Transparency Act. At least five women accused Trump of rape or sexual assault in Epstein’s company, according to interviews conducted by the committee. Others said there were “apparent attempts” by Epstein and his longtime girlfriend and associate Ghislaine Maxwell to traffic them to the real estate magnate.
Beatrice Keul told lawmakers that Trump “violently raped” her at a November 1993 pageant at the Plaza Hotel. She further described the beauty pageants as a “hunting ground” that Epstein and Trump would use to find and “prey” on young women and girls. Jill Harth also shared an account of a party at Mar-a-Lago in 1994, where she said Trump aggressively pursued and sexually assaulted her. Trump and Epstein were the only men present, Harth told lawmakers.
In total, at least 28 women have accused Trump of rape or sexual misconduct.
The report concludes that Trump and Paolo Zampolli, a former modeling agent and longtime friend of the president as well as a former senior Trump administration official, “may have been co-conspirators” in Epstein’s sex trafficking ring in the 1990s.
The report also claims that Trump’s Justice Department deliberately or prematurely shut down investigations into Trump-related allegations made by some Epstein survivors. It also accuses Attorney General Todd Blanche of specifically instructing Maxwell not to discuss a survivor whose account implicated the president.
“Trump’s Justice Department appears to be covering up these crimes to protect the President,” said Judiciary Committee ranking member Jamie Raskin in a statement. “The women who survived Epstein deserve to be heard, Congress must have the complete files required by law, and an independent special counsel must investigate Epstein’s co-conspirators and DOJ’s cover-up and ensure that state and foreign prosecutors have the evidence they need to pursue justice.”
Last week, reports emerged that White House officials were involved in drafting the infamous July 2025 FBI-DOJ memo that effectively shut down future Epstein-related investigations. The memo created explosive public backlash even among MAGA supporters for declaring at the time that there was no Epstein “client list,” no punishable co-conspirators to the pedophile’s crimes, and no grounds for further investigation."

76,2026-10-26,"The first few weeks of the season always see dramatic moves in the NBA Power Rankings. Some teams are better than we thought, others are worse, but mostly it’s small sample size theater — if the Knicks dropped three straight games in January, the NBA would just shrug.
Still, we have a fair amount of ground to cover in our first edition of Stock Up, Stock Down, looking at the teams that are the most dramatic risers and fallers in the NBC Sports NBA Power Rankings. Here are the teams to keep an eye on.

• The Oklahoma City Thunder move up two spots to take over No. 1, although the gruesome leg injury to Jalen Williams has put a dark cloud over any celebrations of the team’s fast start.
• The Detroit Pistons and Houston Rockets each move up four spots to take over the No. 2 and No. 3 spots in the power rankings.
• The Minnesota Timberwolves jumped up seven spots from outside the top-10 to No. 4. We knew their LaMelo Ball and Anthony Edwards offense would be good, but the defense has been near the league’s best to start the season.
• The Toronto Raptors jumped up seven spots to be No. 7 in this week’s rankings, another team with an elite defense — when Kawhi Leonard is healthy.
• The Memphis Grizzlies jumped up eight spots to No. 17 after an unexpected 3-0 start, behind the equally surprising but unstoppable Ty Jerome, who is averaging 34.7 points a game so far.
• Shout out to the Charlotte Hornets, which climbed seven spots up to No. 19 behind a fast start from Brandon Miller, while still missing some key players (such as Kon Knueppel).
• Nobody’s stock fell faster than the New York Knicks, who fell a dozen spots from No. 1 all the way down to 13th. Losing to East contenders such as Philadelphia and Boston is one thing, but losing to a Banchero-less Orlando is another. Physicality and shooting seem to have gone missing in NYC, but nobody there is panicking. Yet.
• The winless Miami Heat fell 10 spots down to 23rd in the latest power rankings. The Heat have trailed by at least 20 points in every game, and the defense that was supposed to anchor them is bottom-five in the league. There might be a little panic in South Beach.
• The San Antonio Spurs fell six spots to No. 8, and Victor Wembanyama has looked almost human to start the season. Expect that to change.

77,2026-10-26,"Bitcoin BTC$84,459.46 is no longer a fringe asset but one with growing acceptance on Wall Street. Yet those tracking blockchain data still can't link most BTC to a known owner.
About 11.1 million BTC, roughly 55% of the supply, was classified as unattributed in Galaxy’s October snapshot. Those coins could belong to individuals, institutions the firm hasn’t identified or holders who can no longer access them.
Galaxy’s head of research, Alex Thorn, sees the figure as evidence that self-custody remains widespread, with people keeping control of their bitcoin keys despite the growth of exchanges, funds and institutional custodians.
""More than half of all BTC supply is sitting in addresses with no known attribution despite all the institutionalization and centralized ownership over the years, still a ton of bitcoin (maybe even most) is held by individuals in self-custody,"" Thorn wrote on X.
Self-custody means holding your own bitcoin keys rather than leaving coins with an exchange or a fund.
Xapo Bank, a bitcoin-focused bank, replied to Thorn's post, saying that large number of coins held in unnamed wallets is a sign that retail investors have been here for over a decade. Bitcoin is often seen as the first market that grew from the bottom up: Retail investors began trading, and institutions then embraced it.
""And why bitcoin is for the people and people 'can be their own bank' Retail have been here for over a decade. Everyone else still catching up,"" the bank wrote.
BTC is the native token of the Bitcoin blockchain, a public ledger that lets anyone, anywhere, send and receive money. Miners verify these transactions and add them to the ledger, earning newly issued BTC as a reward. The setup is often held up as an example a perfectly competitive market.
A bitcoin address is like a bank account number on bitcoin's public ledger. Anyone can see how many coins sit at an address, while hiding the identity of the owner. Research firms like Galaxy try to put names to addresses by tracking wallets known to belong to exchanges, funds and other big holders.
The entire unnamed 55% stack doesn't necessarily belong to individuals holding their own coins for the long term.
It could include coins that are likely lost, sitting in wallets whose owners have forgotten their keys or have passed away. 
Some were mined in bitcoin's early years by miners other than the one behind the Patoshi pattern, a set of bitcoin's earliest mined blocks that researchers link to a single miner, bitcoin's anonymous creator, Satoshi Nakamoto. 
Some could belong to institutions Galaxy hasn't identified, such as funds, companies and OTC desks, which are brokers that arrange large bitcoin trades privately, away from exchanges. Some coins could belong to custody setups that spread coins across many addresses, making them harder to trace.
The other 45% does have names attached, and some of them are big names, including exchanges, ETFs led by BlackRock, the U.S. government and the single biggest holder, ""Satoshi Nakamoto (Patoshi Pattern),"" at 1,123,658 BTC. Galaxy counts those coins separately, as ""the Satoshi-era miner pattern.""
Still, for all the ETF headlines, the largest slice of bitcoin is the one nobody can name.
Diversified RWA stablecoins sustain 5-7% yield from real credit as crypto funding compresses to ~4%. GENIUS pushes yield off-chain; TAM grows to $4B in 3 years.

S&P Dow Jones Indices has partnered with prediction market platform Kalshi in a deal that will allow the exchange to offer event contracts tied to the financial index provider’s benchmarks.
According to Monday’s announcement, Kalshi will become a preferred platform for contracts linked to S&P DJI indices, with settlement based on official index values published by the company.
S&P DJI will also explore developing indexes using Kalshi’s prediction market data, potentially tracking expectations around economic developments, political outcomes and sporting events. The gauges would use prices from Kalshi’s event contracts to measure market expectations about future outcomes, rather than tracking the performance of traditional financial assets.
The companies did not disclose a timeline for either initiative. S&P DJI did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for additional details.
Related: Utah AG sues Kalshi, pursues profits from prediction market company
The agreement follows moves by traditional financial market operators into prediction markets. Intercontinental Exchange, parent of the New York Stock Exchange, committed up to $2 billion to Polymarket in October 2025. 
In March, Nasdaq filed to offer prediction market-style contracts tied to the Nasdaq 100 Index, before partnering with Kalshi in August to provide market surveillance technology.
S&P Dow Jones Indices parent, S&P Global, separately announced Monday that it completed its acquisition of blockchain security firm OpenZeppelin, expanding its risk assessment capabilities for digital assets. The deal was first announced in September and financial terms were not disclosed.
OpenZeppelin provides smart contract security audits and development tools. Its open-source contract libraries underpin more than $38 trillion in value transferred, while its security team has conducted over 900 engagements, according to S&P Global.
OpenZeppelin will continue operating as its own business unit under founder Demian Brener, who will report to Yann Le Pallec, president of S&P Global Ratings.
S&P Global has also expanded its digital asset risk assessment work beyond smart contract security. Earlier this month, S&P Global Ratings launched a framework for evaluating crypto lending vaults across six risk categories, including blockchain security, liquidity and governance.

There’s plenty of media portraying stepmothers as monsters. They’re cruel creatures who refuse to accept their partner’s children and go out of their way to make their lives miserable. 

In reality, of course, this usually isn’t the case. But one person who did have a stepmother from hell reached out to the internet to share their story. Below, you’ll find all of the details that they posted on Reddit explaining why their father finally filed for divorce, as well as some of the replies invested readers left them. 

cottonbro studio / Pexels (not the actual photo)




SHVETS production / Pexels (not the actual photo)





Kevin Malik / Pexels (not the actual photo)





Alena Darmel / Pexels (not the actual photo)



magnific / Magnific (not the actual photo)



u/Responsible-Road-921

magnific/ Magnific (not the actual photo)
















Ketut Subiyanto / Pexels (not the actual photo)




Shamsuddin Habib / Pexels (not the actual photo)




magnific / Magnific (not the actual photo)




Alex Green / Pexels (not the actual photo)



magnific / Magnific (not the actual photo)



u/Responsible-Road-921

RDNE Stock project / Pexels (not the actual photo)
Despite what stories like Cinderella and Snow White will tell you, not all stepmothers are evil. In fact, it’s extremely common to have a stepparent and step-siblings. According to a survey by the Pew Research Center, 42% of adults in the United States say that they have at least one step relative. And 18% have a living stepparent.
Understandably, though, relationships with stepparents are often not as close as those with biological parents. 85% of Americans say they feel very obligated to take care of their mother and father, while only 56% say they feel the same way about their stepparent(s).
At the same time, 78% of parents feel very obligated to take care of their adult children, while only 62% of stepparents feel the same obligation towards their partner’s children.
Now, being a stepparent certainly isn’t easy for everyone. Step Family Solutions notes that there are some harsh realities that come along with this role. Both the stepparent and their stepchildren will have to choose to and learn to love each other, as their love is not inherent.
It can also be difficult for a stepmother to have to deal with the “evil stepmother” stereotype. She might be judged more harshly by the children, which can make it harder for her to fall in love with them and want to develop a strong relationship with them.
But ultimately, as the parent and older person in the relationship, it is a stepparent’s responsibility to treat their stepchildren kindly and fairly. There’s never going to be a valid reason for being cruel, throwing away their things, and tampering with their food.
When it comes to coping with a toxic stepparent, The Scapegoat Club notes that there are a few common issues that arise between stepparents and stepchildren. First, they mention that it’s common for stepparents to overstep in the relationship. They may try to create too many rules, police the child’s behavior, or control the family dynamic.
Conflicts can also arise when the biological parent dismisses their child’s concerns to side with their spouse. This can be extremely painful for the child, even if they are an adult, and it can create a tense environment at home. The Scapegoat Club also notes that unhealthy dynamics are often created when a stepparent’s toxic behavior is ignored.
As for what to do in these situations, they recommend acknowledging that your parent’s choice is theirs to make. If they want to side with their spouse, they can, even if it may damage their relationship with you. 
Don’t be afraid to communicate your feelings with your parent, though. It’s possible that they didn’t even realize what kind of dynamic had been created, and they might be eager to fix whatever damage has been done.
Meanwhile, setting and enforcing healthy boundaries with a toxic stepparent is necessary. You may need to create distance and get plenty of space from them to protect your own peace and mental health.
We would love to hear your thoughts on this situation in the comments below, pandas. Have you ever dealt with a toxic stepparent of your own? And what would you have done if you were in the author’s shoes? Then, if you’re looking for another article from Bored Panda discussing similar family drama, look no further than right here.
