80,2026-10-26,"Latest news
Microsoft used October 9, 2026 to introduce a model that refuses to write you a paragraph. Microsoft-Decision-1 doesn’t chat, summarize, or draft emails. It scores a fixed list of answer options in a single forward pass and hands back a probability for each one, then gets out of the way. The company built it on top of Alibaba’s Qwen3.5-9B, made it available through Microsoft Foundry immediately and through OpenRouter shortly after, and is already pointing to internal uses ranging from incident response to scientific discovery.
It’s a narrow launch by design, and that’s exactly what makes it interesting. While the rest of the industry races to ship bigger, chattier assistants, Microsoft spent its October announcement on a model built to do one thing: pick an answer from a list, fast, and tell you how confident it is. For engineering teams building agent pipelines, routing layers, and automated verification systems, that’s arguably more useful than another general-purpose chatbot.
Don't miss new tech stories on Google
Add Shattered once in the Google app and our stories show up in your news suggestions.
Most AI headlines this year have been about models that generate more: more tokens, more context, more creative output. Microsoft-Decision-1 moves in the opposite direction. According to the company’s own announcement, the model reads supplied content alongside a set of predefined answer options and returns a calibrated probability score for each one, instead of generating open-ended text. That’s a meaningfully different job than what GPT-6 Sol, Gemini 4 Argon, or Claude Opus 5.5 are built to do.
The Shattered Newsletter
The week's biggest stories in tech, gaming and security, in one email.

Free, once a week. Unsubscribe any time.
Microsoft named four core use cases for the model at launch: routing, classification, prioritization, and verification, plus what it calls workflow control. Think of a support ticket that needs to be routed to the right team, a piece of user content that needs a yes/no moderation call, or an AI agent’s output that needs a pass/fail check before it’s allowed to proceed. Those are high-volume, low-latency decisions, and they’re exactly the kind of task that a 9-billion-parameter specialist model can do more cheaply than a full-sized reasoning model.
The distinction matters for anyone building with large language models today. A chatbot generates a response and you have to parse it, guess at its confidence, and hope it followed your formatting instructions. A decision-scoring model skips that parsing step entirely: the output format is already a probability distribution over options you defined. That’s a smaller, more mechanical problem, and it’s one Microsoft is betting plenty of production systems have in volume.
The detail that generated the most commentary wasn’t the use case, it was the foundation. Microsoft-Decision-1 is post-trained from Qwen3.5-9B, a 9-billion-parameter open-weight model from Alibaba. The Register flagged the choice directly, framing the launch as Microsoft leaning on a Chinese AI lab’s open-weight release to get the product out the door quickly, while noting Microsoft’s own stated intent to migrate the model’s foundation over time.
Microsoft has been explicit that this is a starting point, not a destination. In its launch post, the company said: “To build Microsoft-Decision-1, we post trained Qwen3.5-9B for fast, single-pass decision scoring and will soon rebase it on other models, including Microsoft AI (MAI) and OpenAI.” That’s a notable admission for a company that has poured billions into its own model development through the Microsoft Foundry platform and its OpenAI partnership. It suggests the Qwen base was chosen for speed to market on a narrow, well-defined task, not as a long-term architectural commitment.
Using someone else’s open-weight model as scaffolding isn’t unusual in 2026. Plenty of smaller, task-specific tools get built this way, the same logic that drove projects like JetBrains’ Mellum2.1 coding model to pick a compact parameter count over raw scale. What’s notable here is that it’s Microsoft, a company with its own frontier model ambitions, publicly choosing a rival lab’s weights for a flagship-named product, then telling customers up front that the underlying engine will change later.
Microsoft is leaning hard on numbers to justify the product. The company says Decision-1 achieved the highest accuracy across a comparison spanning 36 benchmarks and approximately 150,000 held-out questions, a scale meant to signal breadth rather than a single cherry-picked test. Microsoft reported 83.5 percent accuracy across that evaluation, a figure the German outlet BornCity also relayed.
Speed claims followed the same pattern of big, specific numbers. Industry newsletter AI Weekly and the AI-tracking site TestingCatalog both reported that Microsoft claims Decision-1 runs 4.5 times faster than a model it calls Quyet-1.0-Large, and 35 times faster than GPT-6 Sol. A separate write-up from Data Studios led with the 35x figure as its headline framing. Microsoft’s own launch material describes the GPT-6 Sol result as approximately 35× lower P50 latency, measured on its own benchmark, and says Decision-1 was 2.5× faster than H2O-Lightning-4B v1.1, the runner-up in its comparison. That is a specific P50 measurement rather than a general-purpose speed advantage, since the systems perform different tasks: Decision-1 scores fixed options while GPT-6 Sol generates text. Taiwan’s Liberty Times reported response times in the neighborhood of 85 milliseconds, though it didn’t specify the hardware or test conditions behind that number.
Here’s where a healthy dose of skepticism belongs. These are Microsoft’s own benchmark results, reported through the company’s launch materials and relayed by outlets covering the announcement. None of the coverage reviewed for this piece includes an independent, third-party reproduction of the 36-benchmark comparison, the 4.5x and 35x speed multipliers, or the 85-millisecond latency figure. That doesn’t make the numbers false, but it does mean they should be read as vendor-reported performance claims rather than peer-reviewed results, at least until someone outside Microsoft runs the same tests.
One figure got less attention but may matter more in production: Microsoft says the model’s decisions were “perturbation-stable” 98.7 percent of the time, according to the company’s launch summary cited by AI Weekly. Stability under small input changes is a real concern for any model making binary or multi-option calls that downstream systems act on automatically. If a routing decision flips because of a trivial rewording, that’s a production bug waiting to happen, so a high stability number, if it holds up under outside testing, would be a genuinely useful data point for engineering teams evaluating the model.
Microsoft’s own announcement and Microsoft Foundry documentation don’t publish a confirmed price in the sources reviewed for this article. What’s circulating instead comes from OpenRouter’s own model listing and was picked up by AI Weekly and the Italian outlet Pasquale Pillitteri: $0.042 per million input tokens, with output free. That pricing structure makes sense for a model whose entire output is a short list of probability scores rather than generated text, since there’s effectively nothing to meter on the output side.
At that price, Decision-1 is positioned as a genuinely cheap option for high-volume routing and classification work, the kind of task that currently gets routed to smaller general-purpose models mostly because nobody wants to pay full-model prices to ask “is this spam, yes or no.” It’s a similar cost logic to what pushed Anthropic to cut Claude Haiku pricing by 75 percent earlier this year: cheap, fast models win the high-frequency, low-complexity slice of the API traffic pie, even when a flagship model would technically do the job too.
Microsoft-Decision-1 launched in public preview on Microsoft Foundry, where documentation describes it as available for classification, routing, ranking, grading, and binary decisions. Microsoft’s launch post initially described OpenRouter availability as “coming soon,” but OpenRouter’s own model listing confirmed the model had gone live under the identifier microsoft/microsoft-decision-1 within roughly a day of the announcement, with OpenRouter posting that it was “live on OpenRouter.”
Weights for the model aren’t being distributed, based on reporting from Data Studios, meaning developers access Decision-1 exclusively through hosted APIs rather than downloading and self-hosting it, a contrast with the fully open Qwen3.5-9B it’s built on. That’s a common pattern for commercial derivatives of open-weight models, but it’s worth flagging for teams that assumed a Qwen-based product would come with the same openness as the base model.
The model accepts text and supports inputs up to 32,000 tokens. It returns calibrated scores for predefined options, and it does not generate explanations or handle images, audio, or video. Teams that need a written rationale or multimodal input will have to pair it with a general-purpose model rather than expect Decision-1 to cover that work.
The confidence score is the part of the output that applications act on. The probability itself is part of the API, not just a ranking score, and applications use confidence to decide when to act, defer, or ask for review. One routing example reads: if urgent is above 0.9 and priority is above 1.5, page the on-call engineer immediately. Another example sets a floor: if the top probability is below 0.65, a local policy can avoid downgrading, keep the current model or request manual selection.
A worked example in the documentation shows the model returning a weighted priority score of 1.9868 with 98.08% confidence. A 90% prediction should be right about nine times out of 10 on representative cases, which is the standard teams should hold the scores to when they test them on their own data. The quickstart needs Python 3.10 or later, so a first pilot can be small. Because Microsoft describes pricing as dynamic, it is worth checking the current Foundry listing before sizing a deployment.
Satya Nadella personally introduced the model on X, writing: “Introducing Microsoft-Decision-1, our new model for fast decision-making. It delivers top performance on structured decision tasks, outperforming both LLMs and other decision models in latency and quality. We’re already testing it across Microsoft for everything from incident response and quality control to scientific discovery.” That’s a specific, named set of internal applications, three in total, and it signals that Microsoft isn’t just shipping Decision-1 to third-party developers. The company is running it through its own operational workflows first.
Incident response is a logical first stop: a decision-scoring model could triage alerts, score severity, or decide whether a ticket needs to escalate to a human, all tasks that fit the “fixed options plus confidence score” shape perfectly. Quality control and scientific discovery are broader claims, and no named Microsoft executive beyond Nadella has gone on record describing how those workflows actually use the model, although Microsoft-published material has since described other internal trials in more detail.
Microsoft reported that Xbox Research used Decision-1 to categorize more than 10,000 pieces of open-ended feedback into predefined themes, with quality described as competitive with GPT-5 and execution reported as 80–100 times faster. Other Microsoft-published material says teams also evaluated the model for Copilot-response assessment and adaptive replanning. These are company-reported results, so they show the intended shape of the work more than an independent measurement.
Microsoft’s documentation also gives a concrete picture of the decisions it has in mind. One example is an incident described as “The checkout service has been unavailable for 20 minutes. Customers cannot complete purchases, and there is no workaround.” The model scores that report against priority levels, and the application decides what to do with the result. Foundry lists support-ticket triage, incident prioritization, content filtering, model, tool and agent selection, and model-output evaluation among the supported uses.
A Microsoft developer community post on decision routing for GitHub Copilot in CLI and VS Code shows the same idea applied to model selection, with the router placed in the native /model command in Copilot CLI and in the Chat Model Picker in VS Code. For deployment, the documented options include Global Standard and, in selected regions, Data Zone Standard. For a team evaluating the model, the practical takeaway is to start with a decision that already has clear options, such as a ticket queue or a model picker, and to measure the scores against past human decisions before letting them trigger any action automatically.
Microsoft isn’t the only company betting that 2026’s AI market has room for small, task-specific models alongside the frontier giants. Amazon shipped its own entry in this space with Strands Decider 2B, a 2-billion-parameter open-source agent model built for fast decisions and clocking around 100 milliseconds in AWS’s own testing. That’s a smaller parameter count than Decision-1’s 9B base, and it’s open source where Decision-1 is API-only, but the two products are chasing the same niche: agent guardrails and routing decisions that don’t need a full reasoning model.
The broader AI model market in October 2026 looks like a barbell. On one end, labs keep pushing frontier model scale, as with Mistral’s Large 4 preview at roughly 1 trillion parameters or the four-tier rollout of GPT-6 inside ChatGPT. On the other end, vendors are shipping cheap, narrow, fast models for the repetitive decision-making that sits underneath those flagship assistants. Decision-1 sits firmly in the second camp, closer in spirit to a classifier than to a chatbot, even though it shares a lineage with general-purpose language models.
The comparison underscores something important: Decision-1 isn’t really competing head-to-head with GPT-6 Sol or Gemini 4 Argon on the same job. It’s competing with them on cost and latency for the subset of requests that don’t need generation at all, the yes/no calls, the A/B/C classification, the pass/fail check. If a meaningful share of enterprise AI traffic really is that mechanical, a cheap specialist model could pull a surprising amount of volume away from general-purpose APIs, even without ever writing a sentence of prose.
The mechanics are straightforward compared to prompting a chatbot. A developer supplies context plus a closed set of options, and the model returns a probability for each one. A simplified request might look like this:
That output format is the entire point. An application can set a confidence threshold, say, route automatically above 0.75 and send anything lower to a human reviewer, without writing a single line of text-parsing logic. It’s a pattern familiar to anyone who has built a classification layer with traditional machine learning, just repackaged on top of a modern transformer base.
Decision-scoring isn’t a new idea, it’s closer to a return to form. Before the generative AI boom, most production machine learning was exactly this: classifiers, rankers, and scoring models trained for a single narrow task. The generative wave of 2022 through 2025 pulled a huge share of engineering attention toward chatbots and agents that could do anything, at the cost of being slower and more expensive for the simple stuff. Microsoft-Decision-1, AWS Strands Decider, and similar products represent an industry correction: use a frontier model when the task genuinely needs open-ended reasoning, and use a small, cheap, fast model when it doesn’t.
The “LLM-as-judge” pattern, where a language model evaluates or grades another model’s output, has been a staple of AI evaluation pipelines for a couple of years now. What’s changed in 2026 is vendors packaging that pattern as a dedicated, priced product rather than something developers hand-rolled with prompt engineering on a general model. Microsoft’s framing of Decision-1 for “AI judging” and “agent guardrails” use cases places it squarely in that lineage, just formalized and sold as infrastructure.
The most direct pressure lands on vendors selling general-purpose models for routing and classification tasks that customers are currently overpaying for relative to the job’s actual complexity. If enterprises start redirecting their high-volume, low-complexity API calls toward purpose-built scoring models, that’s a real dent in token volume for providers whose business model depends on charging full-model rates for tasks that don’t need full-model reasoning.
It also puts competitive pressure on Google and Anthropic to decide whether they need their own named decision-scoring product, or whether they’ll fold similar functionality into existing lightweight tiers. Google has already been tightening its free-tier model lineup, as seen with Gemini 4 Argon’s pricing and benchmark split this year, while Anthropic’s cost cuts to Claude Haiku suggest the same instinct: hold the frontier model’s price up top, and compete hard at the bottom of the market where volume lives.
There’s also a cloud infrastructure angle. Microsoft Foundry is Microsoft’s platform for selling AI compute and model access to enterprise customers, and a cheap, high-volume, sticky workload like routing and classification is exactly the kind of traffic that keeps customers inside one cloud’s ecosystem rather than shopping API calls across providers. A $0.042-per-million-token product, if it performs as claimed, could become a quiet volume driver for Foundry even if it never becomes a headline product the way a new chatbot would.
Microsoft’s stated plan to rebase Decision-1 on MAI and OpenAI models raises an obvious operational question for any team that adopts it now: will scores, calibration, and latency hold steady across that transition? Microsoft describes the MAI and OpenAI rebases as planned rather than released, and it hasn’t published a migration timeline or committed to backward compatibility for existing integrations in the sources reviewed here. Teams building production systems on the API identifier microsoft/microsoft-decision-1 today are implicitly betting that a future swap of the underlying model won’t meaningfully change how their routing or classification thresholds behave.
That’s a reasonable bet for a low-stakes use case like content routing, and a much riskier one for anything touching compliance, safety classification, or financial decisions, where a silent shift in a model’s calibration could change real-world outcomes without an obvious code change to point to. Expect Microsoft to publish versioning and changelog practices around this before enterprise customers commit meaningful volume to it.
A few gaps stand out in what’s publicly confirmed so far. Microsoft hasn’t named a spokesperson beyond Nadella’s own social post, so there’s no detailed technical explainer yet from the product or research team behind Decision-1. The benchmark methodology, including which 36 benchmarks were used and how the comparison models were configured, hasn’t been published in a form that outside researchers can reproduce. And the speed multipliers, including the approximately 35× lower P50 latency against GPT-6 Sol, remain Microsoft’s own benchmark claims rather than independently confirmed results.
None of that is disqualifying. Plenty of legitimate product launches lead with marketing numbers ahead of full technical disclosure. But buyers evaluating Decision-1 for anything beyond a low-stakes pilot should expect to run their own benchmark comparison rather than taking the 35x and 4.5x speed claims at face value.
For teams already running classification or routing logic on a general-purpose model, Decision-1 is worth a pilot, not a wholesale migration. Pick one low-stakes workflow, run it against both the current model and Decision-1 for two to four weeks, and compare accuracy, latency, and cost directly rather than trusting Microsoft’s published multipliers. For teams building new agent pipelines from scratch, it’s worth comparing Decision-1 against AWS’s Strands Decider 2B side by side, since the two sit close enough in purpose that the choice will likely come down to hosting preference (API-only versus open weight) and whatever cloud the rest of the stack already lives in.
Anyone touching compliance, safety, or financial decisioning should wait for Microsoft to publish a versioning policy before putting Decision-1 in that path. The upside of a fast, cheap scoring model is real, but so is the risk of an unannounced calibration shift once the promised rebase on MAI or OpenAI technology actually happens.
What is Microsoft-Decision-1?It’s a specialized AI model from Microsoft, announced October 9, 2026, built to score fixed answer options with a calibrated probability rather than generate open-ended text. Microsoft positions it for routing, classification, prioritization, verification, and workflow control.
What model is Microsoft-Decision-1 based on?Microsoft post-trained Qwen3.5-9B, a 9-billion-parameter open-weight model released by Alibaba, to build the first version of Decision-1. Microsoft has said it plans to rebase future versions on its own Microsoft AI (MAI) models and on OpenAI technology.
Where can I access Microsoft-Decision-1?It’s available now in public preview through Microsoft Foundry, and it went live on OpenRouter under the model identifier microsoft/microsoft-decision-1 within about a day of the launch announcement.
How much does Microsoft-Decision-1 cost?Microsoft listed the price at $0.042 per million input tokens, with no output-token charge, for the listed US and EU Datazone offerings in Foundry, and OpenRouter’s listing and secondary reports show the same figure. Microsoft Community guidance says pricing is dynamic, so deployments should use the current Foundry listing.
Is Microsoft-Decision-1 faster than GPT-6 Sol or other large models?Microsoft claims it is roughly 35 times faster than GPT-6 Sol and 4.5 times faster than a model it refers to as Quyet-1.0-Large, based on the company’s own benchmark comparison. These are vendor-reported figures that have not been independently reproduced in the coverage reviewed here, so treat them as a starting point rather than a verified result.
Can I download and self-host Microsoft-Decision-1’s weights?No. Reporting indicates the weights are not being distributed, so access is limited to Microsoft’s hosted API through Foundry or OpenRouter, unlike the fully open Qwen3.5-9B model it’s derived from.
How is this different from AWS Strands Decider 2B?Both are small, fast models built for agent-style decisions rather than open-ended generation. Strands Decider 2B is open source and smaller at 2 billion parameters, while Decision-1 is larger at a 9B base and only accessible through Microsoft’s hosted API, not self-hostable.
Will Microsoft-Decision-1 replace general-purpose chatbots for businesses?No. It’s built for a narrow slice of tasks, picking an answer from a fixed list with a confidence score, not for conversation, content generation, or open-ended reasoning. It’s meant to sit alongside general-purpose models, handling the high-volume, low-complexity decisions that don’t need a full chatbot response.

81,2026-10-26,"Lyndon Faulkner will be formally elected as WRU chairman in November
New Welsh Rugby Union chair Lyndon Faulkner has confirmed he will review the controversial plan to cut a professional club in Wales.
The millionaire business magnate and former rugby player does not officially start his role until next month.
But he has already drawn up a list of priorities to get to grips with, including a review of the future number of regions and the union's ""worrying"" financial performance.
Faulkner - the nephew of Wales great Tony 'Charlie' Faulkner - hit back at suggestions he has been appointed to simply wave through the highly contested shake-up of professional rugby which will reduce the number of regions from four to three in 2028.
""There's talk out there that whoever took this job was under the condition that they can't talk about that [regional shake-up] but that's absolutely incorrect,"" said Faulkner.
""I wouldn't have taken the job with those conditions or if it were me needing to be a yes man. That's not me.""
In his first interview since accepting the role, Faulkner admitted he was not aware of the full extent of the WRU's debt until it was announced last week.
""I feel it's my role to review everything we've got going on, challenge it, probe it and hopefully bring my experience to bear where I feel we can do things better.
""I haven't done what I've done in business by not doing that.""
Asked if he would be prepared to row back on the WRU's decision to cut a region, Faulkner replied: ""Absolutely - and that's on anything I see.""
WRU bosses under pressure to resign after Â£18m loss
Scrum V: Is Welsh rugby living beyond its means?
WRU publishes data on why west Wales team will be cut
The 66-year-old is a fan of any potential Anglo-Welsh competition, having played in a similar competition for Pontypool in the 1980s, and broadly agreed with the WRU's decision to rescue Cardiff. 
Faulkner will not be part of the WRU executive team, but he will chair the board and while he does not make the final decisions, he will wield major influence.
""They picked me because of my experience so it would be foolish to not take consideration of what I say,"" he said.
He is ""empathetic"" to the feelings of distrust from Ospreys and Scarlets - the two sides most at threat from closure - and was eager that a review would not prolong any decision over the regional shake-up which, he admitted, had already dragged on.
He said: ""We've got four regions, all with their history in our great game. So I am understanding of the emotions that it drives. At the end of the day, we've got a review to do and then look at how that comes out.""
Faulkner has plenty of experience of turning around the fortunes of struggling businesses.
The WRU may not be failing but, speaking at the Arms Park before a Super Rygbi Cymru match, Faulkner visibly grimaced when asked about the Â£18m loss posted for the latest financial year.
""That was quite a surprise to me because the previous finances didn't look that bad, but they are worrying,"" he said.
""So there is an absolute business aspect that has to be fixed and without that, we haven't got the right investments to go where we need the investments to go. 
""There are no simple reasons, but there will be things we change or improve that will make a difference to those numbers, to manage costs and get revenue in. 
""There's probably not a company in the UK at the moment, unless you're in oil and gas, that's not having some of the same issues with utility bills, minimum wages, national insurance or cost of food.""
Faulkner watched games in Cardiff and Swansea on Saturday
Coming from a famous rugby family, Faulkner made a name for himself after a knee injury ended his playing career, working for and eventually owning Welsh technology company Nimbus.
He talked Richard Branson into investing in CDs and then succeeded Robert Maxwell as owner of the business he later floated on the New York stock exchange.
He helped launch the Xbox and Microsoft Office before making his fortune trading and investing with some of the biggest tech companies in the United States - and even featured on Apprentice-style TV show The Mentor.
""$100m doesn't frighten me,"" he quipped.
But while Faulkner joked that not only his wife, but also close friends questioned his decision to get involved in the chaos of Welsh rugby, there was a heartfelt belief he can help provide solutions.
And he admitted he was frustrated by the ""noise"" generated by those voicing opinions ""from the side of the ring"" but unwilling to roll up their sleeves.
Swansea council leader Rob Stewart, ex-player Alex Cuthbert and businesswoman Hayley Parsons all called on WRU bosses to resign after the reported financial loss last week.
""Let's be honest, I'm putting my impeccable professional reputation on the line here,"" admitted Faulkner.
""But I'm actually getting in the ring. It frustrates me there's a lot of voices out there telling everybody what to do, but many of those voices are not prepared to actually get in the fight - and that list is quite extensive.
""It's also pretty distracting from the playing side. There are positive things happening, but we need people to support.""
He added: ""I don't need a job. I love Welsh rugby and this is purely motivated around trying to make a difference. I can apply all the things I've done in business but as an ex-player, I also understand rugby.
""There's no shortage of people telling me what we should be doing, but I think everybody in Wales wants us to do well, on and off the field. That's my goal.""
Faulkner twice faced Australia with Pontypool in 1981 and 1984
Criticisms of his predecessor Richard Collier-Keywood were that he neither lived in Wales nor had any grounding in Welsh rugby. The merits of those arguments can be debated, but cannot apply to Faulkner.
As a centre, he played in the fearsome Pontypool team of the 1980s that was among the best in the UK and included the likes of Graham Price, Eddie Butler and David Bishop.
He won the Welsh Cup, represented Monmouthshire and twice faced Australia. 
And while Wales honours eluded him behind the likes of Mark Ring and Dai Richards, Faulkner was shaped by working under inspirational coach Ray Prosser.
""He was an amazing man and there's a whole mix of emotions and things you learn under coaches like that,"" he recalled.
""I've always believed in the skills rugby teaches you, not just on the field but teamwork, camaraderie, competition, winning. 
""And frankly, my success in business was taking many of those things to a business environment, building a team, having everybody look after each other's back and being tough.""
While his uncle 'Charlie' Faulkner was a mainstay of a legendary Wales team of the past, Lyndon - whose son is finding his feet as a flanker in the Cardiff pathway - clearly believes he can be part of galvanising Welsh rugby's future.

vivo Android Announcement

The vivo Y600 is now available for pre-order in China, as promised, with open sales set to begin on October 30. The phone packs a massive 10,000mAh battery and has also earned a Guinness World Record for battery endurance.
As per the Guinness World Records test, the phone lasted for 32 hours and 37 minutes during a continuous livestream test. The 10,000mAh battery supports 44W wired fast charging and reverse wired charging.
The handset sports a 6.83-inch AMOLED display with a 120Hz refresh rate and is powered by the Dimensity 7300e chipset. It also gets a 50MP primary rear sensor and an 8MP selfie camera.
The vivo Y600 offers an under-display fingerprint scanner, NFC support, and an IP68 rating as well.
Looking at the specs and the design, it’s pretty much confirmed that the Y600 is a rebranded vivo S2 FE/V80 Lite for the Chinese market.
Pricing starts at CNY 1,999 ($280) for the 6GB/128GB variant, with the 8GB/128GB option priced at CNY 2,099 ($295). It is also available in 6GB/256GB and 8GB/256GB models, which are priced at CNY 2,199 ($310) and CNY 2,499 ($350), respectively.
The vivo Y600 is being offered in Diamond Gold, Obsidian Black, and Sapphire Blue color options.

84,2026-10-26,"The Federal Reserve has just two more meetings left in the year, and markets see at least one of them delivering another rate hike. That would be a mistake, Moody's Analytics' top economist Mark Zandi says. 
Zandi said in an X thread on Monday morning that the Fed can skip more hikes, pointing to the bond market as a reason the central bank doesn't have to rush to tighten financial conditions. 
""The 10-year Treasury yield is swinging wildly around 5%, and fixed mortgage rates are firmly above an unaffordable 7%,"" Zandi said. ""Corporate bond spreads have also widened. Unless rates recede soon, this will do real damage. Bond investors are already doing the Fed's work for it.""
The ""work"" that Zandi sees the bond market doing is making borrowing costs higher, which is what the Fed aims to do when it tries to cool inflation. Prior to the Fed's September rate increase, Zandi said that he viewed the decision as a ""serious mistake"" that could prove costly for the US economy.
A month later, the economist is still focused on the threats that further rate hikes could pose at a time when macro and geopolitical uncertainty remains high. 
""The Fed is trying to navigate between war-fueled inflation and mounting threats to a fragile economy,"" he noted. ""Raising rates now risks running it aground.""
Zandi noted that global oil prices are surging back toward wartime peaks, as tensions with Iran show no signs of easing. 
He acknowledged this will mean higher inflation, which will be a key focus of this week's Fed meeting, but he added that rate hikes can't fix the problems with oil supply. They could also ultimately slow the economy. 
""Higher oil prices will further undermine real wages and, ultimately, consumer spending, something the Fed shouldn't dismiss,"" he added.

Zandi has been analyzing the economic toll that the war with Iran has imposed on US households for months. In June 2026, he estimated the war had cost the typical American family an additional $1,000, but since then, gas prices have kept rising, thrusting the cost-of-living issue into the spotlight as the midterm elections loom.

CBS's Cupertino is a David vs. Goliath legal drama set in the heart of Silicon Valley that follows a lawyer (Mike Colter) who is being cheated out of his stock options by his former employer, a tech start-up. 88,2026-10-26,"Meta is making a massive financial play to secure its leadership in the AI race. The tech giant has launched concurrent public offerings involving billions of dollars in Class A common stock and depositary shares representing preferred stock.

The financing plan features roughly $35 billion in underwritten public offerings. This total breaks down into about $22.5 billion of Class A common stock and approximately $12.5 billion in depositary shares representing preferred stock.
In addition, major industry players like Nvidia, AMD, and Broadcom have indicated an interest in purchasing up to an aggregate of $5.0 billion of shares in the common stock offering.
This multi-billion dollar capital raise is designed to support the company’s continuous investments in artificial intelligence infrastructure, compute capacity, and personal superintelligence. With capital expenditures expected to reach staggering heights, Meta is turning to the public markets to diversify its funding sources alongside its robust operating cash flow, investment-grade debt, and strategic partnerships.
Meta plans to establish a future at-the-market program upon the trading commencement of its newly introduced Class C common stock. This additional program could potentially bring in up to an additional $60 billion over time, giving the company enormous financial flexibility as it scales its operations.
The social media giant’s core platforms like Facebook and Instagram are seeing record user engagement driven by video growth, while WhatsApp continues to thrive as a primary messaging hub. Meanwhile, new initiatives like the Muse AI network and business agent tools are quickly gaining millions of active users and showing strong commercial intent.
To power these services, Meta is expanding its data center footprint across multiple states and building massive compute superclusters. By leveraging custom silicon and advanced inference optimization, the company aims to make its massive data infrastructure more efficient over the long term.

Oct 26 : Meta Platforms said on Monday it plans to raise up to $95 billion in its largest-ever equity fundraising, as the social media giant spends aggressively on its AI infrastructure expansion.
The Facebook parent has launched public offerings worth about $35 billion in common and preferred stock, and said it also plans to launch a separate at-the-market share sale program worth up to $60 billion.
The company will launch the larger share sale program after its new non-voting Class C shares begin trading, which is expected in the first quarter of 2027, but said it has not yet finalized its timing and terms of the sale.
Meta's fundraising plan, among the biggest by an AI company, has also drawn interest of up to $5 billion in total from Nvidia, Advanced Micro Devices and Broadcom for Class A shares.

The latest fundraising further underscores a shift among big technology companies increasingly tapping debt markets and ​raising equity ⁠to bolster their AI infrastructure, having previously relied on cash to fund their investments.
Earlier this year, Alphabet increased the size of its equity offerings to $84.75 billion.
Separately on Monday, SpaceX said an affiliate of Apollo Global Management will buy AI chips and server hardware and lease them to the rockets-to-AI firm, in a deal with total principal payments of about $40 billion.
META'S AI SPENDING IS BURNING CASH
Meta has committed to spend about $825 billion in the future, primarily driven by its growing investments in data centers, AI-related infrastructure and cloud computing power.
But the aggressive expansion has eroded its historically strong free cash flow.
The company reported a $2.12 billion cash burn in the third quarter, its first negative free cash flow in over a decade, compared with a positive cash flow of $10.63 billion a year ago.
Still, the early popularity of its consumer-focused AI agent Muse and release of a competitive new series of AI models have helped partly justify Meta's aggressive investments.
""We believe that Meta will ultimately produce a profitable AI business and its returns longer-term should help the company diversify, although the path may not be linear,"" William Blair analysts said.
The company had cash, cash equivalents and marketable securities of $82.45 billion and long-term debt of $80.92 billion as of September 30, according to its latest quarterly filing.
Last week, Meta raised the lower end of its annual capital expenditure forecast to a range of $135 billion to $145 billion.
The company has said it would issue Class C common stock in January 2027, effectively resulting in a two-for-one split, to fund acquisitions and retain top talent while maintaining CEO Mark Zuckerberg's control.
Meta agreed to grant Zuckerberg Class C shares that he can sell to help pay a potential California billionaire wealth tax, in exchange for a commitment to keep him in California.

British imperial policy in the 1840s promoted Protestant institutions and Jewish restoration in Palestine. Since 1977, Likud’s Revisionist Zionists made a strategic priority of partnering with Christian fundamentalists. The theology came first. The political alliance came later.

By Olivia Rosane, the opinion editor and a staff writer for Common Dreams. Originally published at Common Dreams 
In a historic comeback, Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva triumphed in Sunday’s run-off election over right-wing challenger Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of former Brazilian President and convicted coup-plotter Jair Bolsonaro.
Lula’s victory comes despite the best efforts of US President Donald Trump and his Big Tech backers, as Trump’s administration openly intervened on behalf of Bolsonaro and Meta boosted pro-Bolsonaro over pro-Lula content on its social media platforms.
“Lula won, Brazil won, democracy won, the Americas won,” said Pennsylvania Democratic House nominee Chris Rabb from Lula’s campaign headquarters in Sao Paulo, Brazil, after the results were announced around 9:15 pm Brasilia time. “They declared victory for Lula da Silva for his fourth term against a raging fascist, rubber-stamped for Donald Trump.”
Rabb noted that Lula’s victory marked an important turning point for the left in Latin America, as far-right parties hadwon every other national election in the region since the start of Trump’s second term. “This is the first victory for democracy in this hemisphere since Trump took office,” Rabb said.
With 99.99% of the votes counted, Lula won by 51.79% to Bolsonario’s 48.21%, making him the first Brazilian candidate since the restoration of democracy in 1985 to win a second-round election after coming in second in the initial round.
“I would like to thank the Brazilian people for the generosity they have shown me for a long time,” Lula said in his victory speech in São Paulo late Sunday. “And, in this second round, I want to thank the generosity of the Brazilian youth, who took to the streets to defend democracy.”
After finishing two percentage points behind Bolsonaro in the October 4 poll, Lula re-energized his campaign by finally shortening the Brazilian workweek from six to five days, as his supporters launched demonstrations against the far-right.
“These elections have represented a historic victory for the majority of the Brazilian people, who mobilized to defend democracy against the threat of fascism,” Francesca Emanuele, senior international policy associate with the Center for Economic and Policy Research (CEPR), told Common Dreams from Sao Paulo, Brazil, where she was monitoring the elections. “It was the Brazilian people themselves (students, working-class, and middle-class Brazilians) who took to the streets and mobilized tirelessly from the very first day after the first round of voting to overturn the first-round results and secure Lula’s victory.”
Reginaldo Lopes, a federal deputy for Lula’s Workers’ Party, told The Guardian: “I feel incredible… This is Brazil telling the world that the extreme right is fascist, that the extreme right is against Black people, is against the poor, against the state, against gay people… We are going to reorganize the democratic forces [in this country.]”
Sunday’s victory did not come easily, as the Lula campaign complained of bus shortages in the key cities of Salvador and Sao Paulo, requesting that the Brazilian Supreme Court extend the voting by two hours. The court granted the request, so polls closed at 7 pm Brasilia time instead of 5 pm.
“Lula has won despite the unfair practices his campaign has faced from Meta, interference from the Trump administration, and severe disruptions to public transportation on election day in major cities (where up to 50% of bus fleets were reportedly out of service),” Emanuele told Common Dreams. “These disruptions disproportionately affected working-class voters, who rely heavily on public transportation and have historically been a key part of Lula’s electoral base.”
She continued: “Despite all these obstacles, Lula’s campaign managed to turn around a first-round result that, just a few weeks ago, had seemed extremely difficult to reverse. This is a major victory for the majority of the Brazilian people.”
Several advocates and lawmakers hailed the election results as a rebuke to the far-right and the Trump administration in particular.
“During his campaign, President Lula characterized 2026’s elections as a choice between ‘sovereignty or submission.’ Brazil has clearly chosen sovereignty,” the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), who sent a delegation to observe both rounds of Brazil’s elections, wrote in a statement.
Progressive Rep. Ro Khanna (D-Calif.) posted on social media: “People all across the world are fighting back against extremism. With Lula in Brazil, they just won. Authoritarian leaders always attack democracy because they stand no chance against the will of their people. Trump must respect the results and not undermine Brazil’s elections.”
Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.) said: “Congratulations to Lula on his extraordinary reelection victory in the Brazilian presidential election. Lula has stood up for Brazilian workers & democracy—and will continue to do so in the next four years.”
“Lula’s hard-fought victory is an indictment on the right-wing wave sweeping through Latin America,” Joseph Bouchard, a journalist and researcher who focuses on Latin America, told Common Dreams. “Except for [ Salvadorian President Nayib] Bukele, the right across the region is still incredibly unpopular, and rightly seen as corrupt, autocratic, and uncommitted to fixing systemic issues. Lula shows there is still a fight for democracy worth having, while the region is plunged into its darkest, most regressive period since the Cold War. The entire region is populated by vassal states for US interests, who have declared states of exception to curtail rights, freedoms, and basic principles of social democracy, in the name of a failed and anti-human war on drugs.”
Lula’s victory also has planetary consequences, as he brought deforestation in the Amazon rainforest back down to record lows after it accelerated during the tenure of Jair Bolsonaro, who attacked environmental protections and Indigenous rights.
“Lula’s win is the first good news for the planet in a while!” wrote 350.org and Third Act co-founder Bill McKibben. “I bet it shaves a tenth of a degree off how hot our Earth eventually gets, which will save millions of lives. Thank you Brazil!”
Lula still faces a fight ahead, as Bolsonaro announced it was “impossible to concede the election result” due to the extension of polling hours, even though his team had also sought an extension in parts of southeastern Brazil because of unfavorable weather.
Bouchard pointed out that “Lula will fight an uphill battle with a far-right Congress, inept Supreme Court, and constant outside pressure..”
“Trump’s silence on the result is worrisome, with Flavio not conceding and demanding an annulment,” he told Common Dreams. “Sadly, much of the corporate press has legitimated these claims, despite the right’s concerted efforts at destroying democracy. No matter how much the authoritarians try to tip the scales, the people can still prevail, if the left is willing to fight. This might prove to be a confidence boost to the demoralized and overpowered left all over the region, and may hopefully set the region back toward progress, hard fought after decades of dictatorship and neoliberal transitions.”
US-based Lula allies urged others in the US to defend Brazil’s democracy from Trump administration interference and to take their own stand against the far-right in the upcoming midterm elections.
The DSA wrote:
However, the fight continues—the thin margin of this victory indicates that continued struggle and mobilization will be crucial. As global capitalism fails to solve worsening problems of its own making, the capitalists turn to right-wing authoritarianism. Brazil’s left-wing coalition will face a right-wing Congress at home, a hostile Trump administration in the US, and hostile capitalists both [at] home and abroad in the years ahead. We of the DSA stand in solidarity with our comrades in the PT [Lula’s Partido dos Trabalhadores], PSOL [Brazil’s left-wing Socialism and Liberty Party], and Brazilian social movements in this fight for democratic self-determination.
This solidarity means recognizing our country’s role in the threat to Brazilian democracy. These elections were marred by interference from the US, whether by Trump’s politically motivated tariffs, or by manipulation of social media by US-based tech companies like Meta. They aimed to subvert Brazil’s democracy to favor its far-right allies grouped in the “Shield of the Americas,” and to get their hands on crucial natural resources, such as lithium, cobalt, and rare earth minerals as well as petroleum and iron ore, which Brazil possesses in abundance and which Lula has sworn to protect as national patrimony. The DSA categorically denounces these meddlesome, imperialist efforts. We fight for a working-class foreign policy—one which centers peace, multilateralism, and respects national self-determination, free from US interference.
Rabb concluded his message with a call to action, and a celebration: “This is a big deal for us in the United States. This shows that Trumpismo is waning, and it is our responsibility now to make sure that we kill the beast. That beast is injustice, it’s oligarchy, it’s neoliberalism. All of these things. Now it’s our turn, folks. November 3 is right around the corner, but right now I’m going to party with these Brazilians.”
