Shawn Hymel is a course creator and instructor for edge AI and embedded systems that inspire and teach developers of all skill levels. Based in Lafayette, Colorado, he can be found giving talks, running workshops, and swing dancing in his free time.
Reinforcement learning (RL) is a subfield within artificial intelligence (AI) that has made dramatic strides in tackling complex tasks. It involves training an agent to accomplish a task through trial and error. The agent is a computer program that observes its environment and decides what action to take next. The rule it uses to make that decision is called its policy.
The agent can also receive a reward  from the environment, such as points in a video game, victory in a game of chess, or making a robot take a step without falling over. This reward is typically a numerical value. The agent uses this reward to improve its policy. Over time and with many interactions with the environment, the agent improves its ability to perform the task given to it by the programmer.
While there will always be a place for classical control schemes, such as a proportional-integral-derivative (PID) loop, a linear-quadratic regulator (LQR), or model predictive control (MPC), RL is gaining a lot of traction in the world of robots. It can be tricky to set up, train an agent, and deploy it to a real robot, but it can produce controllers that are remarkably robust to variations in the environment. Rather than trying to mathematically encode each possible state or scenario, you can train an RL agent to handle a wide variety of scenarios, and then the robot should be able to tackle similar situations.
In this guide, I’ll walk you through a conceptual overview of reinforcement learning and then show you how to train your own balance bot using the popular proximal policy optimization (PPO) algorithm. While balancing a two-wheeled robot has long been solved by classical control methods, this guide acts as an introduction to RL concepts and demonstrates how to train an RL agent, which should take only a few hours. You’ll see how the training and deployment process works, preparing you to tackle more complex robots, like Pollen Robotics/Hugging Face’s new bipedal Microduck.
You can accomplish most of this tutorial using just your computer, as we’ll be simulating most of the training. If you want to deploy to real hardware, you’ll need to purchase an M5Stack Bala2-Fire self-balancing robot kit.
Reinforcement learning dates back to the 1950s with Richard Bellman’s research into dynamic programming, Marvin Minsky’s work in computational trial-and-error learning, and Arthur Samuel’s famous checkers-playing program. Most of this early work in RL was influenced by animal-learning research, notably Thorndike’s law of effect and B.F. Skinner’s operant conditioning.
In the decades that followed, most RL research focused on algorithms that learn from experience rather than from a complete mathematical model of the environment. Bellman’s dynamic programming works well when you know exactly how the world behaves and the number of possible situations is manageable, but that’s rarely true. Even games with well-defined rules can have too many positions to calculate exactly. In 1992, IBM’s Gerald Tesauro combined temporal-difference learning with a neural network to create TD-Gammon, which taught itself backgammon through self-play and reached near-world-champion level. The real world is harder still. Unlike board games, moving a robotic arm through space means dealing with enormous amounts of variability, such as sensor noise, motor backlash, someone bumping the arm, and so on.
The deep learning revolution that started in the early 2010s marked a turning point for the RL field as well, ushering in the era of deep reinforcement learning. In deep RL, neural networks can estimate how much future reward to expect from a given situation, they can represent the policy itself, or they can do both. As a result, RL has seen a whole host of rapid successes. Most literature points to 2013 being the turning point of this deep RL revolution, with DeepMind’s DQN learning to play dozens of Atari games by examining pixel data.
In 2016, DeepMind’s AlphaGo defeated Lee Sedol, one of the world’s top Go players, in a five-game match that shocked the AI community and demonstrated the power of RL. Then in 2019, OpenAI Five, another RL program, defeated the professional eSports team OG in the popular video game Dota 2. Deep RL has since spread well beyond games. It’s used to fine-tune large language models like ChatGPT and Claude, and it’s driving rapid progress in robotics.
We can also see how deep RL is being used to make incredible progress in robotics. In 2024, Boston Dynamics announced that it had integrated RL into Spot’s locomotion controller, working alongside the classical MPC it had used for years. That same year, Disney Research published a paper on its BDX droids, which use RL to perform expressive, animator-designed movements that give them lifelike personalities.
Reinforcement learning is proving to be a breakthrough technology used to advance the field of robotics. Let’s turn our attention to how it works in practice.
Reinforcement learning consists of a relatively straightforward learning loop. As we noted earlier, the agent is the decision-making part of the code. Other parts of the code read from sensors, look at pixel data, etc. and provide an observation to the agent. The agent looks at the information in that observation and chooses an action as an output.
The output of the agent is used to drive whatever we’re hoping to accomplish. This could be controller inputs for a video game character, a movie recommendation, a decision to buy or sell a stock, or, in our case, the speed and direction of each motor on the balance bot.
Note that the agent refers to a very particular piece of code: the decision-making policy, the value estimator (if one is used), and the learning algorithm that updates them. Everything else, including the supporting code that reads sensors and calculates rewards, the motors, the electronics, the robot chassis, and the rest of the world, is considered the environment.
After an action is taken, the environment reacts in some way. For our robot, the motors spin the wheels, which tips the robot in some direction, possibly driving it forward or backward. The supporting code then reads new data from the sensors, which include an inertial measurement unit (IMU) and two motor encoders for this example. These sensor measurements become the next observation that is fed to the agent.
During the learning process, we also provide a reward describing how the environment reacted to the agent’s previous decision. For our robot, we’ll construct a single numerical reward from a combination of the following:
The learning algorithm (we’ll use PPO in this example) uses rewards to automatically update the internal workings of the agent so that it ideally takes actions in the future that maximize the total rewards it collects over time.
When we’re happy with the performance of the agent, we can deploy it to the robot (or simulation of the robot). We usually deploy only the policy portion of the agent (often called the actor in deep RL). PPO’s value estimator (often called the critic) helps the learning algorithm during training, but the robot doesn’t need it to make decisions afterward. Additionally, our deployment code can remove the reward signal, because now we only need the agent to take in an observation and make a decision.
In our case, we will perform learning in a simulated environment. This allows us to easily reset the robot if it falls, run the physics engine faster than real life, and run multiple, parallel environments to speed up the process. When we deploy the agent, we must take special care to ensure that the observation values match up with those seen in simulation. That means any filtering, normalization, or unit conversions applied in simulation must also be applied on the real robot. For example, if we used m/s2 units for acceleration in simulation but our real sensors provide g-force, we need to convert them to m/s2 before feeding that data to the agent.
Policies are also usually designed to output normalized actions, which in our case means a motor control value between –1 and 1. In simulation, this value maps to a continuous command ranging from “full power backward” to “full power forward.” On the real robot, we scale that same value to a PWM signal for the motor driver. For example, if the policy outputs –0.73 for the left motor and our maximum PWM value is 255, we set the driver to reverse and send a PWM value of 186. It’s also good practice to clip the policy’s output to the –1 to 1 range before scaling, since the raw values can occasionally fall outside it. Whatever mapping you choose, the goal is consistency between simulation and reality. An output of 1.0 should mean the same thing to the real motors as it did to the simulated ones.
With an understanding of how the overall training loop works, let’s turn our attention to the PPO algorithm we’ll be using.
The world of RL has dozens of useful algorithms that have been developed over the years. Choosing the correct one can be an art in itself. For this demo, we’re going to use PPO, which was described in a 2017 academic paper written by OpenAI researchers. It is still used today as one of the most popular RL algorithms for controlling robots and fine-tuning LLMs. We won’t cover the gritty details of PPO, as there is a lot of underlying math involved, but let’s go over the basics.
While a PhD student at Drexel University, Costa Huang created reference implementations for a number of RL algorithms stored at github.com/vwxyzjn/cleanrl. We’ll be using a modified version of his ppo_continuous_action.py code. Feel free to look through that file as we briefly talk about how PPO works.
PPO is a type of actor-critic method that uses two separate neural networks in the agent.
The critic is a neural network that takes in the observation, which represents the state of the environment. An observation may capture the full state or only part of it. Our robot’s sensors, for example, can’t tell whether a wheel is slipping. Recall that the observation is a collection of values from our sensor readings (or other input from the environment, such as pixel data). The critic’s purpose is to try to answer the question, “How good is this state?”
In complex cases like our robot, we don’t know exactly how the environment will respond to any given action. Even if we understand the physics of a balance bot, sensor noise and motor variations add randomness that’s impossible to model exactly. So rather than trying to model the environment, we have the critic learn something simpler: an estimate of the total reward we can expect from a particular state.
The robot spends some time actually interacting with the environment (or simulation in our case). During this time, we keep a log of which actions were performed, the observations at each timestep, and the rewards received from the environment. This data collection process is known as a rollout. We then use this rollout information to train the critic to predict the future cumulative rewards from a given state.
Cumulative rewards are known as the return. In practice, rewards further in the future are discounted, so they count for a bit less than immediate ones. This encourages the agent to prefer good outcomes sooner rather than later and keeps the return from growing endlessly. In the code, this future factor is controlled by the gamma value. We use the standard neural network update algorithm known as back propagation to update the internal parameters in the critic to better predict future returns.
As the robot moves through the environment collecting rollouts, the critic ideally improves in its prediction capabilities. It might start out randomly guessing what return is to be expected from a given state. Over time, however, it learns to predict the total cumulative rewards from any given observation (state). This information is crucial for training the actor.
The actor is the neural network that acts as the policy for the agent. Just like the critic, it takes in an observation as input at each timestep. Instead of trying to predict the value of a given state, it provides the suggested action as output. In some deep RL algorithms, the output might be a set of discrete values. In this iteration of PPO, we’re allowing for a continuous set of actions for each motor. In other words, we want the actor to tell us how much to drive each motor based on the current observation.
The actor’s output is not a single action. Rather, the actor provides (for each motor) a mean, which is paired with a standard deviation to describe a Gaussian (normal) probability distribution. In some PPO implementations, the actor also provides a standard deviation with the mean. In the CleanRL implementation, the actor network calculates the mean from the observation, while the standard deviation is a separate value (one per motor) that the learning algorithm adjusts over time. During training, the agent samples a random action from this distribution at each timestep.
Providing a probability distribution like this offers an additional benefit beyond just working for continuous actions: it allows us to control the exploration-exploitation tradeoff during training. The actor might guess at a mean and provide a very wide standard deviation at the start (i.e. the actor says “I don’t know what action to choose, just pick something at random”). As the agent tries random actions, the actor ideally learns which actions provide good future returns. As time goes on, the mean moves around and begins to center on the actions that provide the best returns. In addition, the standard deviation shrinks so that the actor goes from guessing a range of actions (exploration) to providing a narrow range of known-good options (exploitation).
Like the critic, the actor is also trained using the backpropagation technique. Rather than trying to map an input of an observation to a future total return, the actor attempts to maximize the returns due to the actions chosen. Because we don’t know exactly how the environment operates (and therefore cannot calculate exact returns in advance), the actor relies on the critic’s predictions to understand how good a given action might be.
After the agent takes an action, we can ask, “Did things go better or worse than the critic expected?” This difference is called the advantage. If an action led to a better outcome than predicted, the actor adjusts to make that action more likely in similar situations. If it went worse, the actor makes it less likely.
As a result, we have the actor and critic working together in the learning process: the critic looks at an observation to approximate how much total return the agent will receive from that state (assuming the agent follows the policy given by the actor), and the actor uses the information from the critic to figure out which actions to choose to maximize that total return.
Actor-critic methods are incredibly powerful, as they can learn policies to accomplish complex goals in a wide variety of environments, even when we can’t fully mathematically model those environments. However, because the actor and critic rely on each other to learn, they can be extremely finicky and sensitive to the hyperparameters. These are the settings we choose by hand, such as the learning rate and gamma, as opposed to the parameters inside the networks that are updated automatically.
While a number of modern deep RL algorithms rely on this actor-critic setup, PPO’s key contribution is a way to keep learning stable. After each rollout, PPO reuses the collected data for several rounds of updates. However, that data was gathered using the old version of the policy, so if the policy changes too much, the data no longer reflects how the agent actually behaves, and, as a result, a single bad update can undo a lot of progress. To prevent this, PPO uses clipping to limit its updates so that the policy can’t change too drastically in one step. Think of it as a speed limit on learning: the agent improves in small, safe increments rather than in big, risky leaps.
With a basic understanding of PPO, let’s dive into actually training our robot!
We will use Google Colab to train the robot, which means you do not need to install anything on your computer for this part. Open a web browser and navigate to colab.research.google.com/github/shawnhymel/make-reinforcement-learning-for-robotics/blob/main/software/make_rl_train_balance_bot.ipynb. Note that we will use MuJoCo as our physics simulator to train the agent.
If you have not used Colab before, it is a free runtime environment for Python that allows you to run snippets of code one chunk at a time in a format known as a Jupyter Notebook. Each chunk is known as a cell. Click on the first cell of code (it starts with # Install required packages) to highlight it. Press Shift+Enter to run the current cell and move to the next cell. Continue to press Shift+Enter to run the cells in sequence.
The first collection of cells installs and imports the required libraries. Some of the libraries are custom-made and come from this repository: github.com/ShawnHymel/make-reinforcement-learning-for-robotics. Feel free to look through the files in that repository. Most notably, it contains:
The next set of cells assigns values to a number of training settings and hyperparameters. Here we’ll create 8 separate, parallel environments to train the robot. You won’t be able to see training happening in real time, as Colab does not have a separate viewer to let you watch MuJoCo. Each environment will take 500,000 total timesteps, where each step includes taking an action and getting an observation and reward in turn. The training algorithm will use that data to update the actor and critic’s internal parameters.
For the PPO hyperparameters, the actor and critic are separate neural networks, each with 2 hidden layers, and each hidden layer has 32 nodes. By setting the timestep value to 0 here, we tell the simulation to take steps as fast as possible (i.e. not to wait the full 5ms), which means the training simulation runs faster than real time.
We then have a number of helper functions that are used to create the environments from our balance_bot_env.py library and MJCF file, generate videos of the robot in action (i.e. see how well the policy is performing), and load an agent from a previous stage (if needed).
After that, we perform two simple tests of the environment. The first test loads the simulated environment and records a video of the robot falling. This shows what happens when the robot does not take any actions (no policy). The second test loads the same environment and records a video of what happens with a random policy. In other words, a random action is chosen for each motor at each timestep. The robot will likely scoot or twist in one direction before immediately falling.
Feel free to play the movies in the cells directly or download them from make-reinforcement-learning-for-robotics/software/output in Colab. If all the cells run successfully, then we are ready to train the agent.
Continue to press Shift+Enter to run each of the cells in the section titled Phase 1: Balance only. We will train our robot to balance using a series of phases, each more difficult than the last. In this first phase, we set the following reward coefficients (we use the alive_bonus default of 1.0):
If you dive into the balance_bot_env.py file, you can find where we construct the reward function:
At each timestep, we use the information from the observation and other world values to construct this reward value. If it’s a positive value, then it’s a reward. If it’s a negative value, then it’s a penalty. Let’s look at each of these values from the last line:
Note that each reward and penalty is multiplied by some coefficient value that we can set in code before training starts. These provide another set of hyperparameter knobs we can tune in order to encourage the robot to perform some actions or discourage others.
Building a reward function is still somewhat of an art in the RL world. You use it to shape the robot’s behavior (without programming it directly), and you have to be careful to avoid instances of reward hacking, where the agent finds a way to maximize the reward without achieving the outcome you intended. For example, if our reward only included the alive bonus and pitch penalty, the agent might learn to roll across the floor in order to stay upright, which would earn it a full reward score without doing what we intended. This is why we added the position and yaw penalties.
Once training is done in phase 1, scroll back up to look at the Tensor Board cell. Tensor Board is a visualization toolkit that will automatically log and graph values. It is extremely popular for watching how machine learning models train over time.
We show a TensorBoard cell at the start of each training phase that populates with data over time. If you do not see such data showing up, press the reload button in the top-right corner of the visualization (it looks like a circular arrow).
PPO is fairly complex, with lots of intermediate steps and values to watch for. However, the most important graph to look at is the one labeled charts/episodic_return. This shows the total return of each episode of simulation (before the robot falls or time runs out). This should approach some maximum value and stay there, indicating that the agent found a way to control the robot in order to maximize total rewards. In our case, 10,000 is the maximum total reward that the agent can obtain per episode. In the image shown, the robot performed very well for phase 1.
If the robot appears to be balancing in the evaluation video, you may proceed to phase 2.
NOTE: The free version of Colab imposes runtime limits to prevent people from using it to train large or complex machine learning models. By default, notebooks cannot exceed a maximum runtime of 12 hours. That being said, you might find that the interface will disconnect from the runtime from time to time, especially if you leave it idle for a while (around 30 minutes). If that happens, try pressing the Reconnect button in the top-right of the window. If that fails to return you to the previous state, you might have to select Runtime→Restart session and run through the entire training process again.
By the end of phase 1, we should have a robot that balances well in simulation. However, as you might guess, simulation is nothing like the real world. We made many assumptions about the robot and environment, including the battery voltage, how long it takes to execute a command given by the agent, the shape of the wheels, sensor noise, and so on. This is known as the sim-to-real gap, and it is an ongoing area of research in the robotics community.
While there are myriad ways to tackle the sim-to-real gap, one of the most powerful techniques in RL is domain randomization(DR). Rather than trying to model the real world exactly in simulation, which is often impossible, we can vary certain properties about the world and the robot. The agent simply learns to deal with these variations while continuing to focus on maximizing its rewards. So long as the agent achieves the intended goal and the real robot’s properties lie within the DR ranges, the robot should still balance.
A simple DR example that’s easy to visualize is adding random nudges to the robot during simulation. This simulates both someone gently pushing the robot or the robot bumping into an object. The agent must learn to handle these non-ideal situations, thus creating a more robust agent.
Most reinforcement learning algorithms start with a random policy. In our case, that means the motors will spin in a random direction with random speed, likely causing the robot to quickly fall down. The simulation resets if the robot tips past 30°, which means the robot will reset very often initially. Most RL algorithms look for when those random actions produce a little more total reward than before. The algorithm updates the agent to take those actions more often. This continues over time, and this trial-and-error approach to learning eventually settles on actions that maximize total rewards.
Rather than try to randomize a lot of properties and train the agent in a single phase, we’re going to introduce the concept of curriculum learning. This technique involves training the agent in successively harder phases, where we slowly increase the difficulty of the task or introduce more DR into the mix. Without curriculum learning, you might find that your agent simply cannot learn the most difficult version of your intended task, or it settles for some unintended actions through reward hacking.
Curriculum learning allows us to slowly shape the agent’s behavior over time. We start with an easier task and increase the difficulty. In our case, we first get the robot to balance. We use that same agent as the starting point in phase 2, where we introduce some DR elements. In this case, we vary the controls and electronics within the robot: sensor noise, up to a 1ms delay for actions to take effect, motor noise, and motor torque (to simulate various battery charge levels).
Press Shift+Enter to run each of the cells in the section titled Phase 2: Domain randomization for controls. Training for this phase will take another 20–30 minutes, so be patient. Once that’s done, refresh the TensorBoard display and check on the charts/episodic_return plot. Make sure it’s still close to about 10,000 reward points per episode.
NOTE: At the end of each training phase, we save the best-performing agent throughout the training process. Even if the agent performed poorly at the end of training, we use the agent with the best total rewards per episode going into the next phase.
Watch the evaluation video for phase 2. The robot should still balance in place. Feel free to try different reward coefficients or DR values to see how the agent responds after additional training.
Once you’re happy with the performance of the robot in phase 2, you may execute the cells in Phase 3: Domain randomization for the physical world. Here, we introduce more DR properties, including nudging the robot in a random direction every now and then, varying the mass of the robot’s parts (I measured them on a scale, but that’s not always exact), and varying the friction between the wheels and the ground (to simulate different surfaces).
Once again, check the TensorBoard charts and evaluation video to ensure the robot is behaving as intended in simulation (i.e. balancing upright without moving or turning). Once you’re happy with the performance, run the final cells in the Clean up and save model section. You’ve trained your balance bot agent!
These final cells close out the environments and export the agent for you to use on the real robot. Recall that the agent for PPO has two separate neural networks: the critic for approximating the value of the current state, and the actor for suggesting actions based on that state. When we deploy the agent, we no longer need the critic, as it was just used for training. The actor outputs the mean of the possible actions, as the standard deviation is dropped. We assume that the mean is the suggested action, so we use that to control our motors.
The final two cells export the actor as an ONNX file and as a .h header file for C. On the left side of the Colab page, click on the folder icon, which allows you to browse the files available in this instance. Click through to make-reinforcement-learning-for-robotics/software/output. Hover over the actor.h file to see the three dots pop up. Click on the three dots and select download, which downloads actor.h to your local computer.
You are welcome to repeat the process to download the ONNX file, but we won’t need it for this demo. You can upload it to the netron.app website in order to inspect the neural network’s layers.
On your local computer, navigate to the project repository at github.com/ShawnHymel/make-reinforcement-learning-for-robotics. Click Code→Download ZIP. Unzip the repository somewhere on your computer.
Copy the actor.h file that you downloaded and paste it in make-reinforcement-learning-for-robotics/software/balance_bot_arduino, overwriting the existing actor.h.
Download, install, and open the Arduino IDE. Go to File→Preferences→Additional boards manager URLs, and add the following URL: https://static-cdn.m5stack.com/resource/arduino/package_m5stack_index.json.
Click OK and let the board definitions update. Open the library manager and search for “M5Unified.” Install the M5Unified library.
Open the Arduino sketch found in make-reinforcement-learning-for-robotics/software/balance_bot_arduino. This should open the actor.h file that you copied in a separate tab. Feel free to look through that file, as it contains the neural network code automatically generated by our training program.
NOTE: This is a relatively simple neural network, so we perform inference with it directly on one of the ESP32’s cores using floating point math. The ESP32 easily runs it within our 5ms control loop. For more complex neural networks, you might need to quantize or compress the model or consider running it on some type of accelerator.
Head back to the balance_bot_arduino.ino sketch and look through the code. In the loop() function, we read from the sensors, construct an observation vector, and feed that as input to our actor neural network (using the actor_forward() function). The neural network’s outputs are the two action values, one for each motor.
We constrain the actor’s output to be between –1 and 1, and then we convert the action outputs to PWM signals for the motors. If the robot has tipped beyond a certain point, we simply shut off the motors so you can move it to a new location.
At the end of the loop, we wait however long is necessary to meet our 5ms timestep requirement.
NOTE: The Arduino code contains a number of pre- and post-processing features, like filters and deadbands. These offer another method of closing the sim-to-real gap, but they start to dive into more classical controls. Most of them have been disabled in this sketch so you can see how the actor behaves without them. If your robot does not balance well, try adjusting some of the parameters (e.g. ACTION_DEADBAND, ACTION_ALPHA) to see if they help with balancing. If your bot drifts while balancing, try slightly setting PITCH_OFFSET to something like –0.05 or 0.05, depending on the drift direction.
Connect your Bala2-Fire robot to your computer. Select Tools→Board→M5Stack→M5Fire. Select the serial port for your robot in Tools→Port. Click Sketch→Upload. Your sketch should compile and be flashed to the ESP32 onboard the Bala2-Fire.
Disconnect the USB cable from the robot and carefully place the robot on a flat surface. After a moment, it should begin to balance on its own. You can try pushing it slightly with your finger. If you push too hard, the robot will fall down, as the policy or motors cannot keep up with such large force changes.
Your balance bot might not be perfect; we’re asking a lot of PPO in such a short amount of training time. Try adding more phases, or adjusting the reward function to encourage better balancing. For example, you might have a fourth phase that adds randomization for dr.ridge_prob and dr.ridge_torque_max_nm to simulate the tire ridges. The simulated robot has basic cylinders for wheels with smooth edges whereas the real robot has rubber tires with ridges. Adding small, random torques to the axles can simulate these ridges. Alternatively, you might adjust the direction of gravity slightly to have the agent learn to balance on a slight slope.
If you’d like to dive into the specifics of setting up PPO and the environment as well as see how to add a remote control to drive the bot around, check out my video series on DigiKey’s YouTube channel:
Hopefully, this helps you get started in the world of reinforcement learning. A balance bot is just the beginning: all the concepts we covered here can be used as a foundation for training much more complex robots, like quadrupeds and bipedal walkers. Good luck, and happy hacking!

This is a regularly $2,499 machine with a solid $200 price drop to delivery the lowest price we have tracked on Amazon to date – this is the same deal Amazon offered for the fall Prime Day sale this year. 
As for the M6 Mac mini, the two most affordable configurations Amazon offers are still sitting with deep discounts right now. This includes the entry 16GB/256GB model at $120 off and the 16GB/512GB model at $949.99 – that’s $149 off the list price and within $1 of the lowest price we have tracked to date:

Tata Communications to invest $400-450 million for India’s AI fibre network
Tata Communications will invest close to $450 million to build a high-speed, low-latency AI network fabric connecting India’s key AI data-centre corridors.
The company plans to lay around 10,000 kilometres of long-haul fibre across the country, creating one of India’s largest AI-ready connectivity networks. Dubbed the “AI Super Highway”, the network is designed to support next-generation AI workloads.
The rollout is expected to take place over three to five years. Phase 1 will focus on the Mumbai-Pune-Hyderabad-Chennai corridor, linking major data-centre clusters and subsea cable landing stations. Later phases will expand connectivity to Visakhapatnam, Bhubaneswar, Gujarat, GIFT City and Jamnagar.
Centre includes E100 in emission framework for flex-fuel vehicles
The Centre has updated the vehicle emission framework to include E100 fuel alongside E85 for flex-fuel vehicles, paving the way for greater ethanol use in specially designed automobiles.
According to a notification issued by the Ministry of Road Transport and Highways, references to E85 in specific emission norms have been replaced with “E85 or E100”. E100 refers to near-pure ethanol fuel, while E85 contains up to 85 per cent ethanol blended with petrol.
India’s current nationwide standard fuel remains E20, which contains 20 per cent ethanol. The government has clarified that no decision has been taken to raise the national blending target beyond E20 at this stage.
Private logistics players tap India Post to expand last-mile reach
India Post’s vast delivery network is increasingly being used by private logistics companies looking to expand into smaller towns and remote regions without building costly new infrastructure.
Recent partnerships with Blue Dart, DTDC and Flipkart highlight the growing demand for India Post’s extensive reach to bridge last-mile delivery gaps.
Blue Dart signed an agreement with the Department of Posts earlier this month to extend services into Tier-III and Tier-IV cities and remote areas. Meanwhile, Flipkart has partnered with India Post to provide last-mile delivery, cash-on-delivery services, OTP-based authentication and real-time tracking across urban, semi-urban and rural markets.
Backdoor attacks on Indian SMB systems jump 52% in H1 2026: Kaspersky
Backdoor malware attacks targeting Indian small and medium businesses surged 52.1 per cent year-on-year during the first half of 2026, according to cybersecurity firm Kaspersky.
Data from the Kaspersky Security Network showed that 4.52 lakh backdoor threats were detected between January and June 2026, compared with 2.97 lakh during the same period last year.
A backdoor enables attackers to bypass authentication systems and gain hidden access to a computer or network. Once installed, it can be used to steal data, move deeper into systems and launch additional cyberattacks.
While backdoor malware detections rose sharply, attacks targeting Remote Desktop Protocol, or RDP, fell 26.3 per cent year-on-year to 37.38 lakh attempts. Exploit detections also declined 35.1 per cent to 4.63 lakh during the period.
(Research and VO: Siddharth Mathew Cherian)

95,2026-10-26,"Watch CBS News

Updated on:  October 26, 2026 / 12:43 PM EDT
            / CBS News
          
Anthropic filed for an initial public offering on Monday, bringing the maker of artificial intelligence platform Claude a step closer to its highly anticipated public market debut and providing a key test for the rapidly emerging AI sector.
In the filing, Anthropic outlined its vision for a future profoundly shaped by AI, touting the technology's potential to transform the global economy and improve lives worldwide.
""We believe the diffusion of AI through the global economy represents one of the largest opportunities in history to improve how humans live and work, and it is just beginning,"" the company said in its S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.
The filing highlights Anthropic's rapid growth, with the company generating $16.3 billion in revenue in the first half of 2026, up from $1.2 billion in the same period last year. That staggering rate of expansion is likely to slow, with Anthropic noting that its revenue growth is expected to decline over time.
The company has yet to turn a profit. Anthropic reported a net loss of $80.7 billion for the first half of the year, more than six times the net loss of nearly $13 billion for the first two quarters in 2025.
Anthropic's founders, including Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, pitched the IPO to investors in part as an opportunity to be part of a history-defining moment.
""We encourage you, as a prospective owner of our stock, not to think of your purchase in purely transactional terms,"" the company's seven co-founders said in a letter included in the S-1. ""We hope you will see it instead as an opportunity to be part of something greater. The advent of machine intelligence is one of the seminal moments in human history.""
The IPO, which market analysts expect as early as November, could instantly rank Anthropic among the world's largest publicly traded companies. The offering could raise as much as $100 billion, according to reports. That would slingshot it past Elon Musk-owned SpaceX, which raised a record-breaking $86 billion when it went public in June.
Anthropic, which was valued at $965 billion in May, could see its value rise to $1.5 trillion or possibly $2 trillion, although its final valuation has not been established. 
Anthropic shares will list on the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index, according to the company's filing. The AI company will trade under the ticker symbol ""ANT.""
More broadly, the transaction will gauge the strength of investor demand not only for Anthropic shares, but also for the widening constellation of data center builders, chipmakers and other companies involved in developing the critical infrastructure powering AI services. 
""This IPO is a referendum on the entire AI trade,"" Nigel Green, CEO of investment firm deVere Group, said in an email. ""If it soars, expect a queue of AI listings and even more capital piling into the theme. If it stumbles, valuations across tech could reset fast.""
The torrent of capital flowing into AI has helped propel the stock market to record highs. The question is whether AI can maintain its momentum amid signs that investors are fretting over when — and if — their money will eventually pay off and deliver the returns they are banking on. 
Anthropic's prospectus shows that the company has funneled massive amounts of money toward building the computing resources necessary to power its AI models. The company spent $8.9 billion in the first half of the year on chips and other infrastructure to run its platform, compared to $2.3 billion over the same period last year.
""Anthropic could become the most valuable company in the world. It could also end up in bankruptcy,"" analysts with Lightshed, an investment advisory firm, said in a research note ahead of the IPO filing. 
Anthropic's IPO filing also outlines various risks that could hamper its growth or cause it to fall behind competitors. Much of the company's future growth hinges on whether it has access to sufficient capital, including debt financing, which more companies are using to finance the AI buildout.
To maintain its competitive edge, Anthropic also said it will need to use its existing AI models to design and train next-generation platforms, a process known as ""recursive self-improvement,"" or RSI.
""If we fail to direct sufficient compute and research efforts to AI-assisted development, or if our competitors more effectively leverage AI to enhance their own innovation cycles, we risk falling progressively further behind. …"" Anthropic said in the filing.
AI experts warn that using AI to build ever more powerful frontier models raises the risk of people losing control of the technology, potentially leading to disasters such as deadly pandemics or cyberattacks that cut off internet, electricity or water.
Anthropic filed a confidential IPO with the Securities and Exchange Commission in June, beating out rival OpenAI, which filed just over a week later. Both companies are barreling ahead with plans to go public despite concerns about the potential threats AI poses to humanity.
Reports of AI agents going rogue continue to pile up, stoking concern that the technology could escape human control and carry out cyberattacks. In September, Amodei called for a slowdown in AI development after one of his company's former researchers said that there's a 10% chance the technology could wipe out humanity in the next decade.
In an interview with CBS News, Amodei said the pace of technological growth should serve as a warning sign, while making a case for continued development of AI.

Snakes that cartwheel, vultures that groan, and a Martian moon full of mystery 

This week in science we check out gymnastic snakes, chatty vultures and missions to faraway moons
By Rachel Feltman, Alli Rodgers & Alex Sugiura 
Edwin Butter/Mohamad alias/Paul Sawer/Shutterstock/Scientific American Illustrations
Happy Monday, listeners! For Scientific American’s Science Quickly, I’m Rachel Feltman. You’re listening to our weekly science news roundup.
Let’s start with some space news. Last week, the Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency, or JAXA for short, successfully launched a half-a-billion-dollar mission to land on Phobos, the largest Martian moon.
The Martian Moons Exploration spacecraft, also known as MMX, is expected to reach the vicinity of Mars in August of 2027. Once there, it will spend three years circling Mars in a path similar to Phobos’s orbit. One of its objectives is to find an ideal spot for landing on Phobos. In 2028 or 2029, MMX will send a rover down to the surface to spend 100 days observing the moon. That data will come in handy later in 2029, when MMX itself will attempt one or even two landings. If all goes well, MMX will return back up to orbit with 10 grams, or one-third of an ounce, of material from Phobos. It’s expected to make it back to Earth sometime in 2031.
If you're enjoying this article, consider supporting our award-winning journalism by subscribing. By purchasing a subscription you are helping to ensure the future of impactful stories about the discoveries and ideas shaping our world today.
So, why all this effort to check out a lumpy little moon? Phobos may be just 14 miles wide, but it’s got a lot to say. With an orbit of just over seven hours, it’s closer to its planet than any other moon in our solar system. That orbit is also gradually shrinking, bringing it on a slow-but-steady collision course with Mars.
Then there’s the unknown origin of Phobos and its little sibling, the Martian moon Deimos. Scientists aren’t sure whether these wee moons were once asteroids that got too close to Mars or if they formed from debris after something collided with the red planet. When the MMX sample comes home, researchers will compare its composition against Martian material and asteroids to try to solve that mystery.
Speaking of weird little guys with adorably tiny orbits, our next story features a little snake that uses cartwheels as a strange evasive maneuver.
Now, I don’t know about you, but I find cartwheels hard enough with two arms and legs to support me. But apparently there are tiny little snakes that put my acrobatic attempts to shame with nary a limb in sight. Scientists first showcased this ability with the dwarf reed snake in 2023, but now they’ve identified that same cartwheeling behavior in two additional species of the same genus, Pseudorabdion. And what’s even better is that they caught the bizarre locomotion on video.
The snakes start out by making a tight little curve, kind of like the letter “C,” with its head up top, facing the direction it wants to go, and something like the middle of its body touching the ground. Then it reels forward until its tail is the part touching the ground, which gives it the momentum to launch itself into the air. While it’s airborne, the snake twists around until it gets itself into the position of the starting point of the next roll, and it alternates which side of its body strikes the ground with each rotation. And did I mention these snakes are tiny and adorable? I’m definitely not doing the footage of their limbless leaping justice, so you really gotta check the video out for yourself. We’ll link to that in our show notes!
Those aren’t the only animal showoffs we saw making headlines last week. In a study published last Wednesday, researchers shared the surprisingly complex range of sounds made by birds not generally known for their gorgeous singing voices: vultures.
When you think of a vulture, you probably picture a relatively solitary and silent bird, maybe like a few of them flying around in an ominous circle but not much more than that. But the thing is that some species are quite social—and, as it turns out, those more gregarious species can also be quite vocal. Rüppell’s vultures, which nest in colonies hundreds of birds strong, are known to follow a literal pecking order when it comes time to pick a carcass clean. Some experts think that social complexity fuels the evolution of complex communication. So, the set hierarchy that these vultures live by made scientists wonder how they might use sound to communicate.
Researchers captured 150 hours of noise from two groups of vultures in captivity, which contained something like 2,500 calls. When they analyzed qualities like pitch and length, they were able to distinguish 16 distinct call types, which could be double the average for birds in general, based on previous research.
If you’re wondering what those calls sound like, well lucky for you we have some examples. The study authors dubbed the most common call the “metallic groan,” and it generally came from birds who were on the receiving end of some kind of aggression. There were also specific brays that came from vultures trying to bully other birds for food, hisses made in response, hoarse mating groans, and special calls made by parents as they lovingly puked food into their babies’ mouths.
For our next story, you know those tabloid stories that show celebrities, like, grocery shopping and stuff with the headline “they’re just like us?” Well slap some Japanese macaques onto those glossy pages. Because according to a study published last week in Royal Society Open Science, these monkeys have a lot in common with humans when it comes to responding to disease outbreaks.
Not unlike the human tendency to stock up on hand sanitizer and wipe down groceries in the wake of a pandemic, the researchers say, primates who have survived life-threatening disease outbreaks remain more cautious about potential food contamination than their peers. That’s especially interesting given the fact that, for a macaque, there’s no public health agency announcing where a deadly ailment has come from. But even with no empirical knowledge of disease transmission, like literally these guys do not have germ theory, this study suggests that a brush with contagion inspires habits that limit exposure to new bugs.
The study looked at Japanese macaques at a primate sanctuary in southern Texas, where they’re grouped into several distinct enclosures. Three groups experienced an outbreak of simian retrovirus Type II, which can cause a sickness similar to AIDS, back in 2020. A fourth enclosure was completely spared, which provided a natural control group.
To test how that experience impacted pathogen avoidance, the researchers offered macaques food from visibly clean surfaces as well as ones seemingly contaminated by monkey poop, though covered by epoxy to prevent any actual disease risk. Previous studies have shown that this species will avoid some food with fecal contamination, but will risk it all for a favorite treat like a peanut. And, indeed, in this new study, monkeys ate from a seemingly dirty surface in 79 percent of trials. But in the groups that had survived an outbreak, monkeys went for the poop food less than half of the time. Even individuals who had not personally been sick were less likely to risk contamination.
Simian retrovirus Type II doesn’t actually spread through food, but via scratches and bites. But we know that disgust and pathogen avoidance are complicated—and not always actually very useful—in humans, too.
We’ll wrap up with a story about the funnest little animals of them all: preschoolers. Researchers were curious about why people seem to think that kids are more creative than adults, despite scientific evidence showing the opposite. In a study published last week in PLOS One, they had 92 preschoolers and 70 adults tackle three creative tasks: spending eight minutes drawing freely on blank paper with colored pens and spending eight minutes inventing and drawing a made-up animal. The study subjects were all asked about how much they enjoyed those activities, how anxious the tasks made them feel, and how confident they felt about the quality of what they’d come up with. Then their results were evaluated by five people trained in a system for rating creative work.
It turns out that, at least in this case, adults were objectively more creative than preschoolers, coming up with more original and effective ideas—but they also enjoyed themselves way less and felt worse about their work. So if you often find yourself wishing you could recapture the boundless creativity of your youth, maybe you should actually just work on your self-confidence. One time a lady in my ceramic studio looked at what I was making and said, “Wow, I wish I gave myself that much freedom.” And I have to tell you, at the time, it did not feel good. But now, I think I might have changed my mind.
That’s all for this week’s news roundup. We’ll be back on Wednesday to see how science is showing up in the midterm elections.
Science Quickly is produced by me, Rachel Feltman, along with Fonda Mwangi, Sushmita Pathak, Allison Rodgers and Jeff DelViscio. This episode was edited by Alex Sugiura. Emily Makowski, Marielle Issa and Aaron Shattuck fact-check our show. Our theme music was composed by Dominic Smith. Subscribe to Scientific American for more up-to-date and in-depth science news.
For Scientific American, this is Rachel Feltman. Have a great week!

97,2026-10-26,"Bernstein analysts raised Robinhood’s revenue estimates through 2026 and 2028, while maintaining their HOOD price target unchanged at $160.
Bernstein analysts expect prediction markets to become one of the incremental earnings drivers for trading platform Robinhood Markets (HOOD), potentially surpassing its crypto-derived revenue.
Prediction markets could drive up to $1.7 billion in business for Robinhood by 2028, surpassing the projected $1.6 billion in crypto-based revenues for that year, according to a Bernstein analysts’ forecast shared with Cointelegraph on Monday.
The analysts expects Robinhood’s prediction market revenue to increase to $1.7 billion in 2028 from about $650 million this year, accounting for roughly 18% of Robinhood’s projected $9.4 billion in total 2028 revenue.
The forecast comes a day ahead of Robinhood’s third-quarter 2026 report and shows that prediction markets are becoming a growing revenue source for the retail-focused trading platform. 
Bernstein expects Robinhood’s Q3 revenue to increase 13% quarter-over-quarter to around $1.5 billion, driven by growing equity notional volumes, options volumes, event contract volumes, along with a recovery in crypto trading volume.
Bernstein raised Robinhood’s revenue estimates by 3% for 2026, 4% for 2027 and 3% for 2028, while keeping its $160 price target unchanged, citing higher equities and crypto revenue assumptions. 
The Nasdaq-traded shares were poised to open down on Monday, with premarket activity paring the stock price down to $93.60, according to Yahoo Finance. HOOD stock is down more than 29% over the past 12 months.
